# KotlinDev — Android App and Game Development Knowledge Base (Kotlin)

## TL;DR

A complete, self-contained knowledge base for developing Android apps
and games in Kotlin, written for AI agents and humans. It covers the
Kotlin language on Android, the Android platform and its versions,
project setup and Gradle, app anatomy and lifecycle, Jetpack Compose
and Views, navigation, architecture, dependency injection, coroutines
and Flow, data persistence, networking, images/media/camera,
background work, permissions/security/privacy, testing, performance,
accessibility/internationalization/theming, build/signing/Play release,
monetization and Play Billing, form factors, Kotlin Multiplatform and
Compose Multiplatform, and a full games track — Compose Canvas 2D,
libGDX / KorGE / Godot, native graphics and the Android Game
Development Kit (AGDK), Play Games services, and game performance,
input, audio, and saves — plus the working material itself:
copy-and-start **templates**, complete **examples**, helper **tools**,
and quick **reference** tables. Versions and policy dates in this repo
are a snapshot checked in November 2026; anything that could not be
verified is marked **Unverified** rather than repeated as fact. Always
re-check versions on Maven Central and policy on the Play Console
before shipping.

## The ground-truth hierarchy

When sources disagree, this order wins:

1. **The official docs and artifacts themselves** — developer.android.com,
   kotlinlang.org, the AndroidX release notes, and Maven Central /
   Google Maven for exact versions and coordinates.
2. **Google Play policy and requirements** — the Play Console help
   pages and the target API / billing / data safety requirements pages.
3. **This repo** — a distillation of 1 and 2 plus verified third-party
   material, with snapshot dates stated.
4. **Everything else** — blog posts, videos, forum answers, and model
   memory. Useful for patterns and history, never as proof that an
   API, version, or policy exists today.

## What this covers

**Apps and games, both, in Kotlin.** Docs 01–22 and 29–33 are the app
track (with the shared platform, language, and tooling foundations).
Docs 23–28 are the games track: choosing a route, building 2D games
with Compose Canvas, using an engine (libGDX, KorGE, Godot), going
native with AGDK / OpenGL ES / Vulkan, wiring Play Games services,
and handling game-specific performance, input, audio, and saves.
A game is still an Android app underneath — it ships as an Android
App Bundle, follows the same target API and billing rules, and needs
the same testing and release discipline.

## Current-versions snapshot (checked November 2026)

Re-check every row before starting a project; see
[`reference/gradle-and-versions-cheatsheet.md`](reference/gradle-and-versions-cheatsheet.md).

| Thing | Snapshot (Nov 2026) | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Kotlin | **2.4.21** | Kotlin 2.x; Compose is enabled with the Compose compiler Gradle plugin, not `kotlinCompilerExtensionVersion` |
| Android Studio | **Rabbit 2 — 2026.2.2** | Use the bundled JDK unless a project pins its own |
| Android Gradle Plugin (AGP) | **9.5** | Pairs with Gradle 9.6 and JDK 17 |
| Gradle | **9.6** | Wrapper version in `gradle/wrapper/gradle-wrapper.properties` |
| JDK | **17** | Required by AGP 9.x |
| Jetpack Compose BOM | **2026.09.00** | Import the BOM; do not pin Compose library versions individually unless overriding deliberately |
| compileSdk | **36** (37 for the newest Compose features) | Newest Compose releases may require compileSdk 37 — check the Compose release notes for the BOM you use |
| targetSdk | **36** | API 36 target **required for new apps and app updates since August 31, 2026** |
| Play Billing Library | **8+** | Billing Library 8 or newer required; older versions are rejected for new submissions/updates per Play policy |
| Min SDK | Project choice (commonly 24–26) | State it explicitly; see [`docs/02-android-platform-and-versions.md`](docs/02-android-platform-and-versions.md) |

## How an AI should use this repo

1. Read [`AGENTS.md`](AGENTS.md) — the non-negotiable rules (never
   invent an API or version, target first, AAB + Play App Signing,
   Billing 8+, Play Games Services v2 only).
2. Identify the **target first**: state compileSdk / targetSdk /
   minSdk and whether the project is an app, a Compose Canvas game,
   an engine game, or a native game ([`docs/02-android-platform-and-versions.md`](docs/02-android-platform-and-versions.md),
   [`docs/23-games-overview-choosing-a-route.md`](docs/23-games-overview-choosing-a-route.md)).
3. Start from [`docs/01-kotlin-language-for-android.md`](docs/01-kotlin-language-for-android.md)
   and [`docs/03-project-setup-gradle-and-tooling.md`](docs/03-project-setup-gradle-and-tooling.md).
4. Check every API, library version, and coordinate against
   [`reference/library-directory.md`](reference/library-directory.md)
   and the official sources in [`SOURCES.md`](SOURCES.md) — and
   re-check Maven Central for anything version-sensitive.
5. Prefer the standard library / AndroidX solution and a maintained
   library over hand-rolling ([`reference/library-directory.md`](reference/library-directory.md)).
6. Start from a template or example instead of a blank page:
   [`templates/`](templates/), [`examples/`](examples/), and the
   helpers in [`tools/`](tools/).
7. Test per [`docs/16-testing-strategy.md`](docs/16-testing-strategy.md); release per
   [`docs/19-build-signing-release-and-google-play.md`](docs/19-build-signing-release-and-google-play.md)
   and the checklists in [`checklists/`](checklists/).

## Using this repo with ChatGPT or another AI, locally

This repo is built to be downloaded and used offline by an AI:

1. Download the repo (Code → Download ZIP) and unzip it anywhere.
2. Point the AI at the folder. It should start with
   [manifest.json](manifest.json) (the file map and reading order)
   and [AGENTS.md](AGENTS.md) (the rules), then read in the manifest's
   order.
3. No time to load a folder? Upload [ai-bundle.md](ai-bundle.md)
   instead — the entire knowledge base concatenated into one markdown
   file. (Generated by [tools/build-ai-bundle.py](tools/); the bundle
   covers the docs and references — templates, tools, and example
   code stay as files, listed in the manifest.)

Everything is plain text/markdown with relative links; nothing here
needs a server, an account, or this repository's website.

<!-- ===== AGENTS.md ===== -->

# AGENTS.md — Instructions for AI agents writing Kotlin Android code

You are using this repository as your knowledge base for creating
Android apps and games in Kotlin. Follow these rules exactly.

## Non-negotiable rules

1. **Never invent APIs, library versions, or Maven coordinates.**
   Every Android/Kotlin API call, Gradle plugin, and library version
   you write must be traceable to one of: developer.android.com,
   kotlinlang.org, the AndroidX release notes, or the artifact's page
   on Maven Central / Google Maven. If you cannot trace it, do not
   write it — say it is **Unverified** instead. Android APIs are
   version-gated and libraries rename artifacts between majors; a
   plausible-sounding coordinate is the single most common AI failure
   on Android code.
2. **Target/flavor first.** Before writing code, state the target:
   compileSdk, targetSdk, and minSdk; app vs game; and for games the
   route (Compose Canvas / libGDX / KorGE / Godot / native AGDK).
   Behavior changes, permission rules, and available APIs all depend
   on these numbers ([`docs/02-android-platform-and-versions.md`](docs/02-android-platform-and-versions.md)).
   Snapshot at time of writing: target API 36 (required for new apps
   and updates since August 31, 2026), compileSdk 36 — 37 where the
   newest Compose features require it.
3. **Kotlin 2.x + the Compose compiler plugin.** Kotlin 2.x projects
   enable Compose with the Compose compiler Gradle plugin
   (`org.jetbrains.kotlin.plugin.compose`) applied alongside the
   Kotlin Android plugin. Never set `kotlinCompilerExtensionVersion`
   in a Kotlin 2.x project — that property belongs to the Kotlin 1.x
   era and breaks or misconfigures modern builds. Use KSP, not KAPT,
   for new annotation processing.
4. **Ship an Android App Bundle (AAB) with Play App Signing.** Release
   builds are `.aab`, signed through Play App Signing (Google holds
   the final signing key; you hold the upload key). Do not plan a
   release around a directly-distributed release APK except for
   sideload/testing builds, and never put a production keystore or
   its passwords in the repo.
5. **Play Billing Library 8 or newer.** Any in-app purchase or
   subscription code targets Billing Library 8+. Older billing
   versions are rejected by Play for new submissions and updates.
   Test purchases with license testers and the Play Billing test
   tracks, never with real charges ([`docs/20-monetization-billing-and-ads.md`](docs/20-monetization-billing-and-ads.md)).
6. **Play Games Services v2 only.** New integrations use Play Games
   Services (PGS) v2 — automatic sign-in, the v2 SDK and APIs. Do
   not write new code against PGS v1 (`GoogleSignIn`-era Games APIs);
   it is the legacy path ([`docs/27-games-services-play-games-and-play-games-on-pc.md`](docs/27-games-services-play-games-and-play-games-on-pc.md)).
7. **Room 3 is not Room 2.** Room 3.x and Room 2.x are different
   majors with different artifacts and setup (Room 3 is KMP-ready
   and KSP-only). Name which one a project uses and use that major's
   coordinates and migration APIs consistently — never mix Room 2
   tutorials into a Room 3 project or vice versa
   ([`docs/11-data-persistence-room-datastore-files.md`](docs/11-data-persistence-room-datastore-files.md),
   [`reference/library-directory.md`](reference/library-directory.md)).
8. **Media3 is the media library.** Video/audio playback uses
   **Jetpack Media3** (`androidx.media3`) — standalone ExoPlayer 2
   (`com.google.android.exoplayer2`) is its predecessor and is not
   used for new code. Image loading default is **Coil 3** with its
   `io.coil-kt.coil3` coordinates — Coil 2 coordinates
   (`io.coil-kt:coil-compose`) in a new project are a version mix-up.
9. **No secrets in code.** No API keys, keystore passwords, service
   account JSON, or tokens committed to the repo or hard-coded in
   source. Use `local.properties` / environment variables locally and
   CI secrets in automation; anything user-sensitive at runtime is
   encrypted at rest. A key that shipped in an APK/AAB is public.
10. **Respect the platform limits.** Runtime permissions are requested
    at point of use with a rationale; background work goes through
    WorkManager or a declared foreground service type; exact alarms,
    full-screen intents, and special permissions are used only where
    the feature genuinely qualifies. Check
    [`reference/permissions-table.md`](reference/permissions-table.md)
    and [`reference/play-requirements-and-deadlines.md`](reference/play-requirements-and-deadlines.md)
    before writing permission or background code.
11. **Verify before declaring done.** Run the matching checklist in
    [`checklists/`](checklists/). State plainly which checks ran
    (build, unit tests, instrumented tests, a device/emulator run)
    and which could not be run — label those **Unverified** rather
    than claiming success. Code that has never compiled is not done.

## Build order (always in this sequence)

1. Name the target: compileSdk / targetSdk / minSdk + app or game
   route (rule 2).
2. Set up the project and version catalog
   ([`docs/03-project-setup-gradle-and-tooling.md`](docs/03-project-setup-gradle-and-tooling.md),
   [`reference/gradle-and-versions-cheatsheet.md`](reference/gradle-and-versions-cheatsheet.md));
   confirm a clean debug build runs on an emulator.
3. Lay out the architecture: UI layer, repository layer, data sources
   ([`docs/08-architecture-patterns.md`](docs/08-architecture-patterns.md)).
4. Build the smallest end-to-end slice (one screen/feature, real
   data path); confirm it runs.
5. Add features one at a time, checking each new API call and library
   version against the ground-truth sources (rule 1) and testing as
   you go.
6. Add persistence, background work, billing/games services, and
   polish as the feature list needs them.
7. Release build: AAB + Play App Signing, R8, Baseline Profile; run
   [`checklists/pre-release-checklist.md`](checklists/pre-release-checklist.md).

## Things that are always wrong

- An API, plugin, or Maven coordinate that appears in no official
  source or artifact repository for the stated version.
- `kotlinCompilerExtensionVersion` in a Kotlin 2.x project.
- A release plan built on a raw APK instead of an AAB for Play.
- Billing code below Billing Library 8, or PGS v1 code in a new game.
- Room 2 and Room 3 coordinates/APIs mixed in one project.
- New playback code on standalone ExoPlayer 2 instead of Media3, or
  new image code on Picasso (maintenance-era) instead of Coil 3/Glide.
- `AsyncTask` (deprecated), `GlobalScope` launches, or network/database
  work on the main thread.
- Secrets, keystores, or passwords committed to version control.
- Requesting every permission at first launch "just in case".
- Treating a blog post, video, or model memory as proof a version or
  policy is current — re-check Maven Central / Play Console help.

# SOURCES.md — Curated source list, verified

Every resource this repo leans on, organized by kind, with its URL and
why it matters. Snapshot checked November 2026. Statuses: **Verified**
(exists as described at check time) · **Unverified** (could not be
pinned to an exact current value in this pass — re-check before use).

Rule of use: official docs and artifact repositories are ground truth
for APIs, versions, and policy. Everything else is learning material.

## Official Android / Kotlin / JetBrains

| Resource | URL | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Android Developers (docs home) | https://developer.android.com/ | The ground truth: API references, guides, behavior changes, training. Start here for any API question. **Verified** Nov 2026. |
| Android API reference | https://developer.android.com/reference | Per-class reference with "added in API level" data — the check for version-gating mistakes. |
| Kotlin language docs | https://kotlinlang.org/docs/home.html | Language ground truth: coroutines, Flow, multiplatform, releases. **Verified** Nov 2026. |
| Kotlin releases | https://kotlinlang.org/docs/releases.html | Which Kotlin is current (snapshot: 2.4.21) and what changed per release. |
| JetBrains / Android Studio downloads | https://developer.android.com/studio | Current Android Studio (snapshot: Rabbit 2, 2026.2.2) and release notes. |
| Gradle releases & compatibility | https://gradle.org/releases/ + https://docs.gradle.org/current/userguide/compatibility.html | AGP ↔ Gradle ↔ JDK compatibility (snapshot: AGP 9.5 / Gradle 9.6 / JDK 17). |
| Google Maven | https://maven.google.com/ | The repository of record for AndroidX, AGP, Compose, Media3, Billing — exact versions live here. |
| Maven Central | https://central.sonatype.com/ | The repository of record for Kotlin, OkHttp, Retrofit, Ktor, Coil, and most third-party libraries. |
| AndroidX documentation | https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx | Library index, per-library guides, and the versions page. |
| Compose documentation | https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose | The Compose guide set: state, layout, theming, performance, testing. |
| Play Games Services docs | https://developer.android.com/games/playgames | PGS v2 setup, sign-in, achievements, leaderboards, saved games. |
| Android Game Development Kit (AGDK) | https://developer.android.com/games/agdk | GameActivity, frame pacing, performance tooling for native/C++ games. |
| Google Play Billing docs | https://developer.android.com/google/play/billing | Billing Library integration (8+), products, subscriptions, testing. |

## AndroidX release notes

| Resource | URL | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| AndroidX release notes (all libraries) | https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/versions | The single feed for what shipped in every AndroidX library. |
| Compose release notes / BOM mapping | https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases/compose + BOM page | Which library versions a Compose BOM (snapshot: 2026.09.00) maps to, and any compileSdk requirements. |
| Room release notes | https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases/room | Room 2.x vs 3.x changes and migration notes — read before mixing majors. |
| Navigation release notes | https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases/navigation | Navigation 2.x and the Navigation 3 line: what is stable vs alpha. |
| Media3 release notes | https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases/media3 | Media3 versions (snapshot: 1.11.1) and ExoPlayer migration notes. |
| WorkManager / Paging / CameraX notes | Via the AndroidX versions feed above | Snapshot: Paging 3.5.3, CameraX 1.6.2 — confirm on the feed before pinning. |

## Play Console / policy

| Resource | URL | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Play Console Help | https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer | Policy center: the binding text for target API, billing, permissions, data safety. |
| Target API requirements | https://developer.android.com/google/play/requirements/target-sdk | The deadline table: API 36 required for new apps/updates since Aug 31, 2026. **Verified** Nov 2026. |
| Play Billing requirements | Via Play Console Help → Payments / Billing | Billing Library 8+ requirement and deprecation schedule. |
| Data safety section help | https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/10787469 | How to declare data collection/sharing accurately. |
| 16 KB page size requirement | https://developer.android.com/guide/practices/page-sizes | Native-code alignment rule; Play enforcement steps up Feb 1, 2027 (see `reference/play-requirements-and-deadlines.md`). |
| Developer Program Policies | https://play.google.com/about/developer-content-policy/ | The full policy set submissions are reviewed against. |

## Official samples

| Resource | URL | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Now in Android | https://github.com/android/nowinandroid | Google's reference app: modularization, Compose, offline-first, testing — the architecture docs in this repo trace back to it. **Verified** Nov 2026. |
| Jetpack Compose samples | https://github.com/android/compose-samples | JetNews, Jetchat, Jetsnack and friends — idiomatic Compose patterns per feature. |
| Android architecture samples | https://github.com/android/architecture-samples | Smaller focused samples for data layer / TODO-MVVM patterns. |
| Android games samples | https://github.com/android/games-samples | AGDK/GameActivity and Play Games samples for the native route. |
| Kotlin coroutines / multiplatform samples | https://github.com/Kotlin (org samples) | JetBrains' own KMP and coroutines examples. |

## Awesome lists & directories

| Resource | URL | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| awesome-android | https://github.com/JStumpp/awesome-android | Long-running curated index of Android libraries and tools. Check dates — entries age. |
| awesome-kotlin | https://github.com/KotlinBy/awesome-kotlin | Kotlin libraries/projects across JVM, Android, and multiplatform. |
| awesome-compose / Android Dev resources lists | GitHub search: `awesome jetpack compose` | Useful discovery layer only — verify every entry on Maven Central before adopting. |

## Books & courses

| Resource | Why it matters |
|---|---|
| *Kotlin in Action* (2nd ed., Manning) | The standard Kotlin language book, updated for modern Kotlin. |
| *Head First Android Development* (O'Reilly) | Beginner-friendly full-path Android book; check its edition targets Kotlin/Compose before relying on version details. |
| Android Basics with Compose (developer.android.com/courses) | Google's free beginner course — the canonical first path for Compose. |
| Advanced Android / MAD Skills series (developer.android.com + YouTube) | Google's topic deep-dives: architecture, performance, Media3, large screens. |
| Kotlin Coroutines by Tutorials / coroutines codelabs | Structured coroutines/Flow learning; pair with the official coroutines guide. |

## Communities

| Resource | Why it matters |
|---|---|
| Kotlin Slack (kotlinlang.slack.com) | JetBrains-run; `#android` and `#compose` channels for library-level questions. |
| AndroidDev / Kotlin Discord servers | Fast informal help; treat answers as leads, verify in docs. |
| Stack Overflow (`android`, `kotlin`, `jetpack-compose` tags) | Searchable problem archive — check answer dates against current APIs. |
| Reddit r/androiddev, r/Kotlin | Release discussion and experience reports. |
| Android Developers on YouTube / Medium | Launch announcements and engineering posts from the platform team. |

## Games sources

| Resource | URL | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| AGDK home + downloads | https://developer.android.com/games/agdk | Native game stack: GameActivity, Frame Pacing, AGDK libraries, Android Game Development Extension for Visual Studio. |
| Android games hub | https://developer.android.com/games | Play Games, Google Play Games on PC, performance and reach guidance. |
| libGDX | https://libgdx.com/ + https://github.com/libgdx/libgdx | The main open-source JVM game framework for Android; wiki covers setup, deployment, backends. **Verified** Nov 2026. |
| KTX (libGDX Kotlin extensions) | https://github.com/libktx/ktx | Idiomatic Kotlin APIs over libGDX — the default way to write libGDX in Kotlin. |
| KorGE | https://korge.org/ + https://github.com/korlibs/korge | Kotlin-native 2D game engine, multiplatform. **Current version: Unverified** in this pass — check the repo releases / Maven Central (`com.soywiz.korge`) before pinning. |
| Godot engine | https://godotengine.org/ + https://docs.godotengine.org/ | Full engine with Android export; the route for teams already on Godot. |
| godot-kotlin/jvm | https://github.com/utopia-rise/godot-kotlin-jvm | Kotlin/JVM language support for Godot — lets a Godot game be written in Kotlin. Check its supported Godot version before starting. |
| Play Games Services | https://developer.android.com/games/playgames | v2 SDK: sign-in, achievements, leaderboards, saved games, Play Games on PC requirements. |
| OpenGL ES / Vulkan on Android | https://developer.android.com/games/graphics | Official graphics guides for the native route. |

## Explicitly Unverified in this pass

| Item | Status |
|---|---|
| KorGE current version | **Unverified** — check https://github.com/korlibs/korge/releases and Maven Central before pinning a version. |
| Firebase BoM exact version | **Unverified** — Firebase ships via a Bill of Materials whose version moves often; read the current BoM version at https://firebase.google.com/docs/android/setup and pin that. |
| Dagger / Hilt exact latest version | **Unverified** — Hilt moves with Dagger releases; read the current version at https://dagger.dev/hilt/ and Google Maven (`com.google.dagger:hilt-android`) before pinning. |

<!-- ===== docs/01-kotlin-language-for-android.md ===== -->

# Kotlin Language for Android

Kotlin is the preferred language for Android. It is concise, null-safe by design, and fully interoperable with Java. This guide covers the features you will use every day in Android apps and games.

Official references: Kotlin docs: https://kotlinlang.org/docs/home.html
- Kotlin for Android: https://developer.android.com/kotlin
- Coding conventions: https://kotlinlang.org/docs/coding-conventions.html
## Why Kotlin on Android

Less boilerplate than Java; null safety in the type system; coroutines and Flow are first-class for async work; data classes, sealed types, and extensions model UI state cleanly; and 100% Java interoperability — Kotlin and Java can mix in one project.

## Variables and Types

```kotlin
val id: Long = 42L          // read-only, preferred
var counter: Int = 0        // mutable, only when needed
val name = "Ada"            // type inferred as String
val price: Double = 9.99
```

Prefer `val` over `var` — immutability makes state easier to reason about, especially with Compose and unidirectional data flow.

Use string templates instead of concatenation: 
```kotlin
val user = "Ada"
val greeting = "Hello, $user — you have ${items.size} items"
```

## Null Safety

Kotlin separates nullable and non-nullable types. A `String` can never be null. A `String?` may be null and must be handled.

```kotlin
val a: String = "hello"
// val b: String = null     // compile error — this is the point
val c: String? = null
val len1 = c?.length ?: 0          // safe call + Elvis operator
val len2 = c?.length ?: error("missing value")

fun printLength(s: String?) {
    if (s != null) {
        println(s.length)  // smart cast: s is String here
    }
}
```

Key operators: 
`?.` safe call (null if receiver is null); `?:` Elvis fallback; `!!` not-null assertion (throws if null — avoid in production); `?.let { }` runs a block only when not null; `requireNotNull(x)` fails fast with a clear message.

Never use `!!` to silence the compiler. Handle the null case, provide a default, or redesign the type to be non-nullable.

## Functions and Lambdas

```kotlin
fun greet(name: String, punctuation: String = "!"): String {
    return "Hello, $name$punctuation"
}
fun double(x: Int): Int = x * 2   // single-expression function
val msg = greet(name = "Sam", punctuation = ".")  // named arguments

repeatAction(3) { index -> println("Item $index") }  // lambda argument
```

Default arguments remove the need for most Java-style overloads.

## Data Classes

Data classes generate `equals`, `hashCode`, `toString`, `copy`, and destructuring automatically. Use them for models and UI state.

```kotlin
data class User(val id: Long, val name: String, val email: String?)
val u1 = User(1, "Ada", null)
val u2 = u1.copy(name = "Ada Lovelace")  // copy with one field changed
val (id, name, _) = u2                   // destructuring
```

## Sealed Classes and Interfaces

Sealed types restrict which subclasses can exist, so `when` can be exhaustive without an `else` branch. This is the standard way to model UI state and results.

```kotlin
sealed interface UiState {
    data object Loading : UiState
    data class Success(val items: List<String>) : UiState
    data class Error(val message: String) : UiState
}
fun render(state: UiState): String = when (state) {
    UiState.Loading -> "Loading…"
    is UiState.Success -> "Loaded ${state.items.size} items"
    is UiState.Error -> "Error: ${state.message}"
}
```

Use sealed types for screen states, navigation events, network results, and game states (menu, playing, paused, game over).

## Classes, Objects, and Companion Objects

```kotlin
class Counter(private var value: Int = 0) { fun increment() { value++ } }
object AppConfig { const val MAX_RETRIES = 3 }   // singleton
class Repository {
    companion object {
        const val TAG = "Repository"  // like Java static
        fun create(): Repository = Repository()
    }
}
```

Prefer constructor injection over singletons for testable code.

## Scope Functions

Scope functions run a block with an object as context. Choose by the context (`this` or `it`) and what you return.

| Function | Context | Returns | Typical use |
|----------|---------|---------|-------------|
| `let` | `it` | block result | transform a nullable value |
| `run` | `this` | block result | compute a result from an object |
| `with` | `this` | block result | group calls on one object |
| `apply` | `this` | the object | configure an object |
| `also` | `it` | the object | side effects, logging |

```kotlin
val text = user?.let { "Name: ${it.name}" } ?: "Unknown"
val paint = Paint().apply { color = Color.RED; textSize = 48f }
list.also { println("Loaded ${it.size} items") }
```

Do not chain scope functions deeply. If the code needs a comment to explain which function does what, write plain statements instead.

## Collections

Kotlin collections are read-only by default. `List`, `Set`, and `Map` are immutable interfaces; `MutableList` and friends allow changes.

```kotlin
val names = listOf("Ada", "Sam", "Jo")
val doubled = listOf(1, 2, 3).map { it * 2 }          // [2, 4, 6]
val adults = users.filter { it.age >= 18 }
val total = listOf(1, 2, 3).fold(0) { acc, n -> acc + n }
val grouped = users.groupBy { it.team }
val first = names.firstOrNull() ?: "none"
val sorted = users.sortedBy { it.name }
```

Common operations: `map`, `filter`, `flatMap`, `firstOrNull`, `any`, `all`, `count`, `sumOf`, `maxByOrNull`, `associateBy`, `partition`, `zip`, `chunked`. Prefer these over manual loops. Sequences (`asSequence()`) defer work for large collections, but for typical Android list sizes a plain collection is simpler and faster.

## Delegation

Delegation forwards interface implementation to another object:

```kotlin
interface SoundPlayer { fun play() }
class MediaSoundPlayer : SoundPlayer {
    override fun play() { /* play sound */ }
}
class GameAudio(player: SoundPlayer) : SoundPlayer by player
```

Property delegates:

```kotlin
val lazyDb: Database by lazy { openDatabase() }  // init on first use
var observed: String by Delegates.observable("init") { _, old, new ->
    println("$old -> $new")
}
```

In Android, `by viewModels()`, `by remember`, and delegated Compose state are all delegation in action.

## Inline and Value Classes

A value class wraps a single value with a distinct type and little or no runtime overhead. Use it to prevent mixing up IDs and units.

```kotlin
@JvmInline value class UserId(val value: Long)
@JvmInline value class Milliseconds(val value: Long)
fun loadUser(id: UserId) { /* ... */ }
// loadUser(Milliseconds(5))  // compile error — types protect you
```

`inline` functions inline the lambda body at the call site — this is how `let`, `run`, and reified generics work:

```kotlin
inline fun <reified T> parse(json: String): T =
    parser.fromJson(json, T::class.java)  // T known at runtime
```

## Extension Functions

Extensions add functions to existing types without inheritance:

```kotlin
fun String.isValidEmail(): Boolean = contains("@") && contains(".")
fun View.show() { visibility = View.VISIBLE }
fun View.hide() { visibility = View.GONE }
```

Keep extensions close to the type they extend, and avoid surprising behavior. An extension cannot access private members of the receiver.

## When Java Interop Matters

- Calling Java libraries without nullability annotations — treat
returns as platform types (`String!`) and validate at the boundary.
- Exposing Kotlin to Java callers — use `@JvmStatic`, `@JvmField`,
`@JvmOverloads`, and `@Throws` where needed.
- Working with reflection-based libraries and older mixed codebases
during a gradual migration.

```kotlin
class Analytics {
    companion object {
        @JvmStatic fun track(event: String) { /* ... */ }
    }
    @JvmField var enabled: Boolean = true
}
```
## Idioms Summary

Prefer `val`, data classes, and immutable collections. Model state with sealed types and exhaustive `when`. Handle nulls with `?.`, `?:`, and smart casts — not `!!`. Use default and named arguments instead of overload chains, and `copy()` to update immutable state. Let the compiler check what Java would check at runtime.

<!-- ===== docs/02-android-platform-and-versions.md ===== -->

# Android Platform and Versions

This guide records the verified platform facts for modern Android and
Kotlin development, the API level table, Google Play target requirements,
and the breaking changes that affect apps targeting recent versions.

Official references:
- Android versions: https://developer.android.com/about/versions
- Play target API policy: https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/11926878
- Behavior changes: https://developer.android.com/about/versions/16/behavior-changes-16
- 16 KB page sizes: https://developer.android.com/guide/practices/page-sizes
- App bundles: https://developer.android.com/guide/app-bundle

## Verified Toolchain — November 2026

These versions were verified for this knowledge base. Always re-check
official release notes before starting a new project, because tooling
moves quickly.

| Component | Verified version | Notes |
|-----------|------------------|-------|
| Kotlin | 2.4.21 stable (Oct 8, 2026) | Current stable language release |
| Android Studio | Rabbit 2 — 2026.2.2 | Current stable IDE release |
| Android Gradle Plugin (AGP) | 9.5 | See requirements below |
| Gradle | 9.6 | Required by AGP 9.5 |
| JDK | 17 | Required to run AGP 9.5 |
| Build Tools | 36 | Bundled with recent SDK |
| Max supported API | 37 | Highest API AGP 9.5 supports |
| Built-in Kotlin | Since AGP 9.0 | AGP ships with Kotlin support built in |

AGP 9.x note: since AGP 9.0, Kotlin support is built into AGP itself.
Projects no longer apply a separate Kotlin Android plugin the old way.
Follow the AGP 9 migration guide when upgrading older projects.

## API Level Table

API level is the integer Android uses to identify a platform release.
`minSdk` is the oldest device you support, `targetSdk` is the version
your app is optimized and tested for, and `compileSdk` is the version
you compile against (normally the latest).

| API | Codename | Android version |
|-----|----------|-----------------|
| 37 | CINNAMON_BUN | Android 17 |
| 36 | BAKLAVA | Android 16 |
| 35 | VANILLA_ICE_CREAM | Android 15 |
| 34 | UPSIDE_DOWN_CAKE | Android 14 |
| 33 | TIRAMISU | Android 13 |
| 32 / 31 | S | Android 12L / 12 |
| 30 | R | Android 11 |
| 29 | Q | Android 10 |
| 28 | P | Android 9 |
| 27 / 26 | O | Android 8.1 / 8.0 |
| 25 / 24 | N | Android 7.1 / 7.0 |
| 23 | M | Android 6.0 |
| 22 / 21 | Lollipop | Android 5.1 / 5.0 |

Practical guidance:

- Set `compileSdk` to the latest stable (36, or 37 when your AGP supports it).
- Set `targetSdk` to meet the current Play requirement (see below).
- Choose `minSdk` from your audience. API 24–26 covers the vast majority
  of active devices in most markets; check Play Console device stats for
  your own app before raising it.

## Google Play Target API Requirements

From August 31, 2026, Google Play enforces these target API floors:

- **New apps and app updates:** must target API 36 or higher.
- **Wear OS and Android Auto:** must target API 35 or higher.
- **Android TV and Android XR:** must target API 34 or higher.
- **Existing apps:** must target API 35 or higher to remain available
  to new users on devices running newer Android versions.

If an existing app targets below the floor, it stays available to users
who already have it but is hidden from new installs on newer devices.
Plan target bumps early — each bump brings behavior changes that need
testing, not just a number change in Gradle.

An extension can be requested in Play Console when a migration genuinely
needs more time, but an extension is not a strategy. Target the current
level during normal development.

## Android App Bundle Is Required

Since August 2021, new apps on Google Play must be published as an
Android App Bundle (AAB), not an APK. Play generates optimized APKs per
device from your bundle (split by ABI, density, and language).

- Build with `./gradlew bundleRelease` to produce the `.aab`.
- APKs are still used for local testing, other stores, and sideloading.
- Nothing changes in your code — the bundle is a publishing format.

## 16 KB Page Sizes

Android devices are moving from 4 KB to 16 KB memory page sizes for
better performance. The rules that matter:

- Apps targeting API 35 or higher must support 16 KB page sizes.
- Enforcement date: February 1, 2027.
- **Pure Kotlin and Java apps already comply.** No action is needed if
  your app ships no native code.
- Apps with native libraries (`.so` files from the NDK, game engines, or
  native dependencies) must recompile those libraries with 16 KB
  alignment, or Play will reject updates and the app may fail to install
  or crash on 16 KB devices.

Check compliance in Android Studio with the APK Analyzer, and test on a
16 KB emulator image. Game projects using native engines should confirm
their engine version is 16 KB-ready.

## Breaking Changes by Target Version

Behavior changes apply when you raise `targetSdk`, even if the device
runs an older version in some cases. Test these areas on every bump.

### Targeting API 34 (Android 14)

- **Foreground service types are mandatory.** Every foreground service
  must declare a type (for example `dataSync`, `mediaPlayback`,
  `health`, `remoteMessaging`) in the manifest and in code, and hold
  the matching permission (for example `FOREGROUND_SERVICE_DATA_SYNC`).
- Runtime permission is required before starting certain FGS types.
- Apps must declare how they use each FGS type in Play Console.

### Targeting API 35 (Android 15)

- **Partial media access.** Users can grant access to selected photos
  and videos only. Handle re-selection and limited grants gracefully;
  do not assume full library access after a grant.
- **Edge-to-edge is enforced.** Apps draw behind the system bars by
  default. Handle window insets in Compose or Views — content hidden
  behind the status or navigation bar is now your bug to fix.
- **dataSync foreground service timeout.** `dataSync` FGS instances
  have a cumulative time limit (about 6 hours in 24 hours). When the
  limit is hit the system stops the service. Use WorkManager for
  deferrable sync instead of a long-running FGS.
- 16 KB page size support is required (see above).

### Targeting API 36 (Android 16)

- **Edge-to-edge opt-out removed.** The temporary opt-out available at
  API 35 no longer works. Edge-to-edge handling is mandatory.
- **Predictive back is on by default.** The predictive back gesture
  (showing a preview of the previous screen) is enabled by default.
  Apps using custom back handling must migrate to the supported APIs
  (`OnBackPressedDispatcher`, Compose back handlers). Legacy
  `onBackPressed` overrides can break the preview and gesture flow.
- **Large-screen restrictions ignored.** On large screens (sw600dp+),
  apps can no longer lock orientation, restrict resizability, or fix
  aspect ratio — those manifest and runtime requests are ignored.
  Apps must support landscape, portrait, and freeform resizing,
  including on foldables and tablets. Games should handle dynamic
  resizing and aspect changes gracefully.

### General Rules for Every Bump

- Read the official "behavior changes for apps targeting X" page for
  the exact level before changing `targetSdk`.
- Test on an emulator or device actually running that Android version.
- Re-check permissions, background work limits, and notification
  behavior — these tighten almost every release.
- Update Play Console declarations (FGS types, permissions, data
  safety) in the same release as the target bump.

## Choosing Your SDK Levels — Checklist

```kotlin
android {
    compileSdk = 36

    defaultConfig {
        minSdk = 26        // example — decide from your audience data
        targetSdk = 36     // meets the Aug 31, 2026 Play requirement
    }
}
```

1. `compileSdk` = latest stable your AGP supports.
2. `targetSdk` = current Play requirement or one below while migrating.
3. `minSdk` = lowest version with meaningful share for your users.
4. Never set `targetSdk` higher than you have tested.
5. Never set `compileSdk` lower than `targetSdk`.

## Version Catalog Note

Keep Kotlin, AGP, and SDK versions in one place — the Gradle version
catalog (`gradle/libs.versions.toml`) — so modules never drift. See
the project setup guide in this knowledge base for the full pattern.


---

<!-- ===== docs/03-project-setup-gradle-and-tooling.md ===== -->

# Project Setup, Gradle, and Tooling

A well-structured Gradle setup saves hours on every later change.
This guide covers Android Studio, the SDK, Gradle with Kotlin DSL,
version catalogs, AGP and Kotlin versions, the Compose BOM, and
module layout.

Official references:
- Create a project: https://developer.android.com/studio/projects/create-project
- Configure your build: https://developer.android.com/build
- Version catalogs: https://developer.android.com/build/migrate-to-catalogs
- AGP release notes: https://developer.android.com/build/releases/gradle-plugin
- Compose BOM: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/bom

## Android Studio and the SDK

Install Android Studio (Rabbit 2, 2026.2.2 verified) and use its SDK
Manager to install:

- The latest stable SDK Platform (API 36, and 37 when available).
- Build Tools 36.
- Android Emulator and at least one system image for testing.
- Command-line Tools — needed for CI and scripted builds.

Keep the SDK in one known location and set `ANDROID_HOME` (or
`sdk.dir` in `local.properties`) so command-line builds work. Never
commit `local.properties` — it is machine-specific and generated.
Create projects from the Studio templates (Empty Activity for
Compose is the usual start). Templates wire correct AGP, Kotlin, and
Compose versions together, which is safer than assembling by hand.

## Gradle with Kotlin DSL

Modern projects use Gradle build scripts in Kotlin DSL
(`build.gradle.kts`): autocomplete, type checking, same language as
the app. Verified baseline: AGP 9.5 requires Gradle 9.6 and JDK 17.
Since AGP 9.0, Kotlin support is built into AGP.

Root `settings.gradle.kts`:

```kotlin
pluginManagement {
    repositories { google(); mavenCentral(); gradlePluginPortal() }
}
dependencyResolutionManagement {
    repositoriesMode.set(RepositoriesMode.FAIL_ON_PROJECT_REPOS)
    repositories { google(); mavenCentral() }
}
rootProject.name = "MyApp"
include(":app")
```

App module `build.gradle.kts`:

```kotlin
plugins {
    alias(libs.plugins.android.application)
    alias(libs.plugins.kotlin.compose)
}
android {
    namespace = "com.example.myapp"
    compileSdk = 36
    defaultConfig {
        applicationId = "com.example.myapp"
        minSdk = 26
        targetSdk = 36
        versionCode = 1
        versionName = "1.0"
    }
    buildTypes {
        release {
            isMinifyEnabled = true
            proguardFiles(
                getDefaultProguardFile("proguard-android-optimize.txt"),
                "proguard-rules.pro")
        }
    }
    buildFeatures { compose = true }
    compileOptions {
        sourceCompatibility = JavaVersion.VERSION_17
        targetCompatibility = JavaVersion.VERSION_17
    }
}
dependencies {
    implementation(platform(libs.androidx.compose.bom))
    implementation(libs.androidx.activity.compose)
    implementation(libs.androidx.compose.material3)
    implementation(libs.androidx.lifecycle.viewmodel.compose)
}
```

Key rules: one repository list in `settings.gradle.kts` only, never
hardcode versions in module files, and keep `namespace` in Gradle —
the manifest `package` attribute is gone.

## Version Catalogs

The version catalog (`gradle/libs.versions.toml`) is the single
source of truth for every version and dependency coordinate.

```toml
[versions]
agp = "9.5"
kotlin = "2.4.21"
coreKtx = "1.17.0"
composeBom = "2026.09.00"
lifecycle = "2.9.4"

[libraries]
androidx-core-ktx = { group = "androidx.core", name = "core-ktx", version.ref = "coreKtx" }
androidx-compose-bom = { group = "androidx.compose", name = "compose-bom", version.ref = "composeBom" }
androidx-activity-compose = { group = "androidx.activity", name = "activity-compose", version = "1.11.0" }
androidx-lifecycle-viewmodel-compose = { group = "androidx.lifecycle", name = "lifecycle-viewmodel-compose", version.ref = "lifecycle" }
androidx-compose-material3 = { group = "androidx.compose.material3", name = "material3" }

[plugins]
android-application = { id = "com.android.application", version.ref = "agp" }
kotlin-compose = { id = "org.jetbrains.kotlin.plugin.compose", version.ref = "kotlin" }
```

In build files you reference `libs.androidx.core.ktx` and
`libs.plugins.android.application`. Update a version once, everywhere.

## AGP, Kotlin, and the Compose Compiler

- **AGP ↔ Gradle ↔ JDK:** AGP 9.5 needs Gradle 9.6 and JDK 17. The
  Gradle wrapper pins the Gradle version — always build through
  `./gradlew`, never a system Gradle, so every machine and CI uses
  the same version.
- **Compose compiler = Kotlin version:** since Kotlin 2.0, the
  Compose compiler ships with Kotlin and is applied with the
  `org.jetbrains.kotlin.plugin.compose` plugin. Its version is the
  Kotlin version — there is no separate compiler version to align.
  Never set `kotlinCompilerExtensionVersion` in a Kotlin 2.x project.
- **Compose libraries ↔ BOM:** the Compose BOM aligns all Compose
  versions. Use BOM 2026.09.00 (verified) as a platform dependency
  and declare Compose libraries without versions.

```kotlin
dependencies {
    implementation(platform(libs.androidx.compose.bom))
    androidTestImplementation(platform(libs.androidx.compose.bom))
    implementation(libs.androidx.compose.ui)
    implementation(libs.androidx.compose.material3)
}
```

## Build Types, Flavors, and Signing

- `debug` — debuggable, no minification, fast iteration.
- `release` — minified with R8, signed with your upload key.
- Product flavors (optional) for variants such as `free` / `paid`
  or `dev` / `prod` environments.

Store signing keys outside the repo. Reference them through
environment variables or a `keystore.properties` file that is never
committed. Prefer Play App Signing, where Google holds the final
signing key and you hold the upload key.

Useful commands:

```bash
./gradlew assembleDebug        # debug APK
./gradlew bundleRelease        # release AAB for Play
./gradlew test                 # unit tests
./gradlew connectedAndroidTest # instrumented tests
./gradlew lint                 # static analysis
```

## Module Layout

Start with a single `:app` module. Split when build time, team size,
or clear boundaries justify it — not before.

```
:app            — entry point, navigation graph, DI wiring
:core:ui        — shared Compose components and theme
:core:data      — repositories, database, network
:core:model     — shared model classes (plain Kotlin module)
:feature:home   — one feature per module
:feature:settings
```

Rules: feature modules depend on core modules, never on each other;
`:app` depends on everything and wires it together; pure Kotlin
modules build fastest — put models and pure logic there when
Android APIs are not needed; keep module APIs small.

## Tooling Beyond Gradle

- **Lint** — run in CI; fix or explicitly baseline warnings.
- **KSP** — Kotlin Symbol Processing runs annotation processors
  such as Room and Hilt compilers, much faster than kapt. Prefer
  KSP libraries; kapt is legacy.
- **R8** — shrinks and obfuscates release builds. Test release
  builds before shipping.
- **Baseline Profiles** — generated profiles that measurably
  improve startup and scroll performance.
- **Android Studio tools** — Layout Inspector, App Inspection,
  Profiler, and APK Analyzer are the first stop for performance
  and size questions.

## Common Setup Mistakes

- Versions scattered across module files instead of the catalog.
- Building with a system Gradle instead of the wrapper.
- Committing `local.properties` or signing keys.
- Adding a repository block in a module file, conflicting with
  `FAIL_ON_PROJECT_REPOS`.
- Raising `targetSdk` without reading that version's behavior changes.
- Splitting into many modules on day one, then fighting coupling.


---

<!-- ===== docs/04-app-anatomy-lifecycle-and-components.md ===== -->

# App Anatomy, Lifecycle, and Components

Every Android app is a set of components the system starts and stops
as needed. Understanding the manifest, the Application class, the
Activity lifecycle, and the four component types prevents most
beginner bugs.

Official references:
- App components: https://developer.android.com/guide/components/fundamentals
- Activity lifecycle: https://developer.android.com/guide/components/activities/activity-lifecycle
- Manifest overview: https://developer.android.com/guide/topics/manifest/manifest-intro
- App links: https://developer.android.com/training/app-links

## The AndroidManifest

`AndroidManifest.xml` declares your app to the system: components,
permissions, features, and metadata. Gradle merges library manifests
into the final one.

```xml
<manifest xmlns:android="http://schemas.android.com/apk/res/android">
    <uses-permission android:name="android.permission.INTERNET" />
    <application
        android:name=".MyApplication"
        android:label="@string/app_name"
        android:theme="@style/Theme.MyApp"
        android:icon="@mipmap/ic_launcher">
        <activity android:name=".MainActivity" android:exported="true">
            <intent-filter>
                <action android:name="android.intent.action.MAIN" />
                <category android:name="android.intent.category.LAUNCHER" />
            </intent-filter>
        </activity>
    </application>
</manifest>
```

Key rules:

- `android:exported` is required on every component with an intent
  filter (API 31+). Set it deliberately: `true` only when other apps
  should start the component.
- Request permissions at the top level; use them at point of use.
- Declare foreground service types and hardware features here too.

## The Application Class

`Application` is created before any other component. Use it only for
app-wide setup that must happen first: the dependency injection
graph, logging, and crash reporting initialization.

```kotlin
class MyApplication : Application() {
    lateinit var appContainer: AppContainer
        private set
    override fun onCreate() {
        super.onCreate()
        appContainer = AppContainer(this)
    }
}
```

Do not store Activity references or mutable screen state here — that
leaks memory. Heavy work in `onCreate` delays startup; defer
anything not needed immediately.

## Activity and Its Lifecycle

An Activity hosts UI. In a Compose app there is usually one Activity
whose content is set once with `setContent`.

```kotlin
class MainActivity : ComponentActivity() {
    override fun onCreate(savedInstanceState: Bundle?) {
        super.onCreate(savedInstanceState)
        enableEdgeToEdge()
        setContent { MyAppTheme { AppNavigation() } }
    }
}
```

Lifecycle callbacks, in order:

1. `onCreate` — inflate UI, restore state, start observing data.
2. `onStart` — UI becomes visible.
3. `onResume` — UI is in the foreground and interactive.
4. `onPause` — losing focus; save light state, pause animations.
5. `onStop` — no longer visible; release heavier resources.
6. `onDestroy` — final teardown; not guaranteed if the process is
   killed first.
7. `onRestart` — returning from stopped to started.

In Compose, prefer `DisposableEffect` and lifecycle-aware collection
over overriding callbacks for UI work:

```kotlin
lifecycleScope.launch {
    repeatOnLifecycle(Lifecycle.State.STARTED) {
        viewModel.uiState.collect { render(it) }
    }
}
```

## Configuration Changes and Process Death

Rotating the device, switching dark mode, or changing locale are
configuration changes. By default the Activity is destroyed and
recreated. State survives three ways:

- **ViewModel** — survives configuration changes, not process death.
- **Saved state** — `SavedStateHandle`, `rememberSaveable`, and
  `onSaveInstanceState` survive recreation and process death. Keep
  only small, serializable values here (IDs, query text).
- **Persistent storage** — Room, DataStore, or files survive all.

Process death: the system can kill a backgrounded app's process at
any time. When the user returns, the app must restore from saved
state and storage. Test with "Don't keep activities" and by
backgrounding under memory pressure. Never rely on static fields or
the Application object to keep screen state alive across a kill.

## The Four Component Types

### Activities
One screen of UI. Modern apps use a single Activity with Compose
destinations or Fragments inside it.

### Services
Background work without UI. A **foreground service** does ongoing
work the user can see (music, navigation, upload with progress) and
requires a notification plus a declared FGS type and permission at
API 34+. For deferrable work, use WorkManager instead of a service.

### BroadcastReceivers
Respond to system or app broadcasts (boot completed, connectivity
changes). Many implicit broadcasts are restricted; prefer
context-registered receivers or platform callbacks in-app.

```kotlin
class BootReceiver : BroadcastReceiver() {
    override fun onReceive(context: Context, intent: Intent) {
        if (intent.action == Intent.ACTION_BOOT_COMPLETED) {
            // Reschedule WorkManager work — keep this fast.
        }
    }
}
```

Receivers must finish quickly (about 10 seconds). Offload real work
to WorkManager.

### ContentProviders
Share structured data through a URI-based API (`content://…`). Most
apps consume providers (contacts, media) rather than publish one.
Publish one only when other apps genuinely need your data; guard it
with permissions and `exported=false` by default.

## Intents

Intents request an action from another component.

```kotlin
// Explicit — target a component in your own app
val intent = Intent(context, DetailActivity::class.java).apply {
    putExtra("item_id", 42L)
}
context.startActivity(intent)

// Implicit — let the system choose an app
val share = Intent(Intent.ACTION_SEND).apply {
    type = "text/plain"
    putExtra(Intent.EXTRA_TEXT, "Check this out")
}
context.startActivity(Intent.createChooser(share, null))
```

Prefer explicit intents inside your app. For implicit intents,
always handle the case where no app can respond.

## Deep Links and App Links

A deep link opens a specific screen from a URI. An App Link is a
verified HTTPS deep link that opens your app instead of the browser.

```xml
<intent-filter android:autoVerify="true">
    <action android:name="android.intent.action.VIEW" />
    <category android:name="android.intent.category.DEFAULT" />
    <category android:name="android.intent.category.BROWSABLE" />
    <data android:scheme="https" android:host="www.example.com" />
</intent-filter>
```

App Links require a Digital Asset Links file at
`https://www.example.com/.well-known/assetlinks.json` with your
package name and signing certificate fingerprint. In Compose
Navigation, declare the same URI pattern as a `deepLink` on the
destination so handling stays in one place. Handle incoming links in
both cold start and `onNewIntent` — a running single-top Activity
receives later links through `onNewIntent`, not a fresh `onCreate`.

## Practical Rules

- One Activity, many destinations, is the default architecture.
- Keep `onCreate` fast; defer non-essential initialization.
- Save small state in saved state, big state in storage, and keep
  in-memory state in a ViewModel.
- Assume process death can happen whenever the app is backgrounded.
- Set `exported` deliberately on every component.


---

<!-- ===== docs/05-jetpack-compose-ui.md ===== -->

# Jetpack Compose UI

Jetpack Compose is the modern, declarative UI toolkit for Android. You describe what the UI should look like for a given state, and Compose updates it when the state changes. No XML layouts, no manual view lookups.

Official references:
- Compose docs: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose
- Thinking in Compose: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/mental-model
- Material 3: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/designsystems/material3
- Compose BOM: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/bom

Use the Compose BOM (2026.09.00 verified) to align library versions, and the Compose compiler plugin — its version follows your Kotlin version since Kotlin 2.0.

## Composables

A composable is a function marked `@Composable` that emits UI.

```kotlin
@Composable
fun Greeting(name: String, modifier: Modifier = Modifier) {
    Text(text = "Hello, $name", modifier = modifier)
}

@Composable
fun ProfileCard(user: User, modifier: Modifier = Modifier) {
    Card(modifier = modifier.padding(16.dp)) {
        Column(modifier = Modifier.padding(16.dp)) {
            Text(user.name, style = MaterialTheme.typography.titleLarge)
            Text(user.email ?: "", style = MaterialTheme.typography.bodyMedium)
        }
    }
}
```

Conventions: every composable accepts `modifier: Modifier = Modifier` and applies it to its root element; composables are fast and side-effect free (no network, database, or file work inside them); names use PascalCase and read like nouns.

## State and Recomposition

When state a composable reads changes, Compose re-runs that composable — recomposition — and updates only what changed.

```kotlin
@Composable
fun Counter() {
    var count by remember { mutableIntStateOf(0) }
    Column {
        Text("Count: $count")
        Button(onClick = { count++ }) { Text("Add") }
    }
}
```

Core tools:

- `remember` — keeps a value across recompositions.
- `mutableStateOf` — observable state Compose tracks.
- `rememberSaveable` — also survives configuration changes and
process death for small, saveable values.
- `derivedStateOf` — computes state from other state, notifying
only when the result actually changes.

Use primitive state types (`mutableIntStateOf`, `mutableFloatStateOf`, `mutableLongStateOf`) for numbers to avoid boxing overhead.

## State Hoisting

State hoisting moves state up so a composable becomes stateless: it receives the value and an event callback.

```kotlin
// Stateless — reusable and testable
@Composable
fun NameField(name: String, onNameChange: (String) -> Unit) {
    TextField(value = name, onValueChange = onNameChange,
        label = { Text("Name") })
}
// Stateful wrapper owns the state
@Composable
fun NameForm() {
    var name by rememberSaveable { mutableStateOf("") }
    NameField(name = name, onNameChange = { name = it })
}
```

Pattern: **state flows down, events flow up.** Screen state normally lives in a ViewModel and reaches composables as a collected flow:

```kotlin
@Composable
fun HomeRoute(viewModel: HomeViewModel = viewModel()) {
    val state by viewModel.uiState.collectAsStateWithLifecycle()
    HomeScreen(state = state, onRefresh = viewModel::refresh)
}
```

`collectAsStateWithLifecycle` pauses collection when the UI is not visible, saving battery and avoiding wasted work.

## Material 3

Material 3 is the default design system. Wrap your app in your theme and use Material components (`Button`, `Card`, `Scaffold`, `TopAppBar`, `NavigationBar`, `TextField`, `Switch`) instead of building equivalents from scratch.

```kotlin
@Composable
fun MyAppTheme(content: @Composable () -> Unit) {
    MaterialTheme(
        colorScheme = lightColorScheme(
            primary = Color(0xFF6750A4),
            secondary = Color(0xFF625B71)),
        typography = Typography(),
        content = content)
}
```

Support dark theme with `darkColorScheme` and dynamic color on API 31+ (`dynamicLightColorScheme` / `dynamicDarkColorScheme`) when your brand allows it. Read colors and text styles from `MaterialTheme.colorScheme` and `MaterialTheme.typography` — never hardcode hex colors inside screens.

## Layouts

- `Column` — vertical arrangement.
- `Row` — horizontal arrangement.
- `Box` — stacked elements, like a frame layout.
- `Spacer` — empty space with a fixed size.
- `Modifier` chains control size, padding, alignment, behavior.

```kotlin
@Composable
fun PriceRow(label: String, price: String) {
    Row(
        modifier = Modifier.fillMaxWidth().padding(16.dp),
        horizontalArrangement = Arrangement.SpaceBetween,
        verticalAlignment = Alignment.CenterVertically
    ) {
        Text(label)
        Text(price, style = MaterialTheme.typography.titleMedium)
    }
}
```

Modifier order matters: `padding` then `background` paints a different result than `background` then `padding`. Plain Row/Column/Box covers most apps; `ConstraintLayout` for Compose and custom `Layout` composables exist for complex cases.

## Lists with LazyColumn

Never put a long list in a regular `Column` — every item is composed at once. Lazy layouts compose only visible items.

```kotlin
@Composable
fun ItemList(items: List<Item>) {
    LazyColumn(
        contentPadding = PaddingValues(16.dp),
        verticalArrangement = Arrangement.spacedBy(8.dp)
    ) {
        items(items = items, key = { it.id }) { item ->
            ItemRow(item = item)
        }
    }
}
```

Always provide a stable `key` so Compose tracks items correctly. Use `LazyRow` for horizontal lists and `LazyVerticalGrid` for grids. Add `contentPadding` instead of padding on the list itself so the first and last items can scroll clear of edges.

## Theming and Design Tokens

Define colors, typography, and shapes once in a `ui/theme` package. Screens reference tokens, not raw values. This makes dark mode, rebranding, and accessibility fixes one-place changes. Use `sp` for text and `dp` for layout so user font-size settings are respected.

## Previews

`@Preview` renders a composable in Android Studio without running the app. Preview the stateless version of each screen.

```kotlin
@Preview(showBackground = true)
@Preview(showBackground = true,
    uiMode = Configuration.UI_MODE_NIGHT_YES)
@Composable
private fun ProfileCardPreview() {
    MyAppTheme { ProfileCard(User(1, "Ada", null)) }
}
```

Add previews for light, dark, loading, empty, and error states — they double as living documentation of your design system.

## Graphics and Canvas — Introduction

`Canvas` gives you a `DrawScope` for custom drawing: charts, game boards, progress rings, and signatures.

```kotlin
@Composable
fun ProgressRing(progress: Float) {
    Canvas(modifier = Modifier.size(120.dp)) {
        drawArc(
            color = Color.Blue, startAngle = -90f,
            sweepAngle = 360f * progress, useCenter = false,
            style = Stroke(width = 12f))
    }
}
```

`drawLine`, `drawRect`, `drawCircle`, `drawPath`, and `drawText` cover most needs. Compose Canvas works well for turn-based and puzzle games; action games usually need a dedicated engine or surface — see the games guides in this knowledge base.

## Performance Basics

- Keep composables small so recomposition stays narrow.
- Pass stable, immutable data (data classes, immutable collections).
- Defer state reads: read changing state as low in the tree as
possible, or pass a lambda for values that change every frame.
- Use `key` in lazy lists and `remember` for expensive calculations.
- Measure before optimizing: Layout Inspector shows recomposition
counts, and Baseline Profiles improve startup and scroll.
- Avoid allocating objects in hot composables where a remembered
value would do.

## Common Mistakes

- Writing to state during composition (use event handlers or
`LaunchedEffect` instead).
- Using `collectAsState` where lifecycle-aware collection is needed.
- Forgetting the `modifier` parameter on reusable components.
- Building scrollable content in `Column` with `verticalScroll` for
hundreds of items instead of `LazyColumn`.


---

<!-- ===== docs/06-views-xml-and-interop.md ===== -->

# Views, XML, and Interop

Jetpack Compose is the default for new UI, but the classic View
system is not gone. Existing apps, some libraries, and a few widget
types still use Views and XML. This guide covers when Views are still
the right choice and how the two systems work together.

Official references:
- View Binding: https://developer.android.com/topic/libraries/view-binding
- Compose in Views: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/migrate/interoperability-apis/compose-in-views
- Views in Compose: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/migrate/interoperability-apis/views-in-compose
- Migration strategy: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/migrate/strategy

## When Views Are Still Right

Keep or choose Views when:

- You are maintaining a large, working Views codebase. A rewrite is
  a cost, not an automatic improvement.
- A required library only ships a View (some map, chart, ad, and
  legacy SDK surfaces).
- A screen depends on a mature custom View with no Compose equal yet.
- The widget in question is a home-screen widget built on
  RemoteViews — Compose has Glance for widgets, but classic
  RemoteViews remains common in existing code.
- WebView-heavy screens: `WebView` itself is a View, wrapped in
  Compose with `AndroidView` when needed.

For all new screens in a new app, start with Compose.

## ViewBinding

ViewBinding generates a binding class per layout, replacing
`findViewById` with type-safe, null-safe references. It is the
standard for any Views code you still write.

Enable it in the module build file:

```kotlin
android {
    buildFeatures { viewBinding = true }
}
```

Use it in an Activity:

```kotlin
class ProfileActivity : AppCompatActivity() {
    private lateinit var binding: ActivityProfileBinding

    override fun onCreate(savedInstanceState: Bundle?) {
        super.onCreate(savedInstanceState)
        binding = ActivityProfileBinding.inflate(layoutInflater)
        setContentView(binding.root)
        binding.nameText.text = "Ada"
        binding.saveButton.setOnClickListener { save() }
    }
}
```

In a Fragment, clear the binding in `onDestroyView` to avoid leaking
the view hierarchy:

```kotlin
private var _binding: FragmentHomeBinding? = null
private val binding get() = _binding!!

override fun onDestroyView() {
    super.onDestroyView()
    _binding = null
}
```

Data Binding is a separate, older feature. Do not adopt it for new
work; ViewBinding plus explicit code is simpler and is what current
guidance recommends.

## RecyclerView Essentials

RecyclerView remains the workhorse list for Views screens. Its pieces:

- **Adapter** — binds data to item views.
- **ViewHolder** — holds references for one item layout.
- **LayoutManager** — linear, grid, or staggered placement.
- **ListAdapter + DiffUtil** — computes minimal updates between list
  versions on a background thread. Always prefer `ListAdapter` over
  a plain adapter with `notifyDataSetChanged()`.

```kotlin
class ItemAdapter : ListAdapter<Item, ItemAdapter.Holder>(DiffCallback) {
    object DiffCallback : DiffUtil.ItemCallback<Item>() {
        override fun areItemsTheSame(o: Item, n: Item) = o.id == n.id
        override fun areContentsTheSame(o: Item, n: Item) = o == n
    }

    class Holder(val binding: ItemRowBinding) :
        RecyclerView.ViewHolder(binding.root)

    override fun onCreateViewHolder(parent: ViewGroup, viewType: Int): Holder {
        val binding = ItemRowBinding.inflate(
            LayoutInflater.from(parent.context), parent, false)
        return Holder(binding)
    }

    override fun onBindViewHolder(holder: Holder, position: Int) {
        val item = getItem(position)
        holder.binding.title.text = item.title
    }
}
```

Submit new data with `adapter.submitList(newList)` and let DiffUtil
animate the changes.

## Compose in Views

Add Compose to an existing Views screen with a `ComposeView`. This
is the main incremental migration path: one screen, or even one
section of a screen, at a time.

```xml
<androidx.compose.ui.platform.ComposeView
    android:id="@+id/compose_section"
    android:layout_width="match_parent"
    android:layout_height="wrap_content" />
```

```kotlin
binding.composeSection.setContent {
    MyAppTheme { PromoBanner(onClick = { /* handle */ }) }
}
```

In Fragments, set a composition strategy appropriate to the view's
lifecycle (the default works in most cases; use
`DisposeOnViewTreeLifecycleDestroyed` when the ComposeView lives in
a Fragment view hierarchy).

## Views in Compose

Embed an existing View inside a composable with `AndroidView`:

```kotlin
@Composable
fun MapPlaceholder() {
    AndroidView(
        factory = { context -> MapView(context) },
        update = { view -> view.onResume() },
        modifier = Modifier.fillMaxWidth().height(240.dp)
    )
}
```

- `factory` creates the View once.
- `update` runs on recomposition to push new state into the View.
- You own the wrapped View's lifecycle: forward `onPause`,
  `onDestroy`, or release calls as the library requires.
- Use `AndroidViewBinding` when you want to embed an entire inflated
  XML layout instead of a single View.

## Sharing State Between the Two Systems

- A single ViewModel can serve both a Fragment and the composables
  inside it — collect its `StateFlow` in Compose and observe it in
  the Fragment with `repeatOnLifecycle`.
- Theme carefully: Compose inside a Views app needs its own
  `MaterialTheme` wrapper; Views inside Compose will follow the
  Activity's XML theme, not the Compose theme.
- Navigation: during migration, the host Fragment or Activity still
  owns navigation until the whole graph moves to Compose.

## Migration Stance

The recommended stance for an existing Views app:

1. New screens in Compose by default.
2. Migrate leaf screens (settings, about, detail) before complex
   core screens.
3. Keep shared design components in Compose once created; do not
   rebuild the same component in both systems.
4. Move navigation last, when most destinations are composables.
5. Remove XML layouts and adapters only when their last caller is
   migrated — dead code removal is a cleanup step, not a migration
   step.

Do not do a big-bang rewrite of a shipping app. Interop exists
precisely so migration can be gradual and reversible.

## What Not to Do

- Do not mix `findViewById` into new code — ViewBinding replaces it.
- Do not create a ComposeView per list item inside RecyclerView
  without measuring; composition per row has a cost. Prefer a full
  Compose list when a list is being rebuilt.
- Do not nest scrollable containers of the same direction (a
  RecyclerView inside a scrolling parent, or a LazyColumn inside a
  `verticalScroll` Column) — measure and restructure instead.
- Do not adopt Data Binding for new Views work.


---

<!-- ===== docs/07-navigation-and-app-structure.md ===== -->

# Navigation and App Structure

Most modern Android apps use a single Activity and navigate between
composable destinations inside it. This guide covers Navigation
Compose with type-safe routes, the newer Navigation 3 library, deep
links, and how to choose between them deliberately.

Official references:
- Navigation Compose: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/navigation
- Type-safe navigation: https://developer.android.com/guide/navigation/design/type-safety
- Navigation 3: https://developer.android.com/guide/navigation/navigation-3
- Deep links: https://developer.android.com/training/app-links

## Single-Activity Structure

One `ComponentActivity` hosts everything:

```kotlin
class MainActivity : ComponentActivity() {
    override fun onCreate(savedInstanceState: Bundle?) {
        super.onCreate(savedInstanceState)
        setContent { MyAppTheme { AppNavHost() } }
    }
}
```

Benefits: one lifecycle to manage, shared app bars and bottom
navigation are easy, transitions are consistent, and deep links land
in one place. Multiple Activities are still valid for strongly
separate flows (a share target, a standalone viewer), but they are
the exception.

## Navigation Compose — 2.10.2

Navigation Compose (version 2.10.2 in the 2.x line) is the mature,
widely used option. Since Navigation 2.8, routes are type-safe:
destinations and arguments are serializable Kotlin objects instead
of string routes with hand-parsed arguments.

Define routes:

```kotlin
@Serializable
object HomeRoute

@Serializable
data class DetailRoute(val itemId: Long)

@Serializable
data class SearchRoute(val query: String = "")
```

Build the graph:

```kotlin
@Composable
fun AppNavHost() {
    val navController = rememberNavController()
    NavHost(navController = navController, startDestination = HomeRoute) {
        composable<HomeRoute> {
            HomeScreen(onItemClick = { id ->
                navController.navigate(DetailRoute(id))
            })
        }
        composable<DetailRoute> { backStackEntry ->
            val route = backStackEntry.toRoute<DetailRoute>()
            DetailScreen(itemId = route.itemId)
        }
        composable<SearchRoute> { backStackEntry ->
            val route = backStackEntry.toRoute<SearchRoute>()
            SearchScreen(initialQuery = route.query)
        }
    }
}
```

Navigation rules:

- Navigate with route objects, never string concatenation.
- Read arguments with `toRoute<T>()` — no manual bundle parsing.
- Use `launchSingleTop`, `restoreState`, and `popUpTo` with
  `saveState` for bottom-navigation tabs so each tab keeps its stack:

```kotlin
navController.navigate(tabRoute) {
    popUpTo(navController.graph.findStartDestination().id) {
        saveState = true
    }
    launchSingleTop = true
    restoreState = true
}
```

- Handle results by sharing a ViewModel scoped to a navigation
  graph, or by passing IDs and re-reading data — not by stuffing
  objects into bundles.

## Navigation 3 — 1.2.1 (Stable, November 2026)

Navigation 3 reached stable 1.2.1 in November 2026. It is a new
library built around a simple idea: **you own the back stack.** The
back stack is a plain list of keys in your state; Navigation 3
renders the top entry and provides scene and transition machinery.

Core shape (simplified):

```kotlin
// Keys are your own serializable types
@Serializable data class HomeKey(val userId: Long) : NavKey
@Serializable data class DetailKey(val itemId: Long) : NavKey

@Composable
fun AppNavigation3() {
    val backStack = rememberNavBackStack(HomeKey(userId = 1))
    NavDisplay(
        backStack = backStack,
        onBack = { backStack.removeLastOrNull() },
        entryProvider = entryProvider {
            entry<HomeKey> { key -> HomeScreen(key.userId) }
            entry<DetailKey> { key -> DetailScreen(key.itemId) }
        }
    )
}
```

Key concepts:

- **Own the back stack** — navigate by adding a key, go back by
  removing one. Testing navigation is testing a list.
- **Scenes** — a Scene decides how entries are displayed: a single
  pane, a list-detail two-pane layout on large screens, or a dialog
  scene. Scenes are how adaptive layouts are expressed.
- **Entry providers** — map each key type to its content.
- State is saved and restored through the same serialization you
  already use for keys.

## Choosing Between Them — A Deliberate Choice

Navigation 3 is **not a mandatory migration**. Navigation Compose
remains fully supported. Choose deliberately:

| Situation | Sensible choice |
|-----------|-----------------|
| Existing app on Navigation Compose | Stay; migrate only for a clear payoff |
| New app, simple single-pane flow | Either — Navigation Compose is familiar and proven |
| New app needing list-detail / multi-pane scenes | Navigation 3 Scenes are a strong reason |
| Team wants direct control of the back stack as state | Navigation 3 |
| Heavy reliance on existing Navigation Compose integrations | Navigation Compose |

Do not run both libraries in one app graph. Pick one navigation
system per app and write it down in your architecture notes.

## Deep Links

Declare deep links in the manifest (see the app anatomy guide) and
mirror them on destinations so Navigation handles the intent:

```kotlin
composable<DetailRoute>(
    deepLinks = listOf(
        navDeepLink<DetailRoute>(basePath = "https://www.example.com/items")
    )
) { /* destination */ }
```

Guidelines:

- Every public content screen should be reachable by a stable URI.
- Deep link arguments are untrusted input — validate IDs and fall
  back to a safe screen when a link is malformed.
- Test links with `adb shell am start -a android.intent.action.VIEW -d "URL"`.
- App Links (verified HTTPS) need the `assetlinks.json` file; plain
  deep links work without it but may show an app chooser.

## App Structure Around Navigation

- **Bottom navigation** for 3–5 top-level destinations. On large
  screens, swap it for a navigation rail or drawer.
- **Nested graphs** group a feature's screens (checkout, onboarding)
  with their own start destination and shared ViewModel scope.
- **Dialogs and bottom sheets** are destinations too in Compose
  navigation (`dialog { }` in Navigation Compose), which keeps back
  behavior correct.
- Keep the NavController (or the Navigation 3 back stack) in the
  top-level app composable. Pass navigation callbacks down as
  lambdas (`onItemClick: (Long) -> Unit`) instead of passing the
  controller into every screen — screens stay testable and reusable
  in previews.

## Common Mistakes

- Passing whole objects as navigation arguments instead of an ID.
- Navigating from a composable body instead of an event handler or
  a `LaunchedEffect` — composition can re-run and double-navigate.
- Forgetting `saveState`/`restoreState` on tab navigation, which
  destroys each tab's scroll position and stack.
- Reading arguments as raw strings when type-safe routes exist.
- Treating Navigation 3 as an obligatory upgrade and rewriting a
  working Navigation Compose graph without a feature reason.


---

<!-- ===== docs/08-architecture-patterns.md ===== -->

# Architecture Patterns

Good architecture keeps an app testable and changeable as it grows.
This guide follows the official Android app architecture guidance:
a UI layer, a data layer, an optional domain layer, unidirectional
data flow, and offline-first data where it matters.

Official references:
- Guide to app architecture: https://developer.android.com/topic/architecture
- UI layer: https://developer.android.com/topic/architecture/ui-layer
- Data layer: https://developer.android.com/topic/architecture/data-layer
- Domain layer: https://developer.android.com/topic/architecture/domain-layer
- Modularization: https://developer.android.com/topic/modularization

## The Layers

```
UI layer        — screens, ViewModels, UI state (Compose observes it)
Domain layer    — use cases (optional; shared business logic)
Data layer      — repositories, data sources (network, database, files)
```

Data flows up as observable streams; events flow down as function
calls. Each layer depends only on the layer below it.

## UI Layer and MVVM

The standard pattern is MVVM: a ViewModel holds screen state and
exposes it as a `StateFlow` of immutable UI state. The UI renders
state and sends user events to the ViewModel.

```kotlin
data class HomeUiState(
    val isLoading: Boolean = false,
    val items: List<Item> = emptyList(),
    val errorMessage: String? = null
)

@HiltViewModel
class HomeViewModel @Inject constructor(
    private val repository: ItemRepository
) : ViewModel() {

    private val _uiState = MutableStateFlow(HomeUiState())
    val uiState: StateFlow<HomeUiState> = _uiState.asStateFlow()

    init { load() }

    fun load() {
        viewModelScope.launch {
            _uiState.update { it.copy(isLoading = true, errorMessage = null) }
            runCatching { repository.getItems() }
                .onSuccess { items ->
                    _uiState.update { it.copy(isLoading = false, items = items) }
                }
                .onFailure { e ->
                    _uiState.update { it.copy(isLoading = false, errorMessage = e.message) }
                }
        }
    }
}
```

Rules:

- One UI state object per screen (or a small set of flows combined
  into one). The UI must be renderable from state alone.
- The ViewModel never holds Views, Composables, or Contexts (an
  Application context through DI is acceptable where required).
- User actions are ViewModel functions: `onSaveClick`, `onQueryChange`.

## Unidirectional Data Flow (UDF)

UDF means state has one source and changes in one direction:

1. UI sends an event to the ViewModel.
2. ViewModel (through repositories) updates the data.
3. New state flows back to the UI, which re-renders.
