UDF makes bugs traceable: if the UI is wrong, the state is wrong, and
there is exactly one place that produces state. Never mutate shared
state from the UI directly.

## MVI — When It Fits

MVI (Model-View-Intent) is a stricter UDF variant: all events go
through one `onEvent(intent)` entry point and state changes happen
in a single reducer. Consider MVI when:

- A screen has many interacting event sources.
- You need an auditable event log for debugging or replay.
- A team already standardizes on it.

For most screens, plain MVVM with explicit event functions is
simpler and equally safe. Do not adopt MVI machinery (stores,
reducers, effect channels) for a two-button screen.

## Data Layer and Repositories

A repository is the single source of truth for one kind of data. It
hides where data comes from — network, cache, or database — behind
a clean API of flows and suspend functions.

```kotlin
interface ItemRepository {
    fun observeItems(): Flow<List<Item>>
    suspend fun refresh()
    suspend fun getItem(id: Long): Item?
}

class DefaultItemRepository(
    private val api: ItemApi,
    private val dao: ItemDao,
    private val ioDispatcher: CoroutineDispatcher
) : ItemRepository {
    override fun observeItems(): Flow<List<Item>> =
        dao.observeAll().map { rows -> rows.map { it.toDomain() } }

    override suspend fun refresh() = withContext(ioDispatcher) {
        val remote = api.fetchItems()
        dao.replaceAll(remote.map { it.toEntity() })
    }

    override suspend fun getItem(id: Long): Item? =
        dao.getById(id)?.toDomain()
}
```

- Expose the repository as an interface; bind the implementation
  through dependency injection. Tests substitute a fake.
- Map between network DTOs, database entities, and domain models.
  Never let a DTO leak into the UI layer.
- The UI never calls an API client or DAO directly.

## Domain Layer and Use Cases

The domain layer is optional. Add use cases when business logic is
shared by several ViewModels or grows complex enough to deserve its
own tests.

```kotlin
class GetSortedItemsUseCase(
    private val repository: ItemRepository
) {
    operator fun invoke(): Flow<List<Item>> =
        repository.observeItems().map { items ->
            items.sortedBy { it.name }
        }
}
```

Skip the domain layer for a small app — repositories plus ViewModels
are enough. Add it when duplication appears, not in advance.

## Offline-First

Offline-first means the local database is the source of truth the UI
reads, and the network refreshes it in the background:

1. UI observes Room through the repository — it always has data to
   show, even offline.
2. A refresh fetches remote data and writes it into Room.
3. Room emits, the UI updates. No spinner-driven screens after the
   first load.

Write conflicts and sync rules (last-write-wins, server wins, queued
mutations) must be decided per data type and documented. Use
WorkManager for reliable background sync with retry and constraints.

## Modularization

Split code into modules when a single `:app` module gets slow to
build or hard to navigate. Common pattern (see the setup guide):
`:app`, `:core:*` shared modules, and one module per feature.

- Features expose only their navigation entry and public API.
- Features never depend on each other; shared needs go in core.
- Modularization pays off at scale and costs indirection at small
  size. A three-screen app in nine modules is over-engineered.

## State Holders Beyond ViewModel

Small, reusable UI logic (a searchable list header, a text field
with validation) can live in a plain state holder class remembered
in Compose, separate from the screen ViewModel. Keep screen and
business state in the ViewModel; keep purely presentational state
(scroll, expansion, draft text) close to the UI.

## Testing Shape

Architecture exists to make testing possible:

- ViewModels: unit test with fake repositories and a test dispatcher.
- Repositories: unit test with fake APIs and in-memory Room.
- Use cases: pure unit tests, no Android dependencies.
- UI: Compose tests drive the stateless screen with fixed state.

If a class cannot be tested without the whole app, a boundary is in
the wrong place.

## Anti-Patterns

- **God Activity / God ViewModel** — one class knowing everything.
- **Logic in composables** — network or database calls in UI code.
- **Repository per screen** — repositories are per data type.
- **Exposing mutable state** — publish `StateFlow`, keep the
  `MutableStateFlow` private.
- **Skipping the repository** "just for this one call" — that call
  becomes five, each with its own caching and error behavior.


---

<!-- ===== docs/09-dependency-injection.md ===== -->

# Dependency Injection

Dependency injection (DI) means a class receives the objects it needs
from outside instead of creating them itself. That one habit makes
code testable, swappable, and honest about what it depends on.

Official references:
- DI on Android: https://developer.android.com/training/dependency-injection
- Hilt: https://developer.android.com/training/dependency-injection/hilt-android
- Hilt cheat sheet: https://developer.android.com/training/dependency-injection/hilt-android#hilt-cheat-sheet
- Koin for Android: https://insert-koin.io/docs/reference/koin-android/

## Manual DI — Always an Option

Manual DI is constructor parameters plus a container you write. For
small apps it is enough, and every developer should understand it
before using a framework.

```kotlin
class AppContainer(context: Context) {
    private val database: AppDatabase by lazy { buildDatabase(context) }
    val itemRepository: ItemRepository by lazy {
        DefaultItemRepository(api = RetrofitItemApi.create(), dao = database.itemDao())
    }
}

// Usage in a ViewModel factory or Composition root:
val viewModel = HomeViewModel(container.itemRepository)
```

Constructor injection is the core idea, with or without a framework:

```kotlin
class CheckoutViewModel(
    private val cartRepository: CartRepository,
    private val payments: PaymentGateway
) : ViewModel()
```

Never use service-locator calls scattered through business logic
(`App.instance.repository`) — the dependency becomes invisible and
untestable.

## Hilt — The Recommended Framework

Hilt is the standard DI library for Android, built on Dagger. It
generates the wiring code from annotations. Verified versions for
this knowledge base: androidx.hilt 1.4.0 and Dagger Hilt 2.57.x.
Use KSP for Hilt's compiler — not kapt — in new projects.

Setup sketch:

```kotlin
// Application
@HiltAndroidApp
class MyApplication : Application()

// Activity
@AndroidEntryPoint
class MainActivity : ComponentActivity()

// ViewModel
@HiltViewModel
class HomeViewModel @Inject constructor(
    private val repository: ItemRepository
) : ViewModel()
```

Provide dependencies Hilt cannot construct itself (interfaces,
library classes) in modules:

```kotlin
@Module
@InstallIn(SingletonComponent::class)
abstract class RepositoryModule {
    @Binds
    abstract fun bindItemRepository(impl: DefaultItemRepository): ItemRepository
}

@Module
@InstallIn(SingletonComponent::class)
object NetworkModule {
    @Provides
    @Singleton
    fun provideApi(): ItemApi = RetrofitItemApi.create()
}
```

- `@Binds` — bind an implementation to its interface (preferred,
  less generated code).
- `@Provides` — build an object with a function body.
- Install components decide lifetime: `SingletonComponent` lives as
  long as the app; `ActivityComponent` and `ViewModelComponent` are
  scoped shorter.

## Scoping

A scope controls how many instances exist and how long they live.

```kotlin
@Singleton
class AnalyticsTracker @Inject constructor() { /* one for the app */ }

@ActivityScoped
class ShareHelper @Inject constructor() { /* one per Activity */ }
```

Rules:

- Unscoped (no annotation) means a new instance at each injection
  site — right for cheap, stateless helpers.
- Scope deliberately. Making everything `@Singleton` creates hidden
  shared mutable state and memory that never clears.
- Never inject a short-lived scoped object into a longer-lived one;
  Hilt reports this as a compile error, which is a feature.

Compose integration: obtain Hilt ViewModels with
`hiltViewModel()` from `androidx.hilt.lifecycle.viewmodel.compose`
inside a navigation destination or route composable.

```kotlin
@Composable
fun HomeRoute(viewModel: HomeViewModel = hiltViewModel()) {
    val state by viewModel.uiState.collectAsStateWithLifecycle()
    HomeScreen(state)
}
```

## Koin — The KMP-Friendly Alternative

Koin (4.x) is a lightweight, pure-Kotlin DI library with no code
generation. It is popular in Kotlin Multiplatform projects because
it works identically on Android and other targets.

```kotlin
val dataModule = module {
    single<ItemRepository> { DefaultItemRepository(get(), get()) }
    factory { ItemApi.create() }
}
val uiModule = module {
    viewModel { HomeViewModel(get()) }
}

// Application.onCreate:
startKoin {
    androidContext(this@MyApplication)
    modules(dataModule, uiModule)
}

// In Compose:
val viewModel: HomeViewModel = koinViewModel()
```

Trade-offs versus Hilt:

- Koin resolves at runtime — a missing binding fails at runtime,
  not compile time. Its verification tools (`checkModules`) help in
  tests.
- Hilt checks the graph at compile time and is the official
  recommendation for Android-only apps.
- Choose Koin when KMP sharing is a real requirement, or a team
  already standardizes on it. Choose Hilt otherwise.

## Testing with DI

DI pays off in tests: replace real dependencies with fakes.

```kotlin
class FakeItemRepository : ItemRepository {
    private val items = MutableStateFlow<List<Item>>(emptyList())
    override fun observeItems(): Flow<List<Item>> = items
    suspend fun add(item: Item) { items.value += item }
    override suspend fun refresh() {}
    override suspend fun getItem(id: Long) = items.value.find { it.id == id }
}

@Test
fun loadsItems() = runTest {
    val fake = FakeItemRepository()
    val vm = HomeViewModel(fake)
    assertTrue(vm.uiState.value.items.isEmpty())
}
```

With Hilt, use `@TestInstallIn` to swap modules in instrumented
tests, and prefer constructing ViewModels directly with fakes in
plain unit tests — no framework needed for the fastest tests.

## Decision Guide

| Situation | Choice |
|-----------|--------|
| Tiny app or sample | Manual DI |
| Android app, any real size | Hilt |
| Kotlin Multiplatform shared DI | Koin |
| Learning DI concepts | Manual first, then Hilt |

## Common Mistakes

- Field injection everywhere instead of constructor injection.
- Scoping everything as singleton.
- Putting business logic inside `@Provides` functions — modules
  wire objects; they do not implement features.
- Two DI systems in one app. Pick one and remove the other.
- Injecting `Context` broadly. Inject the narrowest thing needed,
  and prefer the Application context for singletons to avoid leaking
  an Activity.


---

<!-- ===== docs/10-coroutines-flow-and-concurrency.md ===== -->

# Coroutines, Flow, and Concurrency

Kotlin coroutines are the standard way to do asynchronous work on
Android without blocking the UI thread. Flow adds an observable
stream on top. Together they cover networking, databases, UI state,
and events.

Official references:
- Coroutines on Android: https://developer.android.com/kotlin/coroutines
- Kotlin coroutines guide: https://kotlinlang.org/docs/coroutines-guide.html
- Flow: https://developer.android.com/kotlin/flow
- Testing coroutines: https://developer.android.com/kotlin/coroutines/test

Verified version: kotlinx.coroutines 1.11.0. Use the matched set —
`kotlinx-coroutines-core`, `kotlinx-coroutines-android`, and
`kotlinx-coroutines-test` all at the same version. A version mismatch
between core and test artifacts is a common source of strange errors.

## Suspend Functions

A `suspend` function can pause without blocking a thread. It must be
called from a coroutine or another suspend function.

```kotlin
suspend fun loadUser(id: Long): User {
    return api.fetchUser(id)      // network call, main-safe by design
}

class UserRepository(private val api: UserApi) {
    suspend fun user(id: Long): User = withContext(Dispatchers.IO) {
        api.fetchUserBlocking(id) // blocking library moved off main
    }
}
```

Design data-layer functions to be **main-safe**: callers on the main
thread should not need to think about threading. The data layer
switches dispatchers internally.

## Dispatchers

A dispatcher decides which thread pool a coroutine runs on:

- `Dispatchers.Main` — UI thread. All UI updates and most app code.
- `Dispatchers.IO` — blocking I/O: files, databases, blocking network.
- `Dispatchers.Default` — CPU-heavy work: sorting, parsing, image work.
- `Dispatchers.Unconfined` — testing and special cases only.

```kotlin
suspend fun parseLargeFile(file: File): Report =
    withContext(Dispatchers.Default) { heavyParse(file) }
```

Inject dispatchers into repositories instead of hardcoding them, so
tests can substitute a test dispatcher.

## Structured Concurrency

Coroutines live inside a scope with a lifetime. When the scope is
cancelled, its children are cancelled. This prevents leaks.

Android provides scopes tied to lifecycles:

- `viewModelScope` — cancelled when the ViewModel clears. The default
  for ViewModel work.
- `lifecycleScope` — in Activities and Fragments, cancelled on destroy.
- Application-level scope — create one deliberately in the Application
  or a singleton for work that must outlive screens, and own it.

```kotlin
class HomeViewModel(private val repo: ItemRepository) : ViewModel() {
    fun refresh() {
        viewModelScope.launch {
            repo.refresh()   // auto-cancelled if the screen goes away
        }
    }
}
```

Rules:

- Never use `GlobalScope`. It has no lifetime and leaks by design.
- `launch` starts work whose result you do not need (returns a Job).
- `async` starts work whose result you need (returns a Deferred);
  always `await` it inside the same structured scope.
- Exceptions in `launch` propagate to the scope's handler; exceptions
  in `async` surface at `await`. Use `supervisorScope` only when one
  child's failure must not cancel its siblings, and handle errors
  explicitly there.

```kotlin
suspend fun loadDashboard(): Dashboard = coroutineScope {
    val user = async { api.fetchUser() }
    val feed = async { api.fetchFeed() }
    Dashboard(user.await(), feed.await())   // parallel, structured
}
```

## Flow Basics

A `Flow<T>` is a cold stream: it produces values only while collected,
and restarts for each collector. Perfect for database observation and
search-as-you-type.

```kotlin
fun search(query: String): Flow<List<Item>> =
    dao.search(query).map { rows -> rows.map { it.toDomain() } }

val debounced: Flow<String> = queryFlow
    .debounce(300)
    .distinctUntilChanged()
    .flatMapLatest { search(it) }
```

Useful operators: `map`, `filter`, `combine`, `flatMapLatest`,
`debounce`, `distinctUntilChanged`, `catch`, `onStart`, `onEach`,
and `stateIn` / `shareIn`.

## StateFlow and SharedFlow

- **StateFlow** — holds one current value, always has a value, and
  conflates (slow collectors skip intermediates). This is the UI
  state holder: `val uiState: StateFlow<UiState>`.
- **SharedFlow** — a broadcast stream with configurable replay and
  buffer. Use it for one-shot events consumers may briefly miss
  (with `replay = 0` and careful design), or shared expensive streams.

```kotlin
private val _uiState = MutableStateFlow(HomeUiState())
val uiState: StateFlow<HomeUiState> = _uiState.asStateFlow()

fun onRetry() { _uiState.update { it.copy(isLoading = true) } }
```

Prefer modeling one-shot effects (snackbars, navigation) as state
the UI consumes and clears, rather than event streams — state
survives rotation and is easier to test. When you do need events,
a `SharedFlow` with explicit acknowledgement or a `Channel` consumed
as flow is the common pattern.

## Collecting in the UI

In Compose, collect with lifecycle awareness:

```kotlin
val state by viewModel.uiState.collectAsStateWithLifecycle()
```

In Views code, collect inside `repeatOnLifecycle(STARTED)` so
collection pauses when the UI is hidden. Collecting a flow in a bare
`lifecycleScope.launch` without lifecycle repetition wastes work and
can update hidden UI.

Convert a cold flow to state at the ViewModel boundary with
`stateIn`:

```kotlin
val items: StateFlow<List<Item>> = repo.observeItems()
    .stateIn(viewModelScope, SharingStarted.WhileSubscribed(5_000), emptyList())
```

`WhileSubscribed(5000)` keeps the upstream alive briefly across
configuration changes, avoiding restart churn on rotation.

## Testing Coroutines and Flow

Use `kotlinx-coroutines-test` (same 1.11.0 version) and Turbine for
flow assertions.

```kotlin
@Test
fun refreshUpdatesState() = runTest {
    val repo = FakeItemRepository()
    val vm = HomeViewModel(repo)
    vm.refresh()
    advanceUntilIdle()
    assertFalse(vm.uiState.value.isLoading)
}

@Test
fun emitsLoadingThenContent() = runTest {
    vm.uiState.test {
        assertEquals(HomeUiState(isLoading = true), awaitItem())
        // trigger load, then:
        assertTrue(awaitItem().items.isNotEmpty())
        cancelAndIgnoreRemainingEvents()
    }
}
```

- `runTest` runs suspend code on a test dispatcher with a virtual
  clock — delays are skipped, not waited through.
- Set `Dispatchers.setMain(StandardTestDispatcher())` when code uses
  `viewModelScope`, and reset it afterwards.
- Turbine's `test { awaitItem() }` is the readable way to assert a
  sequence of flow emissions.

## Common Mistakes

- Blocking the main thread with network, database, or file work.
- `GlobalScope.launch` for anything.
- Swallowing `CancellationException` in a broad `catch (Exception)` —
  always rethrow it so structured concurrency keeps working.
- Collecting the same cold flow in several places and duplicating
  its work; share it with `shareIn` or `stateIn`.
- Using `SharedFlow` for UI state (no guaranteed current value) or
  `StateFlow` for events that must never be conflated away.
- Mismatched coroutines artifact versions between main and test.


---

<!-- ===== docs/11-data-persistence-room-datastore-files.md ===== -->

# Data Persistence: Room, DataStore, and Files

Android offers three persistence tiers: a real database (Room) for
structured data, DataStore for settings and preferences, and files
for documents, media, and caches. Choosing the right tier per data
type keeps apps fast and migrations manageable.

Official references:
- Room: https://developer.android.com/training/data-storage/room
- Room migrations: https://developer.android.com/training/data-storage/room/migrating-db-versions
- DataStore: https://developer.android.com/topic/libraries/architecture/datastore
- App storage overview: https://developer.android.com/training/data-storage
- Scoped storage: https://developer.android.com/about/versions/11/privacy/storage

## Room — The Local Database

Room is a SQLite abstraction with compile-time checked queries,
coroutine and Flow support, and a migration system. Verified version
for the established line: **Room 2.8.5 stable**. There is also a new
major: **Room 3.0.3**, published under `androidx.room3`, rebuilt
KMP-first (Kotlin Multiplatform) with KSP-only processing.

**Room 2 and Room 3 are different majors.** Their artifacts,
packages, and setup differ. Name which major a project uses and
never mix the two in one project or copy setup code across majors.

### Room 2.x Shape (androidx.room)

```kotlin
@Entity(tableName = "items")
data class ItemEntity(
    @PrimaryKey val id: Long,
    val title: String,
    val updatedAt: Long
)

@Dao
interface ItemDao {
    @Query("SELECT * FROM items ORDER BY title")
    fun observeAll(): Flow<List<ItemEntity>>

    @Query("SELECT * FROM items WHERE id = :id")
    suspend fun getById(id: Long): ItemEntity?

    @Insert(onConflict = OnConflictStrategy.REPLACE)
    suspend fun upsertAll(items: List<ItemEntity>)

    @Query("DELETE FROM items")
    suspend fun clearAll()
}

@Database(entities = [ItemEntity::class], version = 1, exportSchema = true)
abstract class AppDatabase : RoomDatabase() {
    abstract fun itemDao(): ItemDao
}
```

Build with KSP (Room's compiler runs on KSP; kapt is legacy):

```kotlin
val db = Room.databaseBuilder(context, AppDatabase::class.java, "app.db")
    .build()
```

Guidelines:

- Observe with `Flow` queries — the UI updates automatically when
  tables change. This is what makes offline-first easy.
- Keep entities as storage models; map to domain models in the
  repository. Do not expose entities to the UI layer.
- All DAO suspend and Flow work is main-safe — Room runs queries
  off the main thread for you.

### Room 3.0.3 (androidx.room3)

Room 3 moves to the `androidx.room3` group, is KMP-first (common
code can define the database for Android and other targets), and is
KSP-only. Choose Room 3 for new KMP-oriented projects; choose the
2.8.5 line when following the widest body of existing Android-only
guidance and libraries. Either way, commit to one major per project
and use that major's documentation.

### Migrations

Every schema change bumps the database `version` and needs a
migration, or users lose data on upgrade.

```kotlin
val MIGRATION_1_2 = object : Migration(1, 2) {
    override fun migrate(db: SupportSQLiteDatabase) {
        db.execSQL("ALTER TABLE items ADD COLUMN priority INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0")
    }
}

Room.databaseBuilder(context, AppDatabase::class.java, "app.db")
    .addMigrations(MIGRATION_1_2)
    .build()
```

- Set `exportSchema = true` and commit schema JSON files — Room
  validates migrations against them.
- Write a migration test for every version pair you ship.
- `fallbackToDestructiveMigration` deletes user data. Acceptable
  only for a pure cache database, never for user content.
- Auto-migrations cover simple changes (added columns with
  defaults); manual migrations handle renames and data transforms.

## DataStore — Settings and Preferences

DataStore replaces SharedPreferences. It is asynchronous (coroutine
and Flow based), type-safe in its Proto form, and cannot block the
UI thread or lose writes the way SharedPreferences could.

Verified version: **DataStore 1.3.0**.

### Preferences DataStore

```kotlin
private val Context.dataStore by preferencesDataStore(name = "settings")

object SettingsKeys {
    val DARK_MODE = booleanPreferencesKey("dark_mode")
    val SORT_ORDER = stringPreferencesKey("sort_order")
}

class SettingsRepository(private val context: Context) {
    val darkMode: Flow<Boolean> = context.dataStore.data
        .map { prefs -> prefs[SettingsKeys.DARK_MODE] ?: false }

    suspend fun setDarkMode(enabled: Boolean) {
        context.dataStore.edit { prefs ->
            prefs[SettingsKeys.DARK_MODE] = enabled
        }
    }
}
```

### Choosing Between the Two DataStore Forms

- **Preferences DataStore** — key-value pairs, no schema. Right for
  settings, flags, and small user choices.
- **Proto DataStore** — a typed schema (protobuf) with migrations.
  Right when settings grow structured or are shared with a backend.

Do not store large or relational data in DataStore — that is Room's
job. Do not keep using SharedPreferences in new code; migrate reads
to DataStore once and keep the old file as the migration source
(DataStore ships a SharedPreferences migration helper).

## Files and Scoped Storage

Android uses scoped storage: your app freely accesses its own
private directories, and shared storage (photos, downloads) goes
through the MediaStore or the system file picker.

Private storage — no permissions needed:

- `context.filesDir` — internal files that persist (documents,
  downloaded models).
- `context.cacheDir` — internal cache; the system may clear it.
  Anything here must be re-creatable.
- External app-specific dirs (`getExternalFilesDir`) — larger files
  visible when the device is connected, still permission-free.

```kotlin
suspend fun saveReport(context: Context, text: String) =
    withContext(Dispatchers.IO) {
        File(context.filesDir, "report.txt").writeText(text)
    }
```

Shared storage rules:

- Use the Storage Access Framework picker (`ActivityResultContracts
  .OpenDocument` / `CreateDocument`) to let the user choose files —
  no broad storage permission needed.
- Use MediaStore to read and write shared photos, video, and audio.
- Broad permissions like `READ_EXTERNAL_STORAGE` are legacy; modern
  versions use per-media permissions and partial access instead.
  Request the narrowest access the feature needs.

## SQLDelight — A Note

SQLDelight is a JetBrains library that generates type-safe Kotlin
APIs from `.sq` SQL files. It is KMP-first and a credible choice
when a database is shared across Android and iOS/desktop targets.
For Android-only apps, Room remains the default recommendation —
larger community, official support, and Flow integration out of the
box. As with Room majors, pick one database stack per project.

## Choosing the Right Tier

| Data | Store |
|------|-------|
| Lists, records, anything queried or related | Room |
| Settings, flags, last-used choices | DataStore |
| Documents, exports, downloaded assets | Files (app-private) |
| Rebuildable downloads and thumbnails | Cache dir |
| Secrets and tokens | Encrypted storage — never plain files or DataStore |

## Common Mistakes

- Storing structured, growing data in DataStore or SharedPreferences.
- Destructive migrations on a database holding user content.
- Writing files on the main thread.
- Assuming cache files still exist — the system can delete them.
- Keeping two database stacks (Room and SQLDelight) in one app.
- Mixing Room 2 artifacts with Room 3 setup code copied from a
  different tutorial.


---

<!-- ===== docs/12-networking-retrofit-ktor-serialization.md ===== -->

# Networking: Retrofit, Ktor Client, and kotlinx.serialization

Most Android apps talk to a web API over HTTPS. This guide covers the two most common Kotlin stacks, how to share one model layer between them, and how to handle caching, errors, and secrets safely.

- **Retrofit 3.0.0 + OkHttp 5.5.1** — Android-only, the default choice for a single-platform app.
- **Ktor Client 3.x** — Kotlin Multiplatform (KMP), the choice when code is shared with iOS, desktop, or server targets.
- **kotlinx.serialization** — works with both, and is the only serializer that runs natively in KMP code.

## 1. Pick a stack

| Question | Retrofit | Ktor Client |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Platforms | Android / JVM only | Android, iOS, JVM, JS, Wasm |
| Engine | OkHttp, always | Pluggable: OkHttp, Darwin, CIO |
| API style | Annotated interfaces | DSL calls, typed extensions |
| Learning curve | Low | Medium |
| Best for | Android-only apps | Shared KMP modules |

Do not mix both in one app without a reason. Pick one, wrap it behind a small repository interface, and keep the rest of the app unaware of which library is underneath.

## 2. Dependencies

Versions used in this guide: Retrofit 3.0.0, OkHttp 5.5.1, Ktor Client 3.x, kotlinx.serialization 1.x.

```kotlin
// build.gradle.kts — Retrofit (Android app module)
dependencies {
    implementation("com.squareup.retrofit2:retrofit:3.0.0")
    implementation("com.squareup.okhttp3:okhttp:5.5.1")
    implementation("com.squareup.okhttp3:logging-interceptor:5.5.1")
    implementation("org.jetbrains.kotlinx:kotlinx-serialization-json:1.9.0")
    implementation("com.squareup.retrofit2:converter-kotlinx-serialization:3.0.0")
}
```

```kotlin
// build.gradle.kts — Ktor Client (KMP commonMain)
dependencies {
    implementation("io.ktor:ktor-client-core:3.3.0")
    implementation("io.ktor:ktor-client-content-negotiation:3.3.0")
    implementation("io.ktor:ktor-serialization-kotlinx-json:3.3.0")
    implementation("io.ktor:ktor-client-okhttp:3.3.0") // Android engine, in androidMain
    implementation("io.ktor:ktor-client-darwin:3.3.0") // iOS engine, in iosMain
}
```

Pin versions in a version catalog (`gradle/libs.versions.toml`) so every module agrees. See the version catalog guide in this series.
## 3. Models with kotlinx.serialization

One model definition serves both stacks and every platform.

```kotlin
import kotlinx.serialization.SerialName
import kotlinx.serialization.Serializable
@Serializable
data class ArticleDto(
    val id: Long, val title: String,
    @SerialName("published_at") val publishedAt: String,
    val summary: String? = null, // null when the server omits it
)
```

Rules that prevent most parsing bugs:

- Mark every network model `@Serializable`. Keep DTOs separate from UI models and map between them.
- Give nullable fields a default of `null` so missing keys do not crash parsing.
- Use `@SerialName` when the JSON name is not a valid or idiomatic Kotlin name.
- Configure `Json { ignoreUnknownKeys = true }` so a new server field does not break older app versions.
- Never parse JSON with string splitting or regular expressions.

Configure it once: `val json = Json { ignoreUnknownKeys = true; explicitNulls = false; coerceInputValues = true }`.

## 4. Retrofit: define the API as an interface

```kotlin
import retrofit2.http.GET
import retrofit2.http.Path
import retrofit2.http.Query
interface NewsApi {
    @GET("articles") suspend fun articles(@Query("page") page: Int): List<ArticleDto>
    @GET("articles/{id}") suspend fun article(@Path("id") id: Long): ArticleDto
}
```

```kotlin
import com.jakewharton.retrofit2.converter.kotlinx.serialization.asConverterFactory
import okhttp3.MediaType.Companion.toMediaType
import retrofit2.Retrofit

val retrofit = Retrofit.Builder()
    .baseUrl("https://api.example.com/v1/") // must end with /
    .client(okHttpClient)
    .addConverterFactory(json.asConverterFactory("application/json".toMediaType()))
    .build()
val api = retrofit.create(NewsApi::class.java)
```

Use `suspend` functions. Retrofit runs them on a background dispatcher and returns the result on the caller's dispatcher.

## 5. Ktor Client: one client, reused everywhere

```kotlin
import io.ktor.client.HttpClient
import io.ktor.client.call.body
import io.ktor.client.plugins.contentnegotiation.ContentNegotiation
import io.ktor.client.request.get
import io.ktor.client.request.parameter
import io.ktor.serialization.kotlinx.json.json
val client = HttpClient(OkHttp) { // engine differs per platform
    install(ContentNegotiation) { json(json) }
    expectSuccess = false // handle status codes yourself, see section 8
}

suspend fun articles(page: Int): List<ArticleDto> =
    client.get("https://api.example.com/v1/articles") {
        parameter("page", page)
    }.body()
```

Create the client once and inject it. Creating a client per request wastes connections and threads.

## 6. OkHttp interceptors

Interceptors see every request and response. Use them for headers, logging (debug builds only), and retry policy — not for business logic.

```kotlin
import okhttp3.Interceptor
import okhttp3.OkHttpClient
import okhttp3.logging.HttpLoggingInterceptor

val authInterceptor = Interceptor { chain ->
    val request = chain.request().newBuilder()
        .header("Accept", "application/json")
        // Token comes from secure storage at runtime, never source code.
        .header("Authorization", "Bearer ${tokenProvider.current()}")
        .build()
    chain.proceed(request)
}

val okHttpClient = OkHttpClient.Builder().addInterceptor(authInterceptor).apply {
    if (BuildConfig.DEBUG) addInterceptor(HttpLoggingInterceptor().apply {
        level = HttpLoggingInterceptor.Level.BASIC; redactHeader("Authorization")
    })
}.build()
```

Ktor has the same idea: use `defaultRequest { header(...) }` and the `Logging`, `Auth`, and `HttpRequestRetry` plugins.

## 7. Caching

Two layers, used together:

1. **HTTP cache** — OkHttp caches responses that carry cache headers. Give it a bounded disk cache, for example 10 MB.
2. **App cache** — store the last good response in Room or a file, and show it while a fresh request runs or when the device is offline.

```kotlin
import okhttp3.Cache
import java.io.File
val cache = Cache(File(context.cacheDir, "http_cache"), 10L * 1024 * 1024)
val cachedClient = okHttpClient.newBuilder().cache(cache).build()
```

Respect the server's `Cache-Control` headers. Do not cache responses that contain private user data on a shared cache key, and never cache `POST` results by hand unless the API defines it as safe.

## 8. Error modelling

Callers should get a small, typed result — not raw exceptions from three different layers.

```kotlin
sealed interface ApiResult<out T> {
    data class Success<T>(val data: T) : ApiResult<T>
    data class HttpError(val code: Int, val message: String?) : ApiResult<Nothing>
    data object Offline : ApiResult<Nothing>
    data class Unknown(val cause: Throwable) : ApiResult<Nothing>
}

suspend fun <T> safeCall(block: suspend () -> T): ApiResult<T> = try {
    ApiResult.Success(block())
} catch (e: IOException) { ApiResult.Offline
} catch (e: HttpException) { ApiResult.HttpError(e.code(), e.response()?.errorBody()?.string())
} catch (e: Exception) { ApiResult.Unknown(e) }
```

Guidance:

- Map 401 to a re-login flow, 404 to an empty state, 429 and 5xx to a retry with backoff, and other 4xx to a message the user can act on.
- Retry only idempotent requests (safe to repeat), such as `GET`.
- Show a plain-language message in the UI; log the technical detail.
- Test the error paths with a fake server or MockWebServer.

## 9. API keys and secrets: never in the repo

- **Never commit an API key** to source control, even in a private repo, even once. History keeps it forever.
- A key shipped inside an APK can be extracted. Treat client keys as public identifiers, not secrets. Real secrets belong on your server, with the app calling your server instead of the third party directly.
- For local builds, read keys from `local.properties` (git-ignored) or an environment variable, and pass them in via `buildConfigField`.
- For CI, store keys in the CI secret store, never in workflow files.
- Restrict keys in the provider console by package name and signing certificate fingerprint where the provider supports it.
- If a key leaks, rotate it first, then clean history.

Store the local key in git-ignored `local.properties` as `EXAMPLE_API_KEY=...`, never in a tracked file.

## 10. Checklist

- [ ] One HTTP stack, wrapped behind a repository interface.
- [ ] DTOs are `@Serializable`, nullable where the server is optional.
- [ ] `ignoreUnknownKeys = true` is set.
- [ ] One shared client instance, injected, not recreated per call.
- [ ] Logging is debug-only and redacts authorization headers.
- [ ] HTTP cache is bounded; offline shows the last good data.
- [ ] Errors are a typed result; retries are idempotent with backoff.
- [ ] No key, token, or password exists anywhere in the repo.

## Official links

- Retrofit: https://square.github.io/retrofit/
- OkHttp: https://square.github.io/okhttp/
- Ktor Client: https://ktor.io/docs/client-create-and-configure.html
- kotlinx.serialization: https://kotlinlang.org/docs/serialization.html
- Android networking overview: https://developer.android.com/training/basics/network-ops
- Network security config: https://developer.android.com/privacy-and-security/security-config


---

<!-- ===== docs/13-images-media-camera-and-playback.md ===== -->

# Images, Media, Camera, and Playback

This guide covers loading remote images, taking photos, letting the user pick media, and playing audio and video. Each job has one current recommended library. Using the current one matters: older libraries miss scoped-storage, Compose, and lifecycle fixes.

| Job | Use | Avoid for new work |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Images in Compose | Coil 3 | — |
| Images in View-heavy screens | Glide 5 | — |
| Camera | CameraX 1.6.2 | Direct Camera2 code |
| Audio / video playback | Media3 1.11.1 (ExoPlayer inside it) | Standalone `exoplayer2` |
| Picking photos / video | Android photo picker | Storage permission requests |

Picasso is deprecated in practice for new projects: it has no Compose support and its development has stalled. Do not start new work on it.

## 1. Images with Coil 3 (Compose first)

Coil 3 moved to Kotlin Multiplatform and a new group and package. The Compose artifact alone does not fetch from the network — the network artifact is a separate, required dependency.

```kotlin
// build.gradle.kts
dependencies {
    implementation("io.coil-kt.coil3:coil-compose:3.3.0")
    implementation("io.coil-kt.coil3:coil-network-okhttp:3.3.0")
    // Ktor instead of OkHttp in KMP code:
    // implementation("io.coil-kt.coil3:coil-network-ktor3:3.3.0")
}
```

```kotlin
import coil3.compose.AsyncImage

@Composable
fun ArticleImage(url: String, modifier: Modifier = Modifier) {
    AsyncImage(
        model = url,
        contentDescription = null, // decorative; describe real content
        modifier = modifier,
        contentScale = ContentScale.Crop,
    )
}
```

Practical rules:

- Always give the image a fixed size (modifier or layout) so the list does not jump while images load.
- Provide a placeholder and an error painter for slow networks.
- Ask the server for a sized image when it supports one; do not download a 4000-pixel photo into a 200-pixel thumbnail.
- Coil caches in memory and on disk by default. Leave the defaults unless profiling shows a problem.
- In KMP shared code the import is `coil3.*`, not `coil.*`.

## 2. Images with Glide 5 (View-heavy apps)

If most screens are still Views and RecyclerViews, Glide is the pragmatic choice. It is mature, handles RecyclerView recycling well, and integrates with lifecycle automatically.

```kotlin
// build.gradle.kts
dependencies {
    implementation("com.github.bumptech.glide:glide:5.0.5")
}
```

```kotlin
Glide.with(imageView)
    .load(url)
    .placeholder(R.drawable.placeholder)
    .error(R.drawable.image_error)
    .centerCrop()
    .into(imageView)
```

Do not use Glide and Coil for the same screens in one app. Pick per app, not per screen, so there is one cache and one set of defaults. For a Compose-first app, that pick is Coil 3.

## 3. Picking photos: use the photo picker

The Android photo picker lets the user choose specific photos or videos without any storage permission. It is the default choice for "attach a picture" flows.

```kotlin
val pickMedia = rememberLauncherForActivityResult(
    ActivityResultContracts.PickVisualMedia()
) { uri: Uri? ->
    if (uri != null) viewModel.onImagePicked(uri)
}

Button(onClick = {
    pickMedia.launch(
        PickVisualMediaRequest(ActivityResultContracts.PickVisualMedia.ImageOnly)
    )
}) { Text("Choose a photo") }
```

- Use `PickMultipleVisualMedia` when the user may select several items.
- The picker is available on older Android versions through Google Play services / AndroidX backports; check availability and fall back to `OpenDocument` if needed.
- A picked URI grants read access to that item only. If you need the access later, copy the file into app storage or take a persistable URI permission for document flows.
- Never request `READ_EXTERNAL_STORAGE` or broad media permissions just to let the user pick one photo. See the permissions guide.

## 4. Camera with CameraX 1.6.2

CameraX is a lifecycle-aware Jetpack library on top of Camera2. It handles device differences that make raw Camera2 code fragile.

```kotlin
// build.gradle.kts
dependencies {
    val camerax = "1.6.2"
    implementation("androidx.camera:camera-core:$camerax")
    implementation("androidx.camera:camera-camera2:$camerax")
    implementation("androidx.camera:camera-lifecycle:$camerax")
    implementation("androidx.camera:camera-view:$camerax")
    implementation("androidx.camera:camera-extensions:$camerax")
}
```

```kotlin
val cameraProvider = ProcessCameraProvider.getInstance(context).await()
val preview = Preview.Builder().build().also {
    it.surfaceProvider = previewView.surfaceProvider
}
val imageCapture = ImageCapture.Builder()
    .setCaptureMode(ImageCapture.CAPTURE_MODE_MINIMIZE_LATENCY)
    .build()

cameraProvider.unbindAll()
cameraProvider.bindToLifecycle(
    lifecycleOwner, CameraSelector.DEFAULT_BACK_CAMERA, preview, imageCapture
)
```

Guidance:

- Request the `CAMERA` runtime permission only when the user opens a camera feature, and explain why in plain words first.
- Save photos into app-specific storage, or into the shared gallery through `MediaStore` when the user expects them in the gallery.
- Test on a real device. Emulator cameras hide focus, rotation, and low-light problems.
- For scanning or face detection, add ML Kit as a CameraX analyzer instead of writing frame handling by hand.
- Release is automatic when bound to a lifecycle owner — bind to the right owner, not the application, for preview use cases.

## 5. Playback with Media3 1.11.1 and ExoPlayer

Media3 is the single Jetpack media library. ExoPlayer now lives inside Media3 as `media3-exoplayer`; the old standalone `com.google.android.exoplayer2` artifacts are deprecated and should not be used for new work.

```kotlin
// build.gradle.kts
dependencies {
    val media3 = "1.11.1"
    implementation("androidx.media3:media3-exoplayer:$media3")
    implementation("androidx.media3:media3-ui:$media3")
    implementation("androidx.media3:media3-session:$media3")
    // Optional: HLS / DASH streaming modules
    implementation("androidx.media3:media3-exoplayer-hls:$media3")
    implementation("androidx.media3:media3-exoplayer-dash:$media3")
}
```

```kotlin
val player = ExoPlayer.Builder(context).build()
player.setMediaItem(MediaItem.fromUri(videoUri))
player.prepare()
player.playWhenReady = true
```

Rules for reliable playback:

- Build the player in a lifecycle-aware place and call `player.release()` when done. Players hold native resources.
- For background audio, put the player in a `MediaSessionService` so system media controls, Bluetooth, and Android Auto work. That service is a foreground service — see the background work guide for its permission and notification duties.
- In Compose, host `PlayerView` through `AndroidView`, or use the Media3 Compose UI artifacts where they fit.
- Handle errors: network loss, unsupported format, and DRM failure each need a different user message and retry choice.
- Respect audio focus: pause or duck when another app takes focus, and resume only when it is polite to do so.
- Prefer adaptive streaming (HLS or DASH) for long video so quality adjusts to the connection instead of stalling.

## 6. Performance and privacy notes

- Downsample large camera photos before upload; send a sized copy, keep the original only if the user needs it.
- Strip location metadata (EXIF GPS) from photos before sharing them publicly, unless the user explicitly wants it kept.
- Cache media on Wi-Fi-friendly terms: do not auto-download large video on mobile data without a setting.
- Give every playable item a content description and captions where the source provides them. Accessibility is part of playback.

## 7. Checklist

- [ ] Coil 3 has both `coil-compose` and a network artifact.
- [ ] No new code uses Picasso or standalone `exoplayer2`.
- [ ] Photo selection uses the photo picker, not a permission.
- [ ] Camera is CameraX, bound to a lifecycle owner.
- [ ] Playback is Media3; background audio uses a media session.
- [ ] Players are released; images have fixed sizes and placeholders.
- [ ] EXIF location is stripped before public sharing.

## Official links

- Coil 3: https://coil-kt.github.io/coil/
- Glide: https://bumptech.github.io/glide/
- CameraX: https://developer.android.com/media/camera/camerax
- Media3: https://developer.android.com/media/media3
- ExoPlayer in Media3: https://developer.android.com/media/media3/exoplayer
- Photo picker: https://developer.android.com/training/data-storage/shared/photo-picker
- MediaStore: https://developer.android.com/training/data-storage/shared/media


---

<!-- ===== docs/14-background-work-and-foreground-services.md ===== -->

# Background Work and Foreground Services

Android limits what an app can do while it is not on screen, to protect battery and privacy. Almost all deferrable work belongs in WorkManager. Foreground services are a narrow exception for work the user can see and stop. This guide covers both, plus the older tools that still have a place.

Assumptions in this guide: WorkManager 2.12.0, `minSdk 24`, and apps targeting API 34, 35, and 36. Permission and timeout rules changed a lot in those releases, so the target version matters throughout.

## 1. Choose the right tool

| Need | Tool |
| --- | --- |
| Deferrable, must eventually run (sync, upload, cleanup) | WorkManager |
| User-started, must finish now even if app closes | WorkManager expedited / foreground service |
| Exact time matters (alarm clock, timer) | `AlarmManager` exact alarm, if eligible |
| System-scheduled job for a platform condition | JobScheduler (mostly via WorkManager) |
| Continuous user-visible work (music, navigation, call) | Foreground service with a declared type |

If the work can wait until conditions are good, it should. Work that "must run instantly in the background" usually does not, and the platform will throttle an app that insists on it.

## 2. WorkManager 2.12.0 basics

```kotlin
// build.gradle.kts
dependencies {
    implementation("androidx.work:work-runtime-ktx:2.12.0")
}
```

```kotlin
class UploadWorker(
    context: Context,
    params: WorkerParameters,
) : CoroutineWorker(context, params) {

    override suspend fun doWork(): Result {
        val id = inputData.getLong("article_id", -1)
        if (id < 0) return Result.failure()
        return try {
            repository.upload(id)
            Result.success()
        } catch (e: IOException) {
            if (runAttemptCount < 3) Result.retry() else Result.failure()
        }
    }
}

val request = OneTimeWorkRequestBuilder<UploadWorker>()
    .setInputData(workDataOf("article_id" to 42L))
    .setConstraints(
        Constraints.Builder()
            .setRequiredNetworkType(NetworkType.UNMETERED)
            .setRequiresCharging(false)
            .setRequiresBatteryNotLow(true)
            .build()
    )
    .setBackoffCriteria(BackoffPolicy.EXPONENTIAL, 30, TimeUnit.SECONDS)
    .build()

WorkManager.getInstance(context).enqueueUniqueWork(
    "upload-42", ExistingWorkPolicy.KEEP, request
)
```

Rules:

- Return `retry()` only for temporary failures (network, 5xx). Retrying a permanent failure (bad data, 4xx) wastes battery.
- Use unique work names for "the same job" so taps do not stack duplicate uploads.
- Keep `doWork()` idempotent: it may run again after a process death, so repeating it must be safe.
- WorkManager persists requests and survives reboots. Do not build your own persistence on top of it.

## 3. Constraints, expedited, and periodic work

**Constraints** describe when work may run: network type, charging, battery, storage, and device idle. Add every constraint the work truly needs, and no more — over-constrained work may wait for days.

**Expedited work** is for important, user-initiated tasks that should start quickly. It counts against a system quota (see section 6).

```kotlin
val expedited = OneTimeWorkRequestBuilder<UploadWorker>()
    .setExpedited(OutOfQuotaPolicy.RUN_AS_NON_EXPEDITED_WORK_REQUEST)
    .build()
```

Always set an out-of-quota fallback. Without one, the request fails when the quota is empty instead of running later.

**Periodic work** repeats at most once per interval, with a minimum interval of 15 minutes. It is not a timer: the system batches it.

```kotlin
val daily = PeriodicWorkRequestBuilder<SyncWorker>(24, TimeUnit.HOURS)
    .setConstraints(Constraints.Builder()
        .setRequiredNetworkType(NetworkType.CONNECTED).build())
    .build()
WorkManager.getInstance(context).enqueueUniquePeriodicWork(
    "daily-sync", ExistingPeriodicWorkPolicy.KEEP, daily
)
```

## 4. Foreground services: types and permissions

A foreground service (FGS) shows a persistent notification and is for work the user started and expects to continue: playback, navigation, a call, a file transfer they are watching.

Since target API 34, every foreground service must:

1. Declare a **type** in the manifest, for example `dataSync`, `mediaPlayback`, `location`, `connectedDevice`, `health`.
2. Request the matching type permission, for example `FOREGROUND_SERVICE_DATA_SYNC`.
3. Pass the same type when starting the service.
4. Request any runtime permission the type needs (for example location) before starting.

```xml
<uses-permission android:name="android.permission.FOREGROUND_SERVICE" />
<uses-permission android:name="android.permission.FOREGROUND_SERVICE_DATA_SYNC" />
<uses-permission android:name="android.permission.POST_NOTIFICATIONS" />

<service
    android:name=".sync.SyncService"
    android:foregroundServiceType="dataSync"
    android:exported="false" />
```

```kotlin
ServiceCompat.startForeground(
    this, 1001, notification,
    ServiceInfo.FOREGROUND_SERVICE_TYPE_DATA_SYNC,
)
```

Other target 34+ changes:

- An app in the background generally cannot start a foreground service. Start it while the app is visible, or from an allowed exemption such as a user interaction or a high-priority message.
- Google Play requires a declaration for each FGS type used, with a video showing the feature. Budget time for that review step.
- The notification must be shown promptly and must let the user understand, and where possible stop, the work.

## 5. Timeouts on target API 35

On target API 35, some FGS types gained system timeouts:

- `dataSync` — about 6 hours total per day across all such services; when the limit is reached the system stops the service.
- `mediaProcessing` — about 6 hours total per day; this type replaced `dataSync` for transcoding and similar media conversion work.

When a timeout approaches, the system calls `Service.onTimeout(startId, fgsType)` (API 35). Implement it: finish or checkpoint the work, call `stopSelf()`, and do not let the system kill the process mid-write.

Design for the limit instead of fighting it: move long sync and media conversion into WorkManager, which can resume, and keep the foreground service only for the part the user is watching.

## 6. Battery quotas and throttling

- **Expedited job quota:** the system gives each app a shared quota of expedited runtime, based on its standby bucket. Apps the user rarely opens get very little. Exhausted quota means delays.
- **Standby buckets:** active, working set, frequent, rare, and restricted. Restricted apps lose background privileges broadly.
- **Battery saver and Doze** defer network and jobs. Test with deviceidle commands rather than assuming timings.
- Batch network calls, prefer constraints over polling, and never hold a wake lock for work WorkManager could schedule.

## 7. JobScheduler and alarms

**JobScheduler** is the low-level system scheduler WorkManager is built on. Use it directly only for platform jobs WorkManager cannot express, such as a job tied to a specific system condition in a system or preloaded app. For normal apps, prefer WorkManager.

**Alarms** are for time, not for work scheduling:

- Inexact alarms (`setAndAllowWhileIdle` and friends) are the default and are batched by the system.
- Exact alarms need `SCHEDULE_EXACT_ALARM` or, for alarm-clock apps, `USE_EXACT_ALARM`. Since target API 31+, exact alarm access is denied by default; since API 33 the permission is not granted automatically and Play restricts it to apps whose core job is alarms, timers, or calendar reminders.
- If exact access is denied, offer the user an inexact fallback and explain the timing difference in plain words.
- Never use exact alarms as a heartbeat or sync timer.

## 8. Checklist

- [ ] Deferrable work is WorkManager, with sensible constraints.
- [ ] Workers are idempotent and retry only temporary failures.
- [ ] Expedited work sets an out-of-quota fallback policy.
- [ ] Periodic intervals are 15 minutes or longer.
- [ ] Every FGS declares a type, its permission, and a notification.
- [ ] `onTimeout` is handled for `dataSync` / `mediaProcessing`.
- [ ] Exact alarms are used only where the feature qualifies.
- [ ] No polling loops, no hand-held wake locks for deferred work.

## Official links

- WorkManager: https://developer.android.com/topic/libraries/architecture/workmanager
- WorkManager versions: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases/work
- Foreground services: https://developer.android.com/develop/background-work/services/fgs
- FGS types: https://developer.android.com/develop/background-work/services/fgs/types
- Background work overview: https://developer.android.com/develop/background-work
- Exact alarms: https://developer.android.com/training/alarms-and-reminders/exact-alarms
- Battery optimization: https://developer.android.com/training/monitoring-device-state/battery-monitoring

<!-- ===== docs/15-permissions-security-and-privacy.md ===== -->

# Permissions, Security, and Privacy

Users trust an app with their photos, location, contacts, and files.
Android's permission system, and Google Play's privacy rules, exist
to protect that trust. This guide covers asking for less, asking at
the right moment, protecting data at rest and in transit, and the
Play Console declarations that ship with the code.

## 1. Least privilege, first

- Request only the permissions the current feature needs.
- Prefer system features that need no permission: the photo picker
  for images, the share sheet for sending, app-specific storage for
  your own files, and the document picker for user-chosen files.
- Prefer approximate over precise location, and one-time over
  always, when the feature still works that way.
- Remove permissions the code no longer uses. Check the merged
  manifest — libraries can add permissions you did not ask for, and
  you can remove one with `tools:node="remove"` when it is truly
  unused.
- Never request a bundle of permissions at first launch "just in
  case". Apps that do this get denied, and reviewed badly.

## 2. Runtime permissions: ask at the point of use

Dangerous permissions are granted at runtime. The pattern is always:
explain, ask, handle both answers gracefully.

```kotlin
val requestPermission = rememberLauncherForActivityResult(
    ActivityResultContracts.RequestPermission()
) { granted: Boolean ->
    if (granted) viewModel.startCameraFeature()
    else viewModel.showCameraUnavailableMessage()
}

Button(onClick = {
    // Show a short in-app explanation first, then:
    requestPermission.launch(Manifest.permission.CAMERA)
}) { Text("Take a photo") }
```

Guidance:

- Show the rationale in your own UI before the system dialog when
  `shouldShowRequestPermissionRationale()` is true.
- If the user denies, keep the rest of the app working. Offer the
  feature again later, in context — do not nag on every launch.
- If the user picks "don't ask again", direct them to Settings only
  when they tap a clear "open Settings" action inside the feature.
- Test all three states: granted, denied once, and permanently
  denied. Each needs a different screen.
- Request permissions from an Activity or Fragment context, not from
  a Worker or a background service.

## 3. Partial media access and modern media permissions

On Android 14 (API 34) and later, when a user grants
`READ_MEDIA_IMAGES` or `READ_MEDIA_VIDEO`, they can choose
**selected photos only**. The app then sees only the items chosen,
plus anything it created.

- Handle partial access as a normal state, not an error. Show the
  selected items and a clear "select more" action that re-launches
  the picker or permission flow.
- Android may upgrade a partial grant to full access silently in
  some flows; re-check the actual granted set instead of assuming.
- Better still: if the user only ever picks a few images, use the
  photo picker and request no media permission at all.
- `READ_EXTERNAL_STORAGE` does nothing useful on modern targets.
  Do not request it in new code.

## 4. Notification permission

Since Android 13 (API 33), showing notifications needs the runtime
permission `POST_NOTIFICATIONS`.

- Ask after the user has seen value, for example after they enable a
  reminder or start a download they want told about — not at launch.
- The app must still work fully when notifications are denied.
  Foreground services need special care: the service still runs,
  but its notification may not be visible the usual way, so provide
  an in-app status surface as well.
- Create notification channels with honest importance levels. Do not
  label marketing messages as high importance.

## 5. Protecting data at rest

Use the Android Keystore for keys, and encrypt sensitive files and
preferences. Never write your own encryption.

```kotlin
// EncryptedFile with a Keystore-backed master key
val masterKey = MasterKey.Builder(context)
    .setKeyScheme(MasterKey.KeyScheme.AES256_GCM)
    .build()

val encryptedFile = EncryptedFile.Builder(
    context,
    File(context.filesDir, "private-notes.dat"),
    masterKey,
    EncryptedFile.FileEncryptionScheme.AES256_GCM_HKDF_4KB,
).build()

encryptedFile.openFileOutput().use { out ->
    out.write(secretBytes)
}
```

- Store tokens in encrypted storage, not plain `SharedPreferences`,
  plain files, or `BuildConfig`.
- Prefer `EncryptedSharedPreferences` replacements from Jetpack
  Security where maintained, or DataStore plus Keystore-backed
  encryption for new code — check the current Jetpack Security
  guidance before choosing, as its recommendations have changed.
- Keys in the Keystore can be set to require device unlock or
  biometric authentication for high-value operations.
- Wipe sensitive data on sign-out, not just the visible session.

## 6. Protecting data in transit

- HTTPS everywhere. Cleartext HTTP is blocked by default on modern
  targets; do not re-enable it globally.
- Use a **network security config** to state the policy in one file:

```xml
<!-- res/xml/network_security_config.xml -->
<network-security-config>
    <domain-config cleartextTrafficPermitted="false">
        <domain includeSubdomains="true">api.example.com</domain>
    </domain-config>
    <!-- Debug-only exception, in the debug source set only: -->
    <debug-overrides>
        <trust-anchors>
            <certificates src="user" />
        </trust-anchors>
    </debug-overrides>
</network-security-config>
```

- Certificate pinning helps high-risk apps but can lock users out
  when certificates rotate. Pin only with a backup pin and a
  rotation plan, or not at all.
- Validate server responses as untrusted input, even over HTTPS.

## 7. Obfuscation is not security

R8 shrinks and obfuscates release builds. That makes code harder to
read; it does not make secrets safe, logic unbreakable, or an app
tamper-proof. Anything inside the app bundle — keys, URLs, rules —
can be extracted by a determined reader.

- Keep real secrets and real authorization checks on a server.
- Use Play Integrity API where the risk justifies it, and treat its
  verdict as one signal, not proof.
- Still enable R8 for release: smaller size, and it raises the bar
  for casual copying. Just do not count it as a security control.

## 8. Privacy Sandbox, Data safety, and account deletion

Shipping includes declarations, not just code:

- **Data safety section (Play Console):** declare what data the app
  collects, shares, and why, matching what the code actually does —
  including data handled by third-party SDKs. Update it whenever a
  new SDK or feature changes the answer.
- **Privacy policy:** required when the app accesses sensitive data;
  link it in the Console and inside the app.
- **Account and data deletion:** Play requires apps with account
  creation to offer account deletion inside the app and through a
  web link, and to let users request data deletion. Build the flow
  before you need it; deleting an account must delete or anonymize
  the associated personal data on the server too.
- **Privacy Sandbox on Android:** advertising features are moving to
  privacy-preserving APIs (Topics, Protected Audience, Attribution
  Reporting) as third-party identifiers are restricted. If the app
  shows ads, follow the current Privacy Sandbox guidance instead of
  building on device identifiers.
- **Identifiers:** do not use the advertising ID or hardware IDs for
  analytics or tracking beyond their allowed purposes. Prefer
  app-scoped, resettable IDs you generate yourself.

## 9. Everyday security habits

- Keep dependencies updated; most real incidents are known bugs in
  old libraries, not clever attacks.
- Set `android:exported` explicitly on every component, and export
  nothing without a reason.
- Validate deep links and intent extras as untrusted input.
- Do not log tokens, personal data, or file contents — in release
  or debug logs that can leave the device.
- Back up rules: exclude tokens and sensitive files from Android
  backup and device transfer unless the user expects them moved.

## 10. Checklist

- [ ] Every permission is requested at point of use, with rationale.
- [ ] Partial media access is handled as a normal state.
- [ ] Notifications are requested after value, not at launch.
- [ ] Tokens and private files are encrypted with Keystore keys.
- [ ] Network security config forbids cleartext for real domains.
- [ ] No secret, key, or password exists in source or history.
- [ ] Data safety section matches the code and its SDKs.
- [ ] In-app and web account/data deletion flows exist.
- [ ] R8 is on for release, and not treated as a security control.

## Official links

- Request permissions: https://developer.android.com/training/permissions/requesting
- Partial media access: https://developer.android.com/training/data-storage/shared/media
- Notification permission: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/notifications/notification-permission
- Jetpack Security / Keystore: https://developer.android.com/privacy-and-security/keystore
- Network security config: https://developer.android.com/privacy-and-security/security-config
- Data safety section: https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/10787469
- Account deletion requirements: https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/13327111
- Privacy Sandbox on Android: https://developer.android.com/design-for-safety/privacy-sandbox
