<!-- ===== docs/18-accessibility-internationalization-and-theming.md ===== -->

# Accessibility, Internationalization, and Theming
## TL;DR
Build for everyone from the start: label every control for TalkBack, use large enough touch targets, respect the user's font size, keep all text in string resources, support right-to-left layouts, and theme with Material 3 — including dynamic color and a proper dark theme. Retrofitting any of these is much harder than starting right.
Official guides: Accessibility https://developer.android.com/guide/topics/ui/accessibility · Compose accessibility https://developer.android.com/jetpack/compose/accessibility · Material 3 https://developer.android.com/jetpack/compose/designsystems/material3
## 1. Accessibility principles
- Every action a sighted user can do must be possible with TalkBack, Switch Access, and a keyboard or D-pad where the device has one.
- Information must never be carried by color alone. Pair color with text, an icon, or a shape — an error also shows an icon and a message, not only a red outline.
- Keep contrast strong: at least 4.5:1 for normal text and 3:1 for large text and essential graphics, in both light and dark themes.
- Test with TalkBack on, large fonts on, and animations reduced. Automated checks catch labels and target size; only a manual pass catches reading order and confusing labels.
Accessibility Scanner and Compose lint help, but they do not replace testing with the real services. Scanner: https://developer.android.com/guide/topics/ui/accessibility/testing
## 2. TalkBack semantics in Views
In View-based UI, TalkBack reads the view hierarchy. Most standard widgets are already accessible; your job is labels and grouping.
```xml
<ImageView android:id="@+id/avatar" android:layout_width="48dp"
    android:layout_height="48dp" android:contentDescription="@string/avatar_description" />
<Button android:id="@+id/save" android:layout_width="wrap_content"
    android:layout_height="wrap_content" android:minHeight="48dp" android:text="@string/save" />
```
- Set `android:contentDescription` on image buttons and meaningful images. Set it to `null`, or mark the view not important for accessibility, for purely decorative images so TalkBack skips them.
- Group related views with a labelled container so TalkBack reads a row like "Ada Lovelace, premium member" once, not as fragments.
- Use headings (`android:accessibilityHeading="true"` on titles) so users can navigate by heading.
- For custom views, set `contentDescription` and class name, add custom actions with `AccessibilityDelegate`, and announce changes with `announceForAccessibility` only when truly needed.
## 3. TalkBack semantics in Compose
Compose builds a semantics tree from your composables. Material components (Button, IconButton, Switch, Slider) already carry the right role, state, and actions. You supply the labels.
```kotlin
@Composable fun SaveButton(onSave: () -> Unit) {
    Button(onClick = onSave) { Text(stringResource(R.string.save)) }
}
@Composable fun AvatarButton(onClick: () -> Unit) {
    IconButton(onClick = onClick) {
        Icon(imageVector = Icons.Default.Person,
            contentDescription = stringResource(R.string.open_profile))
    }
}
```
```kotlin
// Merge a row into one TalkBack stop, with a clear action label.
Row(modifier = Modifier
    .clearAndSetSemantics { contentDescription = "Ada Lovelace, premium member" }
    .clickable(onClickLabel = "Open profile") { openProfile() }
) { Text("Ada Lovelace"); Text("Premium member") }
```
Key APIs: `contentDescription` on `Icon` / `Image` (`null` means decorative); `Modifier.semantics { }` for state, role, custom actions, headings (`heading()`), progress (`progressBarRangeInfo`), and live regions; `clearAndSetSemantics` only when merging children into one label, since overuse hides controls from TalkBack. Toggle and selection state comes free with `Switch`, `Checkbox`, and `RadioButton` in a labelled row — make the whole row clickable.
Test with `composeRule.onNodeWithContentDescription(...)` and by walking the app with TalkBack in order, top to bottom. Semantics guide: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/compose/semantics
## 4. Content descriptions: writing good labels
- Describe what the control **does**, not what it looks like: "Delete note", not "Trash can icon".
- Do not repeat the role: TalkBack adds "button" itself, so use "Save", not "Save button".
- Keep labels short. Long help goes in a hint, a help screen, or a longer semantics description where needed.
- Label images that carry information ("Sales chart, up 12 percent this month"); hide decorative images from accessibility services.
- Update dynamic labels when content changes — a play button that becomes pause must change its description too.
## 5. Touch targets and layout
- Make every touch target at least **48 x 48 dp**, even when the visible icon is 24 dp. Material components do this by default; preserve it when you set custom sizes.
- Keep at least 8 dp between separate targets so they are not hit by mistake.
- Do not rely on hover, long-press alone, or precise gestures for an essential action. Offer a visible button alternative.
- Ensure focus order matches visual order for keyboard, D-pad, and TalkBack swipe navigation. In Compose, avoid custom focus order unless reading order would otherwise be wrong.
Guide: https://developer.android.com/guide/topics/ui/accessibility/principles
## 6. Font scaling and non-linear scaling
Users can set system font size well above 100%. Android scales `sp` text, and from Android 14 font scaling is **non-linear**: large text grows by a smaller factor than small text, so huge headlines do not explode while body text still gets bigger.
- Always size text in `sp`; Compose Material typography uses sp by default. Never size text in `dp`.
- Test at 85%, 100%, 130%, and 200% font scale. Text must wrap or the layout must scroll — never clip, overlap, or push buttons off screen.
- Give containers room to grow: prefer flexible heights over fixed heights for text blocks.
- Check dialogs, list rows, and bottom bars first, since they clip first.
```kotlin
Text(text = title, style = MaterialTheme.typography.titleMedium, // sp-based type
    modifier = Modifier.padding(16.dp)) // dp is for spacing, not text
```
## 7. Internationalization: strings and plurals
Internationalization (i18n) means the app can be translated without code changes. Localization is providing the actual translations.
- Put **every** user-visible string in `res/values/strings.xml`. No hard-coded text in Kotlin, layouts, or Compose — use `stringResource(R.string.…)` in Compose and `@string/…` in XML.
- Never build sentences by joining pieces, because word order differs by language. Use one full string with placeholders:
```xml
<string name="items_count">You have %1$d items</string>
<string name="welcome">Welcome back, %1$s</string>
<plurals name="notes_saved">
    <item quantity="one">%d note saved</item>
    <item quantity="other">%d notes saved</item>
</plurals>
```
```kotlin
Text(stringResource(R.string.items_count, count))
val saved = pluralStringResource(R.plurals.notes_saved, count, count)
```
- Use plurals so grammar is correct in every language; languages have different plural rules and some have six forms.
- Format dates, numbers, and currency with locale-aware formatters (`DateTimeFormatter`, `NumberFormat`), never hard-coded patterns like `MM/dd/yyyy` for display.
- Keep string names stable, add a short comment for translators describing where the string appears, and mark strings that must not be translated with `translatable="false"`.
Guides: https://developer.android.com/guide/topics/resources/localization · https://developer.android.com/guide/topics/resources/string-resource
## 8. RTL and layout direction
Languages like Arabic and Hebrew read right to left (RTL). Android mirrors layouts automatically when you use relative attributes.
- In Views use `paddingStart` / `paddingEnd` and `layout_marginStart` / `End`, never `Left` / `Right` for layout. In Compose use `padding(start = …, end = …)` and arrangements that respect `LayoutDirection`.
- Do not mirror icons that point in a physical or playback direction (play, volume, charts). Do mirror directional navigation icons like back/forward arrows — use `autoMirrored` icons in Compose.
- Test with the Arabic locale and with "Force RTL layout" in Developer Options, even before any translation exists, and declare `android:supportsRtl="true"` in the manifest.
Guide: https://developer.android.com/training/basics/supporting-devices/languages#SupportDifferentLayoutDirection
## 9. Per-app languages
Android 13+ lets users pick a language per app, different from the system language. Support it so users are not forced to change their whole device for one app.
- Store locale resources in `res/values-<language>` folders as usual.
- Use the AppCompat per-app language APIs / `LocaleManager` on Android 13+, and declare the locales config so the system Settings page lists your languages.
- Generate the locales config automatically with AGP where possible (`generateLocaleConfig = true`) or provide `res/xml/locales_config.xml`.
- Changing the in-app language should recreate the UI with the new resources and keep the user's place and data.
```xml
<locale-config xmlns:android="http://schemas.android.com/apk/res/android">
    <locale android:name="en-US" /><locale android:name="es" /><locale android:name="ar" />
</locale-config>
```
Guide: https://developer.android.com/guide/topics/resources/app-languages
## 10. Material 3 theming and dynamic color
Theme with Material 3 (`MaterialTheme`) from a single source of truth: color, typography, and shapes. Do not scatter raw hex colors through screens.
```kotlin
private val LightColors = lightColorScheme(primary = Color(0xFF6750A4), onPrimary = Color.White)
private val DarkColors = darkColorScheme(primary = Color(0xFFD0BCFF), onPrimary = Color(0xFF381E72))
@Composable fun AppTheme(dark: Boolean = isSystemInDarkTheme(), content: @Composable () -> Unit) {
    val context = LocalContext.current
    val colors = when {
        Build.VERSION.SDK_INT >= Build.VERSION_CODES.S ->
            if (dark) dynamicDarkColorScheme(context) else dynamicLightColorScheme(context)
        dark -> DarkColors
        else -> LightColors
    }
    MaterialTheme(colorScheme = colors, typography = Typography, content = content)
}
```
**Dynamic color** (Android 12+, API 31+) builds a color scheme from the user's wallpaper. Offer it as the default where it fits the brand, with your own light/dark schemes as fallback for older devices and for brands that must keep exact colors. Check that dynamic schemes still meet contrast rules for key screens.
Use semantic theme colors in UI — `primary`, `surface`, `surfaceVariant`, `error`, `onSurface` — not literal colors, so a theme change updates the whole app at once.
Links: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/compose/designsystems/material3 · https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/system/dynamic-color · https://m3.material.io/styles/color/system/overview
## 11. Dark theme
- Provide a real dark theme, not an inverted filter. Dark surfaces are dark greys from the color scheme, text is a lighter tone, and elevation is shown with a lighter surface tint in Material 3.
- Follow the system setting by default (`isSystemInDarkTheme()` in Compose, `AppCompatDelegate` / DayNight in Views), with an in-app Light / Dark / System choice saved as a preference.
- Check images, icons, charts, and screenshot tests in both themes. Avoid pure black for large areas unless the brand requires it, and avoid pure white text on pure black, which is harsh.
- Test dark theme together with large fonts and TalkBack — that combination is what many low-vision users actually run.
Guide: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/views/theming/darktheme
## Checklist
- [ ] Walked every main screen with TalkBack; order and labels make sense.
- [ ] All interactive images and icons have content descriptions.
- [ ] All touch targets are at least 48 x 48 dp.
- [ ] Layout tested at 200% font scale with no clipped text.
- [ ] No hard-coded user-visible strings; plurals and formats are locale-aware.
- [ ] RTL tested with a forced RTL layout and an RTL locale.
- [ ] Per-app languages listed in system Settings (Android 13+).
- [ ] Light, dark, and dynamic color checked for contrast on key screens.


---

<!-- ===== docs/19-build-signing-release-and-google-play.md ===== -->

# Build, Signing, Release, and Google Play
How to take an Android app from a debug build to a signed release on Google Play. Covers build types, variants, signing, the Android App Bundle, versioning, release tracks, and key Play Console policies. When a rule here and an official page disagree, the official page wins.
## Build types
A build type controls how an app is compiled for a purpose. Most projects start with two:
- `debug` — for development. Debuggable, signed with a debug key, optimizations off for fast builds.
- `release` — for users. Not debuggable, code/resources shrunk and optimized, signed with your release key.
```kotlin
android {
    buildTypes {
        getByName("debug") {
            applicationIdSuffix = ".debug"
            isDebuggable = true
        }
        getByName("release") {
            isMinifyEnabled = true
            isShrinkResources = true
            proguardFiles(getDefaultProguardFile("proguard-android-optimize.txt"), "proguard-rules.pro")
            signingConfig = signingConfigs.getByName("release")
        }
    }
}
```
`isMinifyEnabled` turns on R8, which removes unused code and obfuscates names. Always test the release build on a real device before uploading — R8 problems do not show up in debug builds.
See: https://developer.android.com/build/build-variants
## Product flavors and variants
A product flavor is an edition of the app, for example `free` and `paid`, or `demo` and `full`. Flavors plus build types produce **build variants**: `freeDebug`, `freeRelease`, `paidDebug`, `paidRelease`.
```kotlin
android {
    flavorDimensions += "tier"
    productFlavors {
        create("free") { dimension = "tier"; applicationIdSuffix = ".free" }
        create("paid") { dimension = "tier" }
    }
}
```
Give each flavor its own source folder when code differs, e.g. `src/free/kotlin/` and `src/paid/kotlin/`. Keep shared code in `src/main/`. Do not copy whole screens between flavors — share logic and vary only the part that differs.
See: https://developer.android.com/build/build-variants#product-flavors
## Version code and version name
Every upload to Google Play needs two version values:
- `versionCode` — a whole number that must **increase** with every upload. Play uses it to decide which build is newer. Users never see it.
- `versionName` — the text users see, such as `1.4.0`. It can follow semantic versioning: major.minor.patch.
```kotlin
android {
    defaultConfig {
        versionCode = 42
        versionName = "1.4.0"
    }
}
```
A common pattern is to generate `versionCode` in CI from the build number so it never repeats. Never reuse a version code, even for a different track — Play will reject the upload.
## App signing and Play App Signing
Android requires every app to be digitally signed. **Play App Signing** is required for new apps published as bundles:
- You hold an **upload key** and sign the bundle you upload.
- Google holds the final **app signing key** and signs the APKs delivered to devices.
- If you lose your upload key, Google can reset it after verifying your identity. Losing an old self-managed signing key meant you could never update the app again.
Enroll in Play Console under **Release > Setup > App signing**. Generate the upload key with `keytool`, store it outside the repo, and never commit the keystore or its passwords to version control. Use environment variables or a secrets store in CI.
```kotlin
// build.gradle.kts — read secrets from the environment, not source
android {
    signingConfigs {
        create("release") {
            storeFile = file(System.getenv("KEYSTORE_PATH") ?: "missing")
            storePassword = System.getenv("KEYSTORE_PASSWORD")
            keyAlias = System.getenv("KEY_ALIAS")
            keyPassword = System.getenv("KEY_PASSWORD")
        }
    }
}
```
See: https://developer.android.com/studio/publish/app-signing and https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/9842756
## Android App Bundle (AAB)
New apps must be published as an **Android App Bundle** (`.aab`), not a plain APK. A bundle contains all code and resources. Play uses it to generate split APKs so each device downloads only what it needs: its language, screen density, and CPU type. That makes downloads smaller.
Build one from Android Studio with **Build > Generate Signed App Bundle / APK**, or from the command line:
```bash
./gradlew :app:bundleRelease
# Output: app/build/outputs/bundle/release/app-release.aab
```
Use `bundletool` locally to test what Play will generate from the bundle before you upload.
See: https://developer.android.com/guide/app-bundle
## Target API requirement
Google Play raises the minimum target API level every year. Apps must target a recent Android version to be discoverable and updatable.
**Rule to plan for:** From **31 August 2026**, new apps and app updates must target **Android 16 (API level 36) or higher** to be submitted to Google Play, with limited extensions available in Play Console for apps that need more time. Existing apps targeting older levels may stop being available to devices running newer Android versions.
Check the current deadline before every release — the date and level move each year:
- https://developer.android.com/google/play/requirements/target-sdk
- https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/11926878
```kotlin
android {
    compileSdk = 36
    defaultConfig { targetSdk = 36; minSdk = 26 }
}
```
Raising `targetSdk` can change behavior (permissions, background work, edge-to-edge display), so test the upgrade — do not just change the number.
## Release tracks and testing tracks
Play Console tracks are separate channels for builds:
| Track | Who sees it | Use it for |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Internal testing | Up to 100 testers you list | Fast daily checks |
| Closed testing | Tester lists or Google Groups | Beta groups, QA |
| Open testing | Anyone who opts in on Play | Public beta |
| Production | All users in chosen countries | The real release |
New personal developer accounts must run a closed test with a minimum number of testers for a set period before applying for production access. Check the current requirement in Play Console.
Promote the **same bundle** from testing to production instead of rebuilding. A rebuild gets a new version code and has not been tested in the exact form users will receive.
## Staged rollout
A production release does not have to reach everyone at once. A **staged rollout** releases to a percentage of users first, for example 10%, then 50%, then 100% over several days.
- Watch crash and ANR (app not responding) rates in Android vitals while rolling out.
- You can halt a rollout at any time if a problem appears.
- Users who already got the new version keep it; everyone else stays on the previous version.
- Releasing a fix requires a new version code.
Start small for any release that touches startup, storage, payments, or background work.
See: https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/6346149
## Play Console policies overview
All apps must follow the Google Play Developer Program Policies. Areas that catch the most developers out:
- **User data and permissions** — request only permissions the feature needs; explain why in the app and in the privacy policy.
- **Deceptive behavior** — no misleading claims, fake system dialogs, or hidden functionality.
- **Malware and security** — no code downloaded after install that changes behavior outside Play's update mechanism.
- **Families** — apps aimed at children have stricter ad, data, and content rules (see the monetization guide in this set).
- **Health, financial, and news apps** — extra declarations and review apply.
Read the policy center before building a feature in a sensitive area, not after review rejects it: https://play.google.com/about/developer-content-policy/
Violations are handled with warnings, rejections, or suspensions depending on severity. Appeals are possible from Play Console.
## Data safety section
Every app must complete the **Data safety** section in Play Console (**Policy and programs > App content**). It is the label users see on the store listing, so it must match reality:
- Which data types you collect, share, and why.
- Whether data is encrypted in transit.
- Whether users can request deletion, and how.
- Data collected by third-party SDKs counts as your data.
"Collect" includes data sent off the device by any library in your app. Audit your SDK list before answering, and update the section whenever you add analytics, ads, or crash reporting.
See: https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/10787469
## Ratings, reviews, and store listing
- **Content rating** — complete the rating questionnaire in Play Console. Answers produce ratings for each region (IARC system). An inaccurate rating can get the app removed in that region.
- **Target audience** — declare who the app is for. Declaring children as an audience triggers the Families policies.
- **Store listing** — title, descriptions, screenshots, and feature graphic must not make claims the app cannot back up, and must not use misleading keywords or fake review quotes.
Respond to reviews in Play Console, and treat repeated reports of the same crash or confusion as a bug report, not noise.
## Pre-launch checklist
1. Release build tested on a real device, including upgrade from the previous production version.
2. `versionCode` increased; `targetSdk` meets the current rule.
3. Bundle built from the release variant and signed for upload.
4. Internal test track passed, then closed test if required.
5. Data safety, content rating, and target audience are current.
6. Privacy policy URL is live and matches the app's behavior.
7. Staged rollout percentage and halt criteria decided in advance.
8. Crash reporting checked in Android vitals after each stage.
## Summary
Build types separate debug from release, flavors create editions, and variants are the combination. Sign with an upload key and let Play App Signing hold the final key. Ship a bundle, keep the version code climbing, target API 36 or higher for submissions from 31 August 2026, test on the testing tracks, and roll out in stages while watching vitals. Policies, data safety, and ratings are part of the release, not paperwork after it.


---

<!-- ===== docs/20-monetization-billing-and-ads.md ===== -->

# Monetization, Play Billing, and Ads
How Android apps earn money the way Google Play requires: through the Play Billing Library for digital goods, with clear rules for subscriptions and one-time purchases, proper testing, and careful use of ads. When a deadline or policy here matters to a release, check the official page linked nearby — these dates move.
## The core rule: digital goods use Play billing
Apps distributed on Google Play must use **Google Play's billing system** for purchases of digital goods and services sold inside the app, including:
- Premium features and unlocked content
- In-game currency and items
- Subscriptions (media, fitness, productivity, dating, and more)
- Cloud storage, ad removal, and digital media
Physical goods and real-world services (ride hailing, food delivery, store merchandise, in-person services) do **not** use Play billing — they use a normal payment method.
Do not add your own card form, a web checkout link, or buttons that steer users to pay outside the app for digital goods. That is the most common billing policy violation.
Policy: https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/10281818 and https://play.google.com/about/monetization-ads/payments/
## Regional exceptions and alternative billing
The rule has real exceptions, and they change by country:
- In some regions, eligible apps may offer an **alternative billing system** alongside or instead of Play billing, under programs such as User Choice Billing or specific regulatory settlements (for example in South Korea, India, the EEA, and under recent US court-ordered changes).
- Each program has enrollment, reporting, and service-fee terms. Alternative billing is never just "add another payment provider and ship."
- Reader apps (books, music, news) have a separate set of rules for linking out to purchases on the web.
If you plan to use any exception, enroll in the exact program in Play Console and follow its declaration steps before release: https://developer.android.com/google/play/billing/alternative
## Play Billing Library version requirements
Google requires apps to use a recent Play Billing Library (PBL). Old versions stop being accepted for new apps and updates.
**Plan for these dates:**
- The **version 7 deadline has passed** — updates still on PBL 6 or older can no longer be submitted as before.
- **From November 2026**, new apps and app updates must use **Play Billing Library version 8 or higher**. Build the upgrade into your schedule well before November; do not wait for a Play Console rejection to start.
Always confirm the current requirement before a release: https://developer.android.com/google/play/billing/release-notes and https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/12154973
Add the library in `build.gradle.kts` (use the highest 8.x patch available when you build):
```kotlin
dependencies {
    implementation("com.android.billingclient:billing:8.0.0")
    implementation("com.android.billingclient:billing-ktx:8.0.0")
}
```
## What changed in Billing Library 8
Version 8 removed some old APIs. The one that breaks the most code:
- **`queryPurchaseHistory` is removed.** It was deprecated earlier and is gone in v8. Replace history queries with `queryPurchasesAsync`, which returns purchases the user currently owns, and keep your own record of past orders on your server if you need a history view.
- Product and purchase models were tightened — one-time products and subscriptions are modeled more explicitly, and several legacy helpers were removed in favor of the `ProductDetails` APIs introduced in v5 and later.
Migration guide: https://developer.android.com/google/play/billing/migrate-gpblv8
A typical query with current APIs:
```kotlin
val params = QueryProductDetailsParams.newBuilder()
    .setProductList(listOf(
        QueryProductDetailsParams.Product.newBuilder()
            .setProductId("premium_monthly")
            .setProductType(BillingClient.ProductType.SUBS)
            .build()
    )).build()
billingClient.queryProductDetailsAsync(params) { result, details ->
    if (result.responseCode == BillingClient.BillingResponseCode.OK) {
        // Show details.productDetailsList to the user
    }
}
```
## One-time products and subscriptions
**One-time products** are bought once: a level pack, an e-book, or a permanent unlock. Consumables (like coins) can be bought again after they are consumed; non-consumables (like "remove ads") stay owned forever.
- Acknowledge every purchase within 3 days or Play refunds it.
- Consume consumables only after granting the item, and grant only once per purchase token.
**Subscriptions** charge on a repeating cycle until cancelled:
- Offer a base plan, and optionally offers with free trials or introductory prices, all configured in Play Console.
- Handle the full lifecycle: new purchase, renewal, grace period (payment failed but access continues for a short time), account hold (access paused), cancellation (access until period end), and expiry.
- Users manage and cancel subscriptions in Google Play, not only in your app. Link to the Play subscriptions screen from your settings.
- Verify subscription state on your server with the Play Developer API for anything valuable — client state can be stale or tampered with.
Docs: https://developer.android.com/google/play/billing/subscriptions and https://developer.android.com/google/play/billing/lifecycle
Minimal purchase launch in Kotlin:
```kotlin
val flowParams = BillingFlowParams.newBuilder()
    .setProductDetailsParamsList(listOf(
        BillingFlowParams.ProductDetailsParams.newBuilder()
            .setProductDetails(details)
            // .setOfferToken(offerToken) // required for subscriptions with offers
            .build()
    )).build()
billingClient.launchBillingFlow(activity, flowParams)
```
Always listen in `PurchasesUpdatedListener`, verify the purchase, grant the entitlement, then acknowledge (and consume if needed).
## Testing with license testers
Never test real purchases with real money. Play gives you a full test setup:
1. In Play Console, add **license testers** under **Settings > License testing** (email addresses or Google Groups).
2. Upload the app to an internal or closed testing track. Billing test flows only work for builds installed from Play.
3. Testers see test payment methods ("Test card, always approves" / "always declines") and are never charged.
4. Test subscriptions renew on a shortened schedule (minutes, not months), so you can test renewal, grace period, and expiry in one sitting.
5. Use the billing testing docs to test error cases as well as success.
Also test: buying twice, restoring on a fresh install, cancelling and re-subscribing, and a purchase on a second device.
See: https://developer.android.com/google/play/billing/test and https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/6062777
## Server verification and security basics
- Verify purchase tokens on your server with the Google Play Developer API before granting valuable entitlements.
- Use Real-time Developer Notifications (RTDN) via Pub/Sub to learn about cancellations, holds, and renewals promptly.
- Never trust a price or entitlement read only on the device.
- Keep product IDs consistent between Play Console, the app, and your server. A typo here fails silently for users.
## Ads basics
Ads are the other common income source, usually through the Google Mobile Ads SDK (AdMob) or a mediation setup.
- Load ads off the main thread, preload sensibly, and never place ads where they cause accidental clicks (next to buttons, over content, or as surprise interstitials on back press).
- Interstitials (full-screen ads) need a clear close button and a natural break point — between levels or articles, not mid-task.
- Label paid and ad content honestly. Deceptive ad behavior is both an AdMob and a Play policy violation.
- Ads SDKs collect data — declare it in your Data safety section and privacy policy (see the release guide in this set).
Docs: https://developers.google.com/admob/android/quick-start and https://play.google.com/about/monetization-ads/ads/
## Consent with the User Messaging Platform (UMP)
In the EEA, UK, and other regulated regions you must gather consent before personalized ads and related data use. Google's **User Messaging Platform (UMP) SDK** is the standard way to do it inside AdMob apps:
```kotlin
val params = ConsentRequestParameters.Builder().build()
val consentInfo = UserMessagingPlatform.getConsentInformation(this)
consentInfo.requestConsentInfoUpdate(this, params,
    {
        UserMessagingPlatform.loadAndShowConsentFormIfRequired(this) { error ->
            if (consentInfo.canRequestAds()) startLoadingAds()
        }
    },
    { error -> /* Log and continue without personalized ads */ }
)
```
- Show the form when required, respect the choice, and provide a way to revisit it (a "Privacy options" entry) where required.
- Do not request ads before consent is resolved where the law requires consent first.
- Test with debug geography overrides, not by travelling.
See: https://developers.google.com/admob/android/privacy
## Families policy caution
If your app targets children, in whole or in part, the rules get much stricter — read them before adding any monetization:
- Only ad SDKs certified for the Families program may serve ads.
- No personalized ads for children; no interstitial tricks, no ads that look like content or gameplay.
- Purchases need parental gates; no pressure tactics aimed at children ("buy now or lose it").
- Mixed-audience apps must use neutral age screens and apply child rules whenever the user is a child.
Declaring the wrong target audience is a common cause of removal. See: https://play.google.com/about/families/ and https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer/answer/9893335
## Checklist
1. Digital goods use Play billing; no outside payment steering.
2. Billing Library is version 8 or higher; no `queryPurchaseHistory`.
3. Purchases verified, acknowledged in time, consumables consumed.
4. Subscription lifecycle states handled and tested with testers.
5. License testers configured; shortened renewals tested.
6. Ads placed safely, Data safety updated, UMP consent working.
7. Families rules checked if children are any part of the audience.
## Summary
Use Play billing for digital goods, stay on Billing Library 8 or higher, test with license testers instead of real money, and verify purchases on your server. Use ads carefully, gather consent with UMP where required, and treat Families rules as a hard constraint whenever children are in the audience.


---

<!-- ===== docs/21-form-factors-wear-tv-auto-foldables.md ===== -->

# Form Factors: Large Screens, Foldables, Wear, TV, Auto, and XR
Android runs on far more than phones: tablets, foldables, Chromebooks, watches, TVs, cars, and headsets. This guide covers how to build one app that adapts well to each shape, what changes with target API 36 for large screens, and which parts need a dedicated experience instead of a scaled-up phone screen. Start with adaptive layouts on phones and tablets. Add a form factor only when your users are actually there — each one adds design, testing, and review work.
## Large screens and the API 36 change
Large-screen devices have a smallest width of **600 dp or more** (`sw600dp`): tablets, foldable inner displays, Chromebooks, and phones in desktop windowing.
**What changed with target API 36:** On large screens, apps targeting Android 16 (API 36) can no longer opt out of resizing and orientation flexibility in the old way. Restrictions such as locked orientation (`android:screenOrientation`), fixed aspect ratio, and `android:resizeableActivity="false"` are **ignored** on `sw >= 600dp` displays. Your app will be resizable and may be shown in portrait or landscape regardless of those manifest values.
In practice this means:
- Do not design a tablet layout that only works in one orientation.
- Handle configuration changes and activity recreation correctly — save state; do not assume the activity lives forever.
- Test in split-screen, freeform windows, and both orientations.
- Letterboxing (black bars around a fixed-size app) is not a design strategy anymore on large screens.
See: https://developer.android.com/guide/practices/device-compatibility-mode-changes and https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/layouts/adaptive
Small phones keep the old behavior — the change is scoped to large screens only.
## Adaptive layouts with WindowSizeClass
Build one layout that adapts, not separate phone and tablet apps. Jetpack Compose Material 3 adaptive APIs classify the window by width (and height) instead of by device model:
- **Compact** — most phones in portrait (width < 600 dp)
- **Medium** — foldables, small tablets, phones in landscape (600–839 dp)
- **Expanded** — tablets, desktops (840 dp and up)
```kotlin
@Composable
fun HomeScreen(windowSizeClass: WindowSizeClass) {
    when (windowSizeClass.widthSizeClass) {
        WindowWidthSizeClass.Compact -> {
            ItemList(onItemClick = { /* navigate to detail */ })
        }
        WindowWidthSizeClass.Medium, WindowWidthSizeClass.Expanded -> {
            Row {
                ItemList(modifier = Modifier.weight(1f), onItemClick = {})
                ItemDetail(modifier = Modifier.weight(2f))
            }
        }
    }
}
```
Get the class at the activity level:
```kotlin
class MainActivity : ComponentActivity() {
    override fun onCreate(savedInstanceState: Bundle?) {
        super.onCreate(savedInstanceState)
        setContent {
            val windowSizeClass = calculateWindowSizeClass(this)
            AppTheme { HomeScreen(windowSizeClass) }
        }
    }
}
```
Prefer the Material 3 adaptive scaffolds for common patterns:
- `ListDetailPaneScaffold` — list + detail (email, settings)
- `SupportingPaneScaffold` — main content + side panel
- `NavigationSuiteScaffold` — switches between bottom bar, navigation rail, and drawer by window size
Docs: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/layouts/adaptive and https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases/compose-material3-adaptive
Layout rules that help everywhere:
- Cap text line length on wide screens; full-width paragraphs are hard to read.
- Use panes, rails, and side sheets instead of stretching cards.
- Keep touch targets at least 48 dp, with extra spacing on TV and in cars (see below).
## Foldables: postures and hinges
Foldables add states a normal phone does not have:
| Posture | Shape | Good use |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Flat | Fully open, one large screen | Two-pane layouts |
| Half-opened (tabletop) | Horizontal fold, like a laptop | Video on top, controls below |
| Half-opened (book) | Vertical fold | Reading, dual-page views |
| Closed | Cover screen only | Compact phone layout |
Use Jetpack WindowManager to observe the fold:
```kotlin
lifecycleScope.launch {
    WindowInfoTracker.getOrCreate(this@MainActivity)
        .windowLayoutInfo(this@MainActivity)
        .collect { layoutInfo ->
            val foldingFeature = layoutInfo.displayFeatures
                .filterIsInstance<FoldingFeature>().firstOrNull()
            // Use foldingFeature?.state, .orientation,
            // .occlusionType, and .bounds for the hinge
        }
}
```
Key points:
- Never place a button or key text under the hinge. Use the hinge bounds to split content into two areas.
- Tabletop posture is ideal for hands-free video, video calls, and timers — content on the top half, controls on the bottom.
- Test on a foldable emulator with posture overrides; hinge and posture bugs do not appear on a flat phone emulator.
See: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/layouts/foldables and https://developer.android.com/guide/topics/large-screens/learn-about-foldables
## Wear OS
Watches are glanceable, small, and used in seconds. Do not port a phone UI — design a separate, minimal Wear experience.
- **Compose for Wear OS** is the UI toolkit, with watch-specific components: `ScalingLazyColumn` for lists, curved text, and time text.
- **Tiles** are swipeable cards reached from the watch face — perfect for "next train", "today's steps", or a quick start button. Build them with the Tiles API (ProtoLayout).
- **Complications** put a small data point on the watch face itself (next event, heart rate, battery).
- Keep interactions to one or two taps. Use voice and haptics (vibration feedback).
- Sync data with the phone through the Data Layer API; the watch app should still show last-known data offline.
```kotlin
@Composable
fun WatchHome() {
    val listState = rememberScalingLazyListState()
    Scaffold(timeText = { TimeText() }) {
        ScalingLazyColumn(state = listState) {
            item { Text("Today") }
            items(3) { index -> Chip(label = { Text("Item $index") }, onClick = {}) }
        }
    }
}
```
Wear apps are reviewed against Wear OS quality guidelines, and heavy background work drains a tiny battery fast — keep work short and batched.
Docs: https://developer.android.com/training/wearables and https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/wear-os
## Android TV
TV is a 10-foot experience: big screen, D-pad remote, no touch.
- Build with **Compose for TV** (the TV Material APIs) or the older **Leanback** library for existing projects. New projects should start with Compose for TV.
- Every interactive element must be focusable, with a clear focus highlight. Test the whole app with only D-pad up/down/left/right, Select, and Back — if you need a pointer, it is broken.
- Use rows of cards for browsing, large text, and safe margins (overscan) around screen edges.
- Playback should support pause from the remote, next/previous, and resume where the user left off.
- TV apps go through extra Play review and need a TV banner and Leanback launcher intent to appear in the TV store.
```xml
<uses-feature android:name="android.software.leanback" android:required="false" />
<uses-feature android:name="android.hardware.touchscreen" android:required="false" />
```
```kotlin
LazyRow {
    items(movies) { movie ->
        Card(onClick = { open(movie) }) { Text(movie.title) }
    }
}
```
Docs: https://developer.android.com/training/tv and https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/tv
## Android Automotive and Android Auto
Two different things share the "car" name:
- **Android Auto** — the phone projects a driver-safe UI onto the car screen. You build templates (media, navigation, messaging, and point-of-interest categories such as parking, charging, and fuel) with the Car App Library. Templates are fixed by Google for safety; you supply content, not custom layouts.
- **Android Automotive OS (AAOS)** — Android runs natively in the car. Apps are closer to tablet apps but must follow driver distraction rules, use car-ready UI, and handle car hardware (multiple displays, rotary controls).
Rules for both: minimal text, large targets, voice first, no video or complex input while driving, and strict category and quality review on Play. Most apps should only consider Auto if they are media, navigation, or messaging apps.
Docs: https://developer.android.com/training/cars and https://developer.android.com/training/cars/apps
## XR note
Android XR extends Android to headsets and glasses, built on familiar Android and Jetpack Compose XR APIs with spatial panels, 3D content, and hand/eye input. It is a newer platform: treat it as an experimental extension of your adaptive work, not a starting point for a first app. Follow the platform docs as APIs stabilize, and do not assume phone interaction patterns (precise touch, small text) work in a headset.
See: https://developer.android.com/develop/xr
## Testing checklist
1. Phone: compact layout, portrait and landscape.
2. Tablet/foldable flat: expanded layout, both orientations, split-screen at several window sizes.
3. Foldable: tabletop and book postures, nothing under the hinge.
4. State survives rotation, folding, and window resizing.
5. Wear: glanceable in under 5 seconds, works offline.
6. TV: complete task with D-pad only, focus always visible.
7. Car: only if in an approved category, templates reviewed.
## Summary
Design adaptive first: WindowSizeClass on phones and tablets, correct resizing on large screens under target API 36, and hinge-aware layouts on foldables. Wear, TV, and cars each need their own focused experience — Compose for Wear OS and Tiles, Compose for TV with D-pad focus, and template-based Car App Library apps. XR is the emerging extension to watch.


---

<!-- ===== docs/22-kotlin-multiplatform-and-compose-multiplatform.md ===== -->

# Kotlin Multiplatform and Compose Multiplatform
Kotlin Multiplatform (KMP) lets you share Kotlin code between Android, iOS, desktop, and the web while still writing native apps on each platform. Compose Multiplatform extends Jetpack Compose so you can also share UI. This guide explains what is stable, what is still beta, the `expect`/`actual` mechanism, what is worth sharing, and the libraries that fit the model. The guiding idea: share the logic that is the same everywhere, keep the parts that should feel native on each platform native.
## Timeline and stability
Key milestones, so you can judge maturity for yourself:
- **Kotlin Multiplatform went stable in Kotlin 1.9.20** (November 2023). The core technology — Gradle model, shared source sets, and `expect`/`actual` — has been stable since then and is suitable for production use.
- **In May 2024, Google announced official support for Kotlin Multiplatform** for sharing business logic between Android and iOS, with tooling support in Android Studio and KMP support in selected Jetpack libraries (Room, DataStore, Collection, and others). Google's recommendation is to share business logic incrementally, not to rewrite the whole app.
- **Compose Multiplatform for iOS went stable in version 1.8.0** (May 2025). Android, iOS, and desktop are the stable Compose Multiplatform targets.
- **Compose Multiplatform for Web (Wasm)** is still in **beta** at the time of writing. It is usable for experiments and new projects that accept beta risk, but check the current status before committing a production web UI to it.
Status pages: https://kotlinlang.org/docs/multiplatform.html and https://www.jetbrains.com/compose-multiplatform/ and https://developer.android.com/kotlin/multiplatform
## How a KMP project is structured
Code lives in source sets:
```
src/
  commonMain/    — shared Kotlin, no platform APIs
  androidMain/   — Android actual implementations
  iosMain/       — iOS actual implementations
  desktopMain/   — JVM desktop (a "jvm" target)
  wasmJsMain/    — Web, when used
```
Gradle declares the targets, and the shared module is consumed by each native app — on Android as a library module, on iOS as a framework linked into the Xcode project.
```kotlin
// shared/build.gradle.kts
plugins {
    alias(libs.plugins.kotlinMultiplatform)
    alias(libs.plugins.androidLibrary)
}
kotlin {
    androidTarget()
    iosArm64()
    iosSimulatorArm64()
    jvm("desktop")
    sourceSets {
        commonMain.dependencies {
            implementation(libs.ktor.client.core)
            implementation(libs.sqldelight.runtime)
        }
        androidMain.dependencies { implementation(libs.ktor.client.okhttp) }
        iosMain.dependencies { implementation(libs.ktor.client.darwin) }
    }
}
```
Start a new project with the official wizard, which generates a working structure with current plugin versions: https://kmp.jetbrains.com/
## expect / actual
`commonMain` cannot call platform APIs directly — there is no Android `Context` on iOS and no `UIDevice` on Android. The bridge is:
- `expect` in common code declares what must exist.
- `actual` in each platform source set provides it.
```kotlin
// commonMain
expect fun platformName(): String
expect class UuidGenerator() { fun newId(): String }
```
```kotlin
// androidMain
actual fun platformName(): String = "Android ${Build.VERSION.SDK_INT}"
actual class UuidGenerator {
    actual fun newId(): String = java.util.UUID.randomUUID().toString()
}
```
```kotlin
// iosMain
actual fun platformName(): String =
    UIDevice.currentDevice.systemName() + " " + UIDevice.currentDevice.systemVersion
actual class UuidGenerator {
    actual fun newId(): String = NSUUID().UUIDString()
}
```
Keep `expect` declarations small and few. A large surface of platform hooks means the code was not really common. Prefer common interfaces with platform implementations injected (see dependency injection below) for anything complex.
Docs: https://kotlinlang.org/docs/multiplatform-expect-actual.html
## What to share, and what not to share
**Good to share — business logic:**
- Data models, validation, and business rules
- Networking (API clients, request/response mapping)
- Local database and caching logic
- Repository layer and use cases / interactors
- Session, sync, and offline-queue logic
- Pure calculations: pricing, scoring, and formatting rules you define yourself (not platform date/number formatting)
**Usually keep native:**
- UI, unless you deliberately choose Compose Multiplatform (see next section) — SwiftUI on iOS and Compose on Android give the most native feel and best platform integration.
- Navigation, notifications, widgets, and OS integrations
- Camera, Bluetooth, background execution, and permissions — the platform models differ too much to hide behind one API.
- Anything that wraps a platform SDK one-to-one; the wrapper costs as much as using the SDK directly.
Google's guidance matches this: adopt KMP **incrementally**, starting with a low-risk shared module such as networking or a single feature's logic, and expand only where it pays off.
See: https://developer.android.com/kotlin/multiplatform
## Compose Multiplatform
Compose Multiplatform reuses the Jetpack Compose programming model — composables, state, modifiers — across platforms:
- **Android** — it *is* Jetpack Compose; fully stable.
- **Desktop (Windows, macOS, Linux)** — stable, JVM-based. Good for tools and companion apps sharing an Android UI.
- **iOS** — stable since 1.8.0 (May 2025). Rendering feels native, with accessibility and platform interop, but test text input, gestures, and performance on real devices for a production app.
- **Web (Wasm)** — beta. Uses WebAssembly and Canvas rendering; browser support and tooling are still evolving.
```kotlin
// commonMain — the same composable runs on Android, iOS, desktop
@Composable
fun App() {
    MaterialTheme {
        var count by remember { mutableStateOf(0) }
        Column {
            Text("Count: $count on ${platformName()}")
            Button(onClick = { count++ }) { Text("Increment") }
        }
    }
}
```
Interop goes both ways: you can embed a Compose Multiplatform screen inside a SwiftUI app, and embed native views (maps, video players, platform pickers) inside Compose with `UIKitView` on iOS and `AndroidView` on Android.
A sensible strategy many teams use: share UI for internal tools, settings, and simple content screens; keep flagship screens native where platform polish matters most.
Docs: https://kotlinlang.org/docs/compose-multiplatform.html and https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/compose/multiplatform
## Libraries that fit KMP
Prefer libraries designed for multiplatform (common code with per-platform engines/adapters):
- **Ktor** — networking. One common client API, with a platform engine underneath: OkHttp on Android/JVM, Darwin on iOS. Also offers a KMP server, if you need one.
- **SQLDelight** — type-safe SQL. You write `.sq` files, it generates Kotlin APIs; drivers exist for Android (SQLite), iOS (native SQLite), JVM, and web. It fits KMP well. (Room also has KMP support in recent versions for teams already invested in it.)
- **Koin** — dependency injection written in Kotlin and built for KMP. Declare modules in common code, provide platform objects at startup on each target.
- **kotlinx.serialization** — JSON and other formats, common-first.
- **kotlinx.coroutines** — structured concurrency everywhere; remember the iOS main thread model for UI work.
- **kotlinx.datetime** — date and time for common code; do not use `java.time` in `commonMain`, it does not exist on iOS.
```kotlin
// Ktor in commonMain — engine supplied by the platform source set
val client = HttpClient { install(ContentNegotiation) { json() } }
suspend fun loadItems(): List<Item> =
    client.get("https://example.com/api/items").body()
```
```kotlin
// Koin: common module + a platform module at startup
val commonModule = module {
    single { ApiClient(get()) }
    single { ItemsRepository(get(), get()) }
}
```
Library directory: https://kotlinlang.org/docs/multiplatform-library.html
## Testing and build notes
- Put tests for shared logic in `commonTest` and run them on every target — a test that only passes on JVM may still fail on iOS (threading, number formatting, file paths differ).
- iOS builds require a Mac with Xcode. CI for iOS targets must run on macOS runners; Android and desktop can build anywhere.
- Binary size and startup on iOS are worth measuring early if you share a lot of code.
- Version the Kotlin, Compose Multiplatform, and library stack together in the version catalog — KMP libraries are sensitive to Kotlin version mismatches.
## Decision checklist
1. Is the shared code really the same logic on every platform? If not, KMP adds cost without the payoff.
2. Start with one module (networking or a feature's logic).
3. Keep `expect`/`actual` small; inject the rest with Koin.
4. Choose UI sharing deliberately: native UI + shared logic is the lowest-risk start; Compose Multiplatform is stable for Android, iOS, and desktop if you want shared UI too.
5. Treat Web/Wasm as beta and isolate it from the core app.
6. Test common code on all targets, including a real iOS device.
## Summary
KMP has been stable since Kotlin 1.9.20, Google supports it for shared business logic between Android and iOS, and Compose Multiplatform reached iOS stability in 1.8.0, with desktop and Android stable and Web/Wasm in beta. Share models, networking, storage, and rules with Ktor, SQLDelight, and Koin; keep OS integration native; and grow the shared surface only where the logic is genuinely identical.


---

<!-- ===== docs/23-games-overview-choosing-a-route.md ===== -->

# Games Overview — Choosing a Route

How to pick the right way to build an Android game in Kotlin.
There is no single best answer. The right choice depends on the
genre, how much custom rendering you need, your team size, and
whether the game must also ship on other platforms.

## TL;DR Decision Table

| Route | Best for | Language | Platforms |
|---|---|---|---|
| Compose Canvas | Simple 2D, puzzles, board games, casual | Kotlin only | Android only |
| libGDX + KTX | 2D and light 3D, cross-platform indie | Kotlin + KTX extensions | Android, desktop, iOS, web |
| KorGE | Pure Kotlin 2D, multiplatform from day one | Kotlin Multiplatform | Android, iOS, desktop, web |
| Godot via godot-jvm | Full engine, editor, 2D + 3D | Kotlin on JVM inside Godot | Android, desktop, consoles via Godot |
| Unity / Unreal | Large 3D, AAA-style, store assets | C# / C++ — Kotlin is glue only | All major platforms |
| Native C++ + AGDK | Maximum performance, custom engine | C/C++ core, Kotlin shell | Android focused |

If you are unsure, start with Compose Canvas for a prototype.
You can measure interest before committing to an engine.

## Route 1: Compose Canvas

Jetpack Compose can draw a game. `Canvas`, `DrawScope`, and
custom layouts give you full 2D drawing with normal Kotlin code.

Strengths:
- No engine to learn. If you know Compose, you know the basics.
- Normal Android lifecycle, navigation, and UI around the game.
- Easy to mix game screens with menus, settings, and stores.
- One codebase, one build system (Gradle).

Limits:
- No built-in physics, tile maps, particle system, or scene editor.
- Performance is fine for hundreds of sprites, not thousands.
- No cross-platform export.

Best genres: puzzles, word games, card games, board games,
turn-based strategy, simple arcade games, educational games.

See `24-games-2d-with-compose-canvas-and-custom-rendering.md`.

## Route 2: libGDX with KTX

libGDX is a mature, open-source, cross-platform game framework.
It is written in Java and has **no native Kotlin support built in**.
Kotlin support comes from KTX — a set of Kotlin extension libraries.

- Repository: https://github.com/libktx/ktx
- libGDX site: https://libgdx.com/
- KTX modules include `ktx-app`, `ktx-graphics`, `ktx-actors`,
  `ktx-scene2d`, `ktx-ashley` (entity-component), and more.

Strengths:
- Very mature, large community, many tutorials and examples.
- True cross-platform: one core module, thin platform launchers.
- Good 2D performance, capable 3D, Box2D physics via extensions.
- Free and open source (Apache 2.0).

Limits:
- You write more boilerplate than in a full editor engine.
- No visual scene editor comparable to Godot or Unity.
- Kotlin version support depends on the KTX release line.
  The KTX 1.13.1 line added support for Kotlin 2 — verify the
  current KTX and libGDX versions together before pinning.

Best genres: platformers, roguelikes, shooters, simulation,
any 2D game that must also run on desktop or iOS.

See `25-games-engines-libgdx-korge-godot.md`.

## Route 3: KorGE

KorGE is a pure Kotlin, multiplatform game engine.

- Site: https://korge.org/
- Repository: https://github.com/korlibs/korge

Strengths:
- Written in Kotlin, designed for Kotlin Multiplatform.
- Live reloading, integrated editor tooling, 2D focus.
- Targets Android, iOS, desktop, and web from shared code.

Limits:
- Smaller community than libGDX or Godot.
- API and versions have changed across major releases —
  always check the current version and migration notes on the
  official site before starting. Do not pin a KorGE version
  from memory.

Best genres: 2D multiplatform games where the team wants to
stay entirely in Kotlin and avoid Java or C#.

## Route 4: Godot via godot-jvm

Godot is a full open-source engine with a visual editor, scene
system, physics, animation, and export for many platforms.
The godot-jvm project lets you write game logic in Kotlin
(and Java) running on the JVM inside Godot.

- Godot: https://godotengine.org/
- godot-jvm: https://github.com/utopia-rise/godot-kotlin-jvm
  (published as utopia-rise / godot-jvm)

Strengths:
- Full editor, visual scene building, strong 2D and 3D.
- Kotlin for gameplay code instead of GDScript or C#.
- Godot handles rendering, physics, audio, and input.

Limits:
- Android export runs via ART; there are platform constraints
  covered in doc 25 (no web export, no 32-bit in that route).
- Larger runtime and build complexity than Compose Canvas.
- You must match Godot, godot-jvm, JDK, and Kotlin versions.

Best genres: 2D and 3D games that need an editor, complex
scenes, animation tooling, or a small team moving fast visually.

## Route 5: Unity or Unreal with Kotlin as a Plugin Layer

Be clear about this route: **you do not write a Unity or Unreal
game in Kotlin.** Unity uses C#. Unreal uses C++ and Blueprints.

Kotlin's role here is only on the Android side:

- A Kotlin Android library (AAR) wrapped as a Unity plugin.
- Android-specific features Unity does not expose cleanly:
  Play Games, billing edge cases, notifications, widgets.
- JNI bridges between the engine and Android APIs.

Choose Unity/Unreal when:
- The game is large 3D and needs their asset stores or tooling.
- The team already knows C# or C++.
- Console or high-end 3D is a requirement.

Do not choose this route to "use Kotlin for games" — the game
itself will not be Kotlin.

## Route 6: Native C++ with AGDK

The Android Game Development Kit (AGDK) is Google's set of
libraries for high-performance native games.

- Docs: https://developer.android.com/games/agdk

The game core is C or C++. Kotlin's role is the shell:

- Activity and lifecycle glue with GameActivity.
- Jetpack features, Play Games, billing, and UI overlays.
- Downloadable content, settings, and account screens.

Choose this when you have an existing C++ engine, need the
lowest latency, or need fine control over rendering and threads.

See `26-games-native-graphics-opengl-vulkan-agdk.md`.

## Genre / Complexity / Team Matrix

| Genre | Solo, simple | Small team | Larger / 3D heavy |
|---|---|---|---|
| Puzzle / board / cards | Compose Canvas | Compose Canvas | Compose or Godot |
| 2D platformer | KorGE / libGDX | libGDX + KTX | Godot |
| 2D shooter / roguelike | libGDX + KTX | libGDX + KTX | Godot / native |
| Simulation / strategy | Compose / libGDX | libGDX / Godot | Godot / Unity |
| 3D adventure | Godot | Godot | Unity / Unreal |
| Racing / FPS | Godot (light) | Unity | Unreal / native |
| Hyper-casual | Compose / KorGE | libGDX / Unity | Unity |
| Educational / kids | Compose Canvas | Compose / Godot | Godot |

## Questions to Answer Before You Commit

1. Android only, or must it ship elsewhere?
2. 2D or 3D? How many objects on screen at once?
3. Do you need a visual editor, or is code-only fine?
4. Does the team know Kotlin only, or also C# / C++?
5. Do you need physics, tile maps, particles, skeletal animation?
6. How important is small APK/AAB size?
7. Will you use Play Games, leaderboards, and cloud saves?
8. What is the update plan — content in code, or downloadable?

## Common Mistakes

- Picking a heavy engine for a puzzle game that Compose handles.
- Picking Compose Canvas for a fast action game with thousands
  of sprites and particles.
- Assuming libGDX "supports Kotlin" without adding KTX.
- Assuming Unity games can be written in Kotlin. They cannot.
- Ignoring Play Games on PC requirements until launch week.
  See doc 27 early if PC reach matters.

## Official Links

- Android Games overview: https://developer.android.com/games
- AGDK: https://developer.android.com/games/agdk
- Compose graphics: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/compose/graphics
- libGDX: https://libgdx.com/
- KTX: https://github.com/libktx/ktx
- KorGE: https://korge.org/
- Godot: https://godotengine.org/
- Godot JVM: https://github.com/utopia-rise/godot-kotlin-jvm

Next: doc 24 for building 2D games with Compose Canvas, or
doc 25 for engine setup details.

<!-- ===== docs/24-games-2d-with-compose-canvas-and-custom-rendering.md ===== -->

# Games 2D with Compose Canvas and Custom Rendering

How to build a real 2D game using only Jetpack Compose: Canvas
drawing, a frame-driven game loop, sprite handling, shaders,
sound, haptics, and gesture input. This route suits puzzles,
board games, card games, and simple arcade games.

Docs: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/compose/graphics

## When Compose Canvas Is Enough

Compose is enough when:
- Sprites on screen number in the tens or low hundreds.
- Movement is smooth but not physics-heavy.
- The game mixes normal UI (menus, dialogs) with the play field.
- You ship Android only.

Move to an engine (doc 25) when you need tile-map editors,
complex physics, particles at scale, or cross-platform export.

## Canvas and DrawScope Basics

`Canvas` gives you a `DrawScope`. Inside it you draw with
coordinates in pixels, with the origin at the top-left.

```kotlin
@Composable
fun PlayField(state: GameState) {
    Canvas(Modifier.fillMaxSize()) {
        drawRect(Color(0xFF101418), size = size)
        for (ball in state.balls) {
            drawCircle(
                color = ball.color,
                radius = ball.radius,
                center = Offset(ball.x, ball.y)
            )
        }
        drawLine(
            color = Color.White,
            start = Offset(0f, size.height / 2),
            end = Offset(size.width, size.height / 2),
            strokeWidth = 2f
        )
    }
}
```

Key `DrawScope` tools:
- `drawRect`, `drawCircle`, `drawOval`, `drawLine`, `drawPath`
- `drawImage` for bitmaps and sprites
- `withTransform { translate(...); rotate(...) }` for transforms
- `clipRect` / `clipPath` to limit drawing to a region
- `drawContext.canvas` for direct Android `Canvas` calls when
  you need a native API Compose does not wrap yet

Keep drawing code pure: read state, draw, return. Never mutate
game state inside the draw block.

## The Game Loop with withFrameNanos

Do not use a `while (true) { delay(16) }` loop. Use the frame
clock so updates sync with the display refresh.

```kotlin
@Composable
fun GameLoop(onTick: (deltaSeconds: Float) -> Unit) {
    var lastNanos by remember { mutableLongStateOf(0L) }
    LaunchedEffect(Unit) {
        while (true) {
            withFrameNanos { frameNanos ->
                if (lastNanos != 0L) {
                    val dt = (frameNanos - lastNanos) / 1_000_000_000f
                    onTick(dt.coerceAtMost(0.05f)) // clamp tab-switch spikes
                }
                lastNanos = frameNanos
            }
        }
    }
}
```

Rules for a stable loop:
- Clamp delta time so a pause does not teleport objects.
- Update physics in fixed steps if collisions matter:
  accumulate real time, step at 1/120 or 1/60, render the rest.
- Pause the loop when the screen is not visible. Tie the
  `LaunchedEffect` to a `playing` flag or the lifecycle.

## Holding Game State

Two good patterns:

1. Snapshot state for small games — a single state holder whose
   fields Compose observes.

```kotlin
class GameState {
    var playerX by mutableFloatStateOf(0f)
    var playerY by mutableFloatStateOf(0f)
    var score by mutableIntStateOf(0)
    val enemies = mutableStateListOf<Enemy>()
}
```

2. `StateFlow` for larger games — an engine class owns the
   state and the UI collects it.

```kotlin
data class Frame(val player: Offset, val score: Int, val enemies: List<Enemy>)

class GameEngine(viewModelScope: CoroutineScope) {
    private val _frame = MutableStateFlow(Frame(Offset.Zero, 0, emptyList()))
    val frame: StateFlow<Frame> = _frame.asStateFlow()
    fun step(dt: Float) { _frame.value = computeNext(_frame.value, dt) }
}
```

## Sprites and Images

Load images once, outside the draw loop.

```kotlin
val sprite = ImageBitmap.imageResource(R.drawable.ship)

Canvas(Modifier.fillMaxSize()) {
    drawImage(
        image = sprite,
        dstOffset = IntOffset(playerX.toInt(), playerY.toInt()),
        dstSize = IntSize(96, 96)
    )
}
```

Sprite sheets: draw a sub-rectangle with `srcOffset` and
`srcSize` on `drawImage`. Precompute frame rects; do not
allocate them per frame.

## Paths and Shapes with graphics-shapes


```kotlin
// Conceptual shape usage — check current graphics-shapes API
val star = RoundedPolygon(numVertices = 5, radius = 80f, centerX = 100f, centerY = 100f)
// Convert to a Path via toPath() and drawPath(...) inside Canvas.
```

## AGSL RuntimeShader (API 33+)

Android Graphics Shading Language (AGSL) lets you write small
shader programs that run on the GPU. In Compose, use
`RuntimeShader` and apply it as a brush or render effect.

```kotlin
@RequiresApi(33)
val shader = RuntimeShader(
    """
    uniform float2 iResolution;
    uniform float iTime;
    half4 main(float2 fragCoord) {
        float2 uv = fragCoord / iResolution;
        return half4(uv.x, uv.y, 0.5 + 0.5 * sin(iTime), 1.0);
    }
    """.trimIndent()
)

// Inside DrawScope or a Modifier.graphicsLayer renderEffect,
// set uniforms per frame: shader.setFloatUniform("iTime", t)
```

Uses: water, glow, distortion, transitions, animated
backgrounds. Guard with an API 33 check and provide a plain
fallback brush on older devices.

Docs: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/views/graphics/agsl

## Sound: SoundPool and Media3

- `SoundPool` — short, low-latency effects (taps, hits, coins).
  Load a small set of sounds at start, play by sound id.
- Media3 / ExoPlayer — music and longer audio, streaming, and
  playback with a media session.

```kotlin
val soundPool = SoundPool.Builder().setMaxStreams(8).build()
val hitId = soundPool.load(context, R.raw.hit, 1)
// Later:
soundPool.play(hitId, 1f, 1f, 1, 0, 1f)
```

Release `SoundPool` when the screen closes. Respect audio
focus: pause music when another app takes focus. See doc 28.

## Haptics and Input Gestures

Haptics: use `LocalHapticFeedback` in Compose.

```kotlin
val haptics = LocalHapticFeedback.current
// On a meaningful event:
haptics.performHapticFeedback(HapticFeedbackType.LongPress)
```

Gestures: Compose gesture modifiers cover most game input.

```kotlin
Modifier
    .pointerInput(Unit) {
        detectTapGestures(onTap = { pos -> onTap(pos) })
    }
    .pointerInput(Unit) {
        detectDragGestures(
            onDrag = { change, drag -> onDrag(drag) }
        )
    }
```

- `detectTapGestures` — taps, double taps, long press.
- `detectDragGestures` — dragging pieces or panning.
- `detectTransformGestures` — pinch zoom and rotation.
- For fast action games, handle raw `PointerEvent` changes in
  a custom `pointerInput` and read all pointers for multi-touch.

## Performance Tips

- Keep allocations out of the frame loop and draw block.
- Reuse `Path`, `Paint`-equivalents, and rect objects.
- Draw only what is visible; skip off-screen sprites.
- Use `Modifier.graphicsLayer` for cheap transforms of whole
  layers instead of redrawing them.
- Test at 60 Hz and 90/120 Hz. High refresh is now common.
- Watch slow frames in Android Vitals (see doc 28).

## Official Links

- Compose graphics: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/compose/graphics
- Canvas and drawing: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/compose/graphics/draw/overview
- AGSL: https://developer.android.com/develop/ui/views/graphics/agsl
- Graphics shapes: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases/graphics
- Gestures: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/compose/touch-input
- SoundPool: https://developer.android.com/reference/android/media/SoundPool
- Media3: https://developer.android.com/media/media3

<!-- ===== docs/25-games-engines-libgdx-korge-godot.md ===== -->

# Games Engines — libGDX, KorGE, and Godot

Engine routes for Kotlin game development beyond Compose Canvas:
libGDX with the KTX Kotlin extensions, the pure-Kotlin KorGE
engine, and Godot driven from Kotlin via godot-jvm. Read doc 23
first for the decision guide.

## libGDX — Java Framework, Kotlin via KTX

libGDX: https://libgdx.com/ — a mature cross-platform framework
for 2D and 3D games, written in Java, Apache 2.0 licensed.

Important fact: **libGDX itself has no native Kotlin support.**
Kotlin support comes from a separate project, KTX.

### KTX — The Kotlin Extensions for libGDX

- Repository: https://github.com/libktx/ktx
- KTX provides idiomatic Kotlin wrappers and extensions:
  - `ktx-app` — application lifecycle helpers
  - `ktx-graphics` — rendering, cameras, shapes
  - `ktx-actors` and `ktx-scene2d` — scene2d UI in Kotlin style
  - `ktx-ashley` — Ashley entity-component system helpers
  - `ktx-box2d` — Box2D physics helpers
  - `ktx-collections`, `ktx-math`, `ktx-async`, and others

```kotlin
// KTX style application skeleton
class MyGame : KtxGame<Screen>() {
    override fun create() {
        addScreen(PlayScreen())
        setScreen<PlayScreen>()
    }
}
```

Version note: the KTX 1.13.1 release line added support for
Kotlin 2. libGDX and KTX versions must be compatible with each
other and with your Kotlin version. Always check the KTX
releases page and the libGDX version together before pinning —
do not copy a version triple from an old tutorial.

### Project Shape

A typical libGDX + KTX project:
- `core` — shared game code (Kotlin, depends on libGDX + KTX)
- `android` — Android launcher, depends on `core`
- `desktop` — desktop launcher for fast iteration (optional)
- `ios` / `html` — additional launchers if needed

Setup tool: the official libGDX setup generates this layout.
Generate in Java mode if needed, then convert modules to
Kotlin and add the KTX dependencies for your modules only.

### What libGDX Gives You

- OpenGL ES rendering, sprite batching, texture atlases
- Scene2D UI, Box2D physics (via extension), particle effects
- Asset manager, audio (OpenAL on desktop, Android APIs on mobile)
- Large tutorial and example ecosystem built over a decade+

### Limits

- No visual editor comparable to Godot or Unity.
- UI and scene layout are code-driven.
- You own the game loop, screens, and asset pipeline.

## KorGE — Pure Kotlin Multiplatform

KorGE: https://korge.org/ — https://github.com/korlibs/korge

KorGE is a game engine written in Kotlin, for Kotlin
Multiplatform. One codebase targets Android, iOS, desktop
(JVM), and web (JS/Wasm).

Highlights:
- Kotlin-first APIs: scenes, views, tweens, tile maps, sprites
- Live reloading during development
- Gradle-based build integrated with Kotlin Multiplatform
- Part of the Korlibs ecosystem (Korio, Korim, Korau, Korge)

```kotlin
// KorGE scene sketch (API shape — verify against current docs)
suspend fun main() = Korge(windowSize = SizeInt(800, 600)) {
    val scene = sceneRoot
    // Build views, add sprites, run animations here.
}
```

**Version check warning:** KorGE has shipped major changes
across releases and its versioning has moved quickly. Before
starting a project, open https://korge.org/ and the GitHub
releases page, read the current getting-started guide, and pin
exactly the version that guide uses. Do not trust a KorGE
version number or API snippet from memory or an old blog post.

Choose KorGE when staying 100% in Kotlin Multiplatform matters
more than having the largest community.

## Godot with Kotlin — godot-jvm

Godot: https://godotengine.org/ — a full open-source engine
with editor, scene tree, physics, animation, and exporters.

Kotlin support comes from godot-jvm:
- Repository: https://github.com/utopia-rise/godot-kotlin-jvm
- Published under the `utopia-rise` organisation as godot-jvm.

Facts to plan around (verify on the project page before a
release, as requirements move with Godot versions):
- The project describes its 1.0 line as production-ready.
- It targets Godot 4.x — recent work tracks Godot 4.7.2+.
- JDK 17 is the required Java level for the toolchain.
- The default Kotlin version in recent releases is in the
  Kotlin 2.3.x line (2.3.20 in the latest documented setup).
- On Android, code runs via ART (the Android runtime).
- There is **no web export** for the JVM route, and
  **no 32-bit** Android support in that route. If you need web
  export or 32-bit devices, this route does not cover them.

```kotlin
// Godot-Kotlin node sketch — class registered with the engine
@RegisterClass
class Player : CharacterBody2D() {
    @RegisterProperty
    var speed: Float = 200f

    @RegisterFunction
    override fun _physicsProcess(delta: Double) {
        // Read input, move the body with Godot physics APIs.
    }
}
```

Workflow:
1. Write Kotlin classes annotated for registration.
2. The godot-jvm Gradle plugin generates the bindings Godot needs.
3. Build and attach scenes in the Godot editor as usual.
4. Export to Android from Godot with the JVM runtime included.

Choose Godot when you want the editor and engine features and
prefer Kotlin over GDScript for gameplay logic.

## What About Cocos?

Cocos (Cocos Creator) is sometimes asked about in Kotlin
discussions. Be clear: **Cocos is a TypeScript / JavaScript
and C++ engine. It is not a Kotlin engine.** There is no
supported route to write a Cocos game in Kotlin. If a plan
requires Kotlin, choose libGDX, KorGE, or Godot instead.

## Side-by-Side Comparison

| | libGDX + KTX | KorGE | Godot + godot-jvm |
|---|---|---|---|
| Language | Kotlin via KTX ext. | Pure Kotlin | Kotlin on JVM |
| Editor | No (code only) | Light tooling | Full visual editor |
| 2D | Excellent | Excellent | Excellent |
| 3D | Capable | Limited focus | Strong |
| Physics | Box2D extension | Built-in basics | Built-in engine |
| Platforms | Android/desktop/iOS/web | Android/iOS/desktop/web | Android/desktop + Godot targets* |
| Community | Large, mature | Smaller, Kotlin-focused | Large (Godot overall) |
| Learning curve | Medium | Medium | Medium (editor + Kotlin) |

*Godot JVM route excludes web and 32-bit Android — see above.

## Choosing Between Them

- Already know libGDX or need its ecosystem? libGDX + KTX.
- Want one Kotlin Multiplatform codebase, web included? KorGE
  (after the version check) or libGDX HTML launcher.
- Want a visual editor and scene workflow? Godot + godot-jvm.
- Need web export from Godot? Use GDScript or C# in Godot —
  the JVM route does not export to web.

## Version Discipline for Engines

Engines move independently of AndroidX. For every engine:
1. Open the official releases page on the day you set up.
2. Record engine + Kotlin + JDK versions together in the repo.
3. Upgrade engine and Kotlin as a pair, in a dedicated change,
   with a device test pass after.
4. Never mix a tutorial's old Gradle snippets with a new engine.

## Official Links

- libGDX: https://libgdx.com/
- KTX: https://github.com/libktx/ktx
- libGDX wiki: https://libgdx.com/wiki/
- KorGE: https://korge.org/
- KorGE GitHub: https://github.com/korlibs/korge
- Godot: https://godotengine.org/
- Godot JVM: https://github.com/utopia-rise/godot-kotlin-jvm
- Godot Kotlin docs: https://godot-kotlin.com/

Next: doc 26 covers native graphics and AGDK for teams that
outgrow these engines or bring their own C++ engine.
