<!-- ===== docs/26-games-native-graphics-opengl-vulkan-agdk.md ===== -->

# Games Native Graphics — OpenGL, Vulkan, and AGDK

Native graphics on Android for game teams with a C/C++ engine
or maximum-performance needs. Kotlin does not draw the game in
this route — it provides the Android shell and Jetpack glue
around a native core.

Docs hub: https://developer.android.com/games/agdk

## Vulkan Is the Primary Graphics API

Google's current guidance for new native games:

- **Vulkan is the primary low-level graphics API on Android.**
  Prefer Vulkan for new native rendering work.
- **OpenGL ES is supported but not actively developed.** Existing
  OpenGL ES engines keep working; new features land in Vulkan.
- Vulkan 1.1 is available on 64-bit devices running Android 10
  and above. Check device capabilities at runtime — do not
  assume a Vulkan version from the OS version alone.
- **ANGLE in Android 15:** Android 15 routes OpenGL ES through
  ANGLE (a translation layer over Vulkan) on many devices.
  This keeps OpenGL ES apps working while the platform moves
  to Vulkan underneath. Test OpenGL ES games on Android 15+
  devices because behaviour now flows through that layer.

Practical rule: new engine work targets Vulkan; maintenance
work on an existing OpenGL ES engine continues, with testing
on current Android versions.

## The Android Game Development Kit (AGDK)

AGDK is Google's collection of libraries and tools for native
games. The parts that matter most:

### GameActivity — Use It Instead of NativeActivity

GameActivity is the AGDK replacement for the old
`NativeActivity`. It is a Jetpack-style component you extend,
with better input handling, lifecycle behaviour, and
integration with modern Android features.

- Artifact family: `androidx.games:games-activity`
- Recent stable line: games-activity 4.4.2 — verify the current
  version on the AndroidX games release page before pinning.
- Docs: https://developer.android.com/games/agdk/game-activity

```cpp
// Native entry point shape with GameActivity (C++)
#include <game-activity/native_app_glue/android_native_app_glue.h>

void android_main(android_app* app) {
    // Create your engine, run the render loop,
    // handle APP_CMD_INIT_WINDOW / APP_CMD_TERM_WINDOW.
}
```

```kotlin
// Kotlin/Java side: declare the activity in the manifest and
// load the native library.
class MainActivity : GameActivity() {
    companion object {
        init { System.loadLibrary("game") }
    }
}
```

GameActivity handles text input, game controller input, and
surface lifecycle in ways NativeActivity did not. New native
games should start here, not with NativeActivity.

### Frame Pacing — Swappy

The Frame Pacing library (Swappy) helps a native game render
at a smooth, consistent rate matched to the display, reducing
jitter and input latency.

- Artifact: `androidx.games:games-frame-pacing`
- Recent stable line: games-frame-pacing 2.1.3 — verify before pinning.
- Integrate Swappy's swap call in place of a raw `eglSwapBuffers`
  or Vulkan queue present, and report the display refresh period.

### Performance Tuner

Android Performance Tuner (part of Play Games tooling with
AGDK) records frame time and fidelity data from real players
and surfaces it in Play Console, so you can see which devices
struggle at which quality settings.

Use it to:
- Define fidelity levels (low / medium / high / ultra).
- Annotate scenes (menu, gameplay, loading) for breakdowns.
- Find device groups that need a lower default quality.

### Other AGDK Pieces

- **Game Text Input** — on-screen keyboard input for native games.
- **Game Controller library** — consistent gamepad handling.
- **Memory advice / performance tooling** — watch memory pressure
  before the system kills the game.
- **Android Game Development Extension (AGDE)** — Visual Studio
  tooling for building and debugging Android native games.

## Kotlin's Role in a Native Game

Kotlin is the shell, not the renderer:

| Responsibility | Where it lives |
|---|---|
| Rendering, physics, game logic | C/C++ core |
| Activity, lifecycle, surface | GameActivity + thin Kotlin class |
| Play Games sign-in, achievements | Kotlin (PGS v2 SDK is Kotlin/Java) |
| Billing (Play Billing Library) | Kotlin |
| Menus, settings, account UI | Kotlin + Compose |
| Notifications, widgets, deep links | Kotlin |
| Crash reporting glue | Kotlin + native crash handling |

Communication between layers:
- JNI for direct calls (keep the boundary narrow and coarse).
- A shared save/config format both sides can read.
- Engine events posted to the Kotlin side for UI updates.

Keep JNI calls infrequent and batched. Per-frame JNI chatter
for every sprite will cost more than it saves.

## Rendering Loop Rules (Native)

- Render on a dedicated thread; never block the UI thread.
- Handle window creation and destruction — the surface can
  disappear (backgrounding, rotation, foldables) at any time.
- Query the actual refresh rate and adapt pacing; 90/120 Hz
  displays are common (see Frame Rate API in doc 28).
- Recreate GPU resources after context/device loss.
- Cap work when the device reports thermal pressure (doc 28).

## When to Go Native — and When Not To

Go native when:
- You already have a C/C++ engine or codebase.
- You need every millisecond of latency or thread control.
- You are porting a desktop/console engine to Android.

Stay managed (Compose, libGDX, KorGE, Godot) when:
- The team is Kotlin-only.
- The game is 2D or moderate 3D.
- Development speed matters more than peak performance.

Native code multiplies build, debug, and crash-analysis cost.
Pay it only when the game truly needs it.

## Build and Packaging Notes

- Build native code with the NDK and CMake via the Android
  Gradle Plugin's `externalNativeBuild` block.
- Ship only the ABIs you support. 64-bit (`arm64-v8a`, and
  `x86_64` for ChromeOS / Play Games on PC) is the baseline;
  Play requires 64-bit for new submissions.
- Use AAB delivery: Play splits native libraries per ABI,
  keeping each user's download small.
- Symbolicate native crashes: keep unstripped symbols per
  release and upload them to Play Console / your crash tool.

## Official Links

- AGDK overview: https://developer.android.com/games/agdk
- GameActivity: https://developer.android.com/games/agdk/game-activity
- Frame Pacing: https://developer.android.com/games/sdk/frame-pacing
- Performance Tuner: https://developer.android.com/games/sdk/performance-tuner
- Vulkan on Android: https://developer.android.com/games/graphics/vulkan
- OpenGL ES on Android: https://developer.android.com/games/graphics/opengl
- NDK: https://developer.android.com/ndk
- AndroidX games releases: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases/games

Next: doc 27 covers Play Games services, and doc 28 covers
performance, input, audio, and saves in depth.

<!-- ===== docs/27-games-services-play-games-and-play-games-on-pc.md ===== -->

# Games Services — Play Games and Play Games on PC

Google Play Games Services (PGS) gives an Android game player
identity, achievements, leaderboards, cloud saves, and reach
onto Windows PCs through Google Play Games on PC. This doc
covers the current v2 SDK only, plus PC requirements.

Docs: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames

## Use PGS v2 Only — v1 Is Removed

This is the most common integration mistake, so it comes first:

- **Use the v2 SDK: `com.google.android.gms:play-services-games-v2`.**
- The v1 SDK (`play-services-games`) has been removed as a
  supported path. **Do not treat play-services-games version
  25 or higher as a "v1 upgrade"** — the old v1 APIs are not
  carried forward that way. Migrate to v2 instead.
- New projects start on v2. Existing v1 projects must migrate:
  sign-in flow, client access, and several APIs changed shape.

Check the current v2 artifact version on the Play Games SDK
release notes before pinning; never copy a version from an old
tutorial.

## Setup Checklist

1. Create the game in Play Console and enable Play Games Services.
2. Add the Play Games app ID to the manifest as meta-data.
3. Add the v2 dependency (see the rule above).
4. Initialise the SDK once, early in app start.
5. Test with a licensed tester account listed in Play Console.

```kotlin
// build.gradle.kts — use the current v2 version from release notes
dependencies {
    implementation("com.google.android.gms:play-services-games-v2:<current>")
}
```

```xml
<!-- AndroidManifest.xml -->
<meta-data
    android:name="com.google.android.gms.games.APP_ID"
    android:value="@string/game_services_project_id" />
```

## Initialisation and Automatic Sign-In

```kotlin
class MyApplication : Application() {
    override fun onCreate() {
        super.onCreate()
        PlayGamesSdk.initialize(this)
    }
}
```

In your main activity, check sign-in state through the games
sign-in client. v2 signs the player in automatically when
possible — there is no mandatory interactive sign-in screen.

```kotlin
val signInClient = PlayGames.getGamesSignInClient(this)
signInClient.isAuthenticated()
    .addOnCompleteListener { task ->
        val authenticated = task.isSuccessful &&
            task.result?.isAuthenticated == true
        if (authenticated) loadPlayerProfile()
        else showSignedOutUi() // game must still be playable
    }
```

Design rule: the game must remain playable signed out.
Achievements and leaderboards enhance the game; they must not
gate basic play.

## Player Identity

```kotlin
PlayGames.getPlayersClient(this).currentPlayer
    .addOnSuccessListener { player ->
        val id = player.playerId
        val name = player.displayName
    }
```

Use the Play Games player ID as the identity key for your own
backend, not the player's real name or email. Respect the
player's profile visibility choices.

## Achievements

- Define achievements in Play Console (hidden, incremental,
  and standard types).
- Unlock and increment from gameplay events, not from UI taps.

```kotlin
val client = PlayGames.getAchievementsClient(this)
client.unlock(achievementId)
client.increment(achievementId, steps)
client.showAchievementsIntent() // standard achievements UI
```

Cache progress locally so offline play still counts, then
submit when the player is authenticated again.

## Leaderboards

```kotlin
val client = PlayGames.getLeaderboardsClient(this)
client.submitScore(leaderboardId, score)
client.getLeaderboardIntent(leaderboardId) // standard UI
```

Submit scores only from verified gameplay results. If your
game has a server, validate scores server-side before trusting
them for prizes or rankings that matter.

## Snapshots — Saved Games

Snapshots are Play Games cloud saves tied to the player.

- One or more named snapshots per player (for example one per
  save slot, plus one for settings/progress metadata).
- Each snapshot holds your save bytes plus metadata: played
  time, progress value, and a cover image if you want one.
- Conflicts happen when two devices save offline. Resolve by
  policy: newest played time, highest progress, or ask the
  player. Never silently discard the longer play history.

See doc 28 for the full local + cloud save design.

## Recall API

The Recall API links your own game accounts to the player's
Play Games profile using recall tokens stored by Google. It
helps a returning player recover their in-game account on a
new device without another password. Use it when your game has
its own account system in addition to Play Games identity.

Server-side only: recall tokens are exchanged from your
backend, never purely from the client. Follow the official
Recall guide for the token flow.

## Play Games on PC

Google Play Games on PC runs Android games on Windows. Games
that meet the requirements earn visibility badges:

- **Playable** — the game runs and is playable on PC.
- **Optimized** — the game meets the higher bar for PC play.

Core requirements and expectations (check the current official
checklist before submitting — details evolve):

- **Frame rate: at least 30 FPS, stable**, on target PC hardware.
- **x86-64 support is strongly encouraged.** PC runs on x86-64;
  shipping the `x86_64` ABI dramatically improves compatibility
  and performance versus translation.
- **Mouse and keyboard must work well.** Menus, gameplay, and
  text input cannot require touch. Map keys sensibly and show
  PC-appropriate control hints.
- **Disable unsupported features on PC.** If a feature needs
  GPS, telephony, a rear camera, or other phone-only hardware,
  hide or replace it in the PC build — do not leave a broken button.
- Window resizing, focus loss, and PC display sizes must be
  handled gracefully.
- The game must meet standard Play Games on PC certification:
  correct lifecycle, no phone-only permission demands, and a
  working sign-out / account experience.

Practical approach: detect the PC form factor at runtime
(Play provides signals for the PC environment), then switch
input hints, default quality, and feature flags accordingly.

## Testing

- Test sign-in with tester accounts, not your developer account.
- Test signed-out, offline, and account-switch cases.
- Test achievement and leaderboard flows in the sandbox before
  publishing the Play Games configuration — publishing is a
  one-way door for some settings.
- Test the PC build on Play Games on PC itself if PC reach is
  a goal; an Android emulator does not prove PC behaviour.

## Common Mistakes

- Adding `play-services-games` (v1) to a new project.
- Blocking game start on sign-in success.
- Using the player's display name as a database key.
- Submitting leaderboard scores from the client for rewarded
  competitions with no server validation.
- Shipping an ARM-only build and expecting good PC performance.
- Leaving touch-only tutorials in the PC build.

## Official Links

- Play Games Services: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames
- PGS v2 migration: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames/unity/unity-start-signin (Android native guidance linked from the PGS hub)
- Android SDK setup: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames/android/android-start
- Achievements: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames/services/common/concepts/achievements
- Leaderboards: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames/services/common/concepts/leaderboards
- Saved games (Snapshots): https://developer.android.com/games/playgames/services/common/concepts/saved-games
- Recall API: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames/recall
- Play Games on PC: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames/pc
- PC requirements checklist: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames/pc/requirements

<!-- ===== docs/28-games-performance-input-audio-and-saves.md ===== -->

# Games Performance, Input, Audio, and Saves

The cross-cutting runtime concerns every Android game shares:
staying smooth, respecting thermals, handling controllers and
touch well, playing audio correctly, and never losing a save.

## Performance Targets and Android Vitals

Google Play measures game quality through Android Vitals.
The headline game metric is **slow sessions**:

- A session is "slow" when too many of its frames miss the
  target frame time. The slow-session threshold to watch is
  **more than 25% slow frames** in a session — crossing quality
  thresholds hurts discoverability on Play.
- Watch vitals per device group. A game that is smooth on a
  flagship and slow on a mid-range phone will show it there.

Set a frame budget and defend it:
- 60 FPS = 16.6 ms per frame total (CPU + GPU).
- 120 FPS = 8.3 ms. Only promise it if you can hold it.

## ADPF — Adaptive Performance APIs

The Android Dynamic Performance Framework (ADPF) is the set of
APIs for adapting to device conditions in real time.

### Thermal API

Read thermal status and react before throttling hits.

```kotlin
val powerManager = getSystemService(PowerManager::class.java)
powerManager.addThermalStatusListener(executor) { status ->
    when (status) {
        PowerManager.THERMAL_STATUS_SEVERE,
        PowerManager.THERMAL_STATUS_CRITICAL -> reduceQuality()
        else -> { /* hold current quality */ }
    }
}
```

Quality reductions that work: lower render resolution, fewer
particles, reduced draw distance, lower physics rate. Reduce
gradually and do not oscillate.

`getThermalHeadroom` (with a forecast window) lets you plan
ahead instead of only reacting. Guard by API level.

### Performance Hint API

`PerformanceHintManager` lets rendering and loading threads
tell the system how long work should take, so CPU frequencies
are scheduled efficiently instead of guessing.

- Create a session for your render thread group.
- Report the target duration per frame, then the actual
  duration after each frame.
- Most valuable on a native render loop; managed games benefit
  mainly through engine or ADPF integrations.

### Game Mode API

Players and OEMs can set a Game Mode (performance, battery
saver, or standard). Your game can:
- Read the current mode and adjust defaults.
- Offer Game Mode interventions you support (for example a
  resolution scaling intervention) via the Game Dashboard.

### Game State API

Tell the system what the game is doing — loading, gameplay,
cutscene, idle — with `GameState`. The system uses it for
power and performance decisions. Set it at real transitions,
not per frame.

Docs: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames/game-mode
and the ADPF guide at https://developer.android.com/games/adpf

## Frame Rate API and Refresh Rates

Displays run at 60, 90, 120 Hz and beyond, and can switch.
Two tools:

1. Preferred display mode / refresh rate hints for the window.
2. The Frame Rate API — `Surface.setFrameRate` (API 30+) and
   its AndroidX and View/Compose equivalents — tells the system
   the frame rate your content actually wants.

```kotlin
// View surface example (API 30+)
surfaceView.holder.surface.setFrameRate(
    60f,
    Surface.FRAME_RATE_COMPATIBILITY_DEFAULT
)
```

Rules:
- Request the rate you can actually sustain. Asking for 120
  while rendering at 40 wastes power and looks worse.
- Menus and static screens should request a low rate or none.
- Re-evaluate when quality level changes (see Thermal above).

## Input — Touch

- Minimise touch-to-photon latency: keep the input → update →
  render path short, avoid extra queued frames, prefer Frame
  Pacing (doc 26) in native games.
- Handle multi-touch correctly: track pointer IDs, not just
  "the touch position", so two-thumb controls work.
- Respect display cutouts and gesture insets; never place a
  critical control under the system gesture area.
- Test with real fingers on real hardware — emulator mouse
  input hides latency and multi-touch bugs.

## Input — Controllers and Gamepads

Many players use Bluetooth or USB controllers, and Play Games
on PC players use keyboard and mouse (doc 27).

- Read gamepad state via `InputDevice` and motion events;
  handle `KEYCODE_BUTTON_*` key events for buttons.
- Support hot-plug: controllers connect and disconnect mid-game.
- Show the right prompts: controller glyphs when a controller
  is active, touch hints when it is not, key names on PC.
- Never require a controller, and never require touch, unless
  the genre truly demands it — and say so on the store listing.
- The AGDK Game Controller library can normalise behaviour
  across devices for native games.

## Audio

### Focus and Routing

- Request audio focus for music/gameplay audio; duck or pause
  when you lose it (calls, alarms, other media apps).
- Use `STREAM_MUSIC` semantics via `AudioAttributes` with
  `USAGE_GAME` so the system routes and ducks correctly.

```kotlin
val attrs = AudioAttributes.Builder()
    .setUsage(AudioAttributes.USAGE_GAME)
    .setContentType(AudioAttributes.CONTENT_TYPE_SONIFICATION)
    .build()
```

### Latency and Choice of Player

- Short effects: `SoundPool` (low latency, small samples).
- Music and long tracks: Media3 / ExoPlayer with a proper
  playback service if music continues outside the screen.
- Native low-latency audio: Oboe (C++) for rhythm games or
  instruments where every millisecond counts.
- Keep sample rates consistent (48 kHz is the safe default) to
  avoid resampling cost and artefacts.

### Volume and Safety

- Respect the device media volume; never force maximum volume.
- Provide separate music / effects / voice sliders in settings.
- Fade music on pause instead of cutting it abruptly.

## Saves — Local Design

A save system must survive crashes, updates, and low storage.

Local rules:
- Save to app-private storage (`filesDir`), never shared storage.
- Write atomically: write to a temp file, flush, then rename
  over the real save. A crash mid-write must not corrupt it.
- Version your save format (`saveVersion` field) and migrate
  upward on load. Never silently discard an unreadable save —
  keep a backup copy and tell the player.
- Keep at least one rotating backup of the previous good save.
- Save at natural points: level end, checkpoint, app pause.
  Autosave quietly; show a subtle indicator, not a modal dialog.

```kotlin
@Serializable
data class SaveGame(
    val saveVersion: Int = 3,
    val level: Int,
    val score: Int,
    val inventory: List<String>,
    val playedSeconds: Long
)

suspend fun writeSave(file: File, save: SaveGame) {
    val tmp = File(file.parent, file.name + ".tmp")
    tmp.writeText(json.encodeToString(save))
    tmp.renameTo(file) // atomic on the same filesystem
}
```

## Saves — Cloud with Snapshots

Layer Play Games Snapshots (doc 27) on top of local saves:
- Local save is the source of truth during play.
- Upload to a Snapshot when online and authenticated, at the
  same natural save points (throttled — not every frame/event).
- On start, if a cloud save exists and differs, resolve the
  conflict by policy: longest played time usually wins for
  progression games; ask the player when both saves have real
  progress on different branches.
- Offline play must work fully. Cloud sync is a bonus, never a
  requirement to play.

## Offline Play Design

- All core gameplay works with no network.
- Queue outbound events (score submits, achievement progress,
  analytics you control) and flush when connectivity returns.
- Cache remote config and content with a last-known-good copy.
- Show offline state honestly in UI ("will sync when online"),
  never with a blocking error dialog.

## Pre-Launch Performance Checklist

- Profile on a low-end and a mid-range device, not only flagships.
- Hold target FPS for a 15-minute session (thermal soak test).
- Slow-session risk reviewed in a pre-release report / vitals.
- Controller, touch, and (if relevant) keyboard all tested.
- Audio focus loss (incoming call simulation) tested.
- Save → kill app mid-write → relaunch: save still loads.
- Airplane-mode full playthrough: nothing blocks.

## Official Links

- ADPF: https://developer.android.com/games/adpf
- Thermal API: https://developer.android.com/games/adpf/thermal
- Game Mode: https://developer.android.com/games/gamemode
- Frame Rate API: https://developer.android.com/games/graphics/frame-rate
- Android Vitals: https://developer.android.com/games/sdk/performance-tuner (quality thresholds linked from Play Console help)
- Game controllers: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames/controllers
- Audio focus: https://developer.android.com/media/optimize/audio-focus
- Oboe: https://developer.android.com/games/sdk/oboe
- Saved games: https://developer.android.com/games/playgames/services/common/concepts/saved-games

<!-- ===== docs/29-backend-sync-and-online-services.md ===== -->

# Backend, Sync, and Online Services

How an Android app or game talks to a backend: Firebase as the
default Google-stack option, open self-hosted alternatives,
offline-first sync design, conflict handling, and key safety.

## Do You Need a Backend at All?

Not every app does. You need a backend when you have:
- Accounts shared across devices
- Multiplayer, leaderboards with anti-cheat, or social features
- Content that updates without an app release
- Purchases or entitlements validated server-side
- Cross-device sync beyond Play Games Snapshots

If none apply, ship local-only. A backend is ongoing cost,
privacy duty, and attack surface — add it for a reason.

## Firebase — The Default Option

Firebase: https://firebase.google.com/docs/android/setup

Firebase covers auth, database, storage, messaging, crash
reporting, and remote configuration in one console.

### The BoM Rule — Never Pin Individual Versions

**Always import the Firebase Bill of Materials (BoM) and never
pin individual Firebase library versions.**

```kotlin
// build.gradle.kts
dependencies {
    // Import the BoM — always use the LATEST BoM version.
    // Check https://firebase.google.com/support/release-notes/android
    implementation(platform("com.google.firebase:firebase-bom:<latest>"))

    // Declare libraries WITHOUT versions — the BoM supplies them.
    implementation("com.google.firebase:firebase-analytics")
    implementation("com.google.firebase:firebase-auth")
    implementation("com.google.firebase:firebase-firestore")
    implementation("com.google.firebase:firebase-messaging")
    implementation("com.google.firebase:firebase-crashlytics")
    implementation("com.google.firebase:firebase-config")
}
```

Why: Firebase libraries are released together and tested
against the BoM. Pinning one library to an old version while
others move creates runtime conflicts that are hard to debug.
When you upgrade Firebase, you change one number: the BoM.

Also apply the Google Services plugin and, for Crashlytics
and Performance, their Gradle plugins per the current setup
docs. Add `google-services.json` per the console flow — it
identifies the project; it is not a secret key, but treat the
console itself as sensitive.

### Firebase Auth

- Email/password, Google sign-in, phone, and anonymous auth.
- Anonymous auth first, upgrade later is a good game pattern:
  the player starts instantly and links an identity when ready.
- On Android, integrate with Credential Manager for sign-in.
- All authorisation decisions happen server-side (Firestore
  rules / your backend), never trust a client-side flag.

### Cloud Firestore and Offline Cache

Firestore caches data locally and syncs when online. That
makes it naturally offline-tolerant for reads and queued writes.

```kotlin
val db = FirebaseFirestore.getInstance()
db.collection("players").document(playerId)
    .addSnapshotListener { snapshot, error ->
        if (error != null) return@addSnapshotListener
        val data = snapshot?.toObject(PlayerProfile::class.java)
        if (data != null) renderProfile(data)
    }
```

Rules to follow:
- Design collections around your screens' queries. Firestore
  has no joins; denormalise deliberately.
- Write security rules before launch and test them in the
  emulator suite. Default-open rules are a breach waiting.
- Watch read/write pricing: chatty snapshot listeners on large
  collections cost real money at scale.

The Realtime Database is the older option; prefer Firestore
for new projects unless you specifically need its latency
profile for presence-style data.

### Cloud Messaging (FCM)

FCM delivers push notifications and lightweight data messages.
- Request the notification permission at a sensible moment
  (Android 13+ requires runtime `POST_NOTIFICATIONS`).
- Handle token refresh; send the token to your backend.
- Data messages wake background handling within platform limits;
  do not design critical sync around push delivery timing.

### Crashlytics

- Add the SDK via the BoM and the Crashlytics Gradle plugin.
- Upload mapping files from release builds so stack traces
  de-obfuscate. Automate it in CI (doc 30).
- Add custom keys and logs at key flows (sign-in, purchase,
  save/load) — they make crash reports actionable.

### Remote Config

- Ship safe defaults in the app; Remote Config overrides them.
- Good uses: feature flags, difficulty tuning, event switches,
  minimum-version prompts.
- Bad uses: secrets (values are visible to clients) and
  anything the app cannot run without on first launch.

## Open and Self-Hosted Alternatives

Choose these when you need data ownership, predictable cost,
or to avoid a single-vendor dependency.

| Option | What it is | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Supabase | Hosted/self-hosted Postgres + Auth + Storage + Realtime | Open source core; SQL data model; Kotlin client via supabase-kt |
| Appwrite | Self-hosted backend: auth, DB, storage, functions | Open source; Docker deploy; Android SDK available |
| PocketBase | Single-binary Go backend: auth, DB, realtime, admin UI | **Pre-1.0** — APIs may change between versions; pin carefully and read changelogs before upgrading |
| Parse Server | Open-source Node backend, successor to hosted Parse | Mature; MongoDB/Postgres storage; self-host or hosted providers |

Evaluation checklist for any alternative:
- Is there a maintained Kotlin/Android client, or will you
  write a REST layer yourself with Ktor/Retrofit?
- How are auth tokens stored and refreshed on Android?
- What is the offline story? (Most alternatives are online-first;
  you will build the local cache and sync queue yourself.)
- Backup, export, and exit: can you get all data out?

## Offline-First Sync Design

The pattern that works for apps and games alike:

1. **Local database is the source of truth for the UI.**
   The UI reads and writes Room only. It never waits on network.
2. **A sync layer moves data.** A repository queues outbound
   changes and applies inbound changes to Room.
3. **WorkManager runs sync** with network constraints and
   backoff, plus an immediate sync attempt on local writes
   when online.
4. **Every record carries sync metadata:** a stable ID (UUID),
   `updatedAt`, a version or revision counter, and a deleted
   flag (tombstone) instead of hard deletes.

```kotlin
@Entity(tableName = "notes")
data class NoteEntity(
    @PrimaryKey val id: String, // UUID, client-generated
    val title: String,
    val body: String,
    val updatedAt: Long,
    val revision: Int,
    val deleted: Boolean = false,
    val dirty: Boolean = true // needs upload
)
```

## Conflict Handling

Conflicts are guaranteed once a user has two devices or edits
offline. Pick a policy per data type, in advance:

- **Last-write-wins** by `updatedAt` — fine for settings and
  profile fields; risky for documents.
- **Server revision check** — upload carries the revision it
  edited; the server rejects stale writes, the client re-fetches
  and reapplies or asks the user.
- **Merge** — for counters (add deltas) and sets (union).
- **Ask the user** — for saves and documents with real work in
  both copies. Show both, let them choose, keep the loser as a
  recoverable copy.

Never resolve by silently dropping data. Log conflicts during
development; they reveal design mistakes early.

## Security Rules That Never Bend

- **Never ship admin keys, service account keys, or database
  root credentials in the app.** Anything inside an AAB can be
  extracted. Admin access belongs on a server you control.
- Client keys that are designed to be public (Firebase API key,
  publishable keys) still get restricted: Android app
  restrictions in Google Cloud, API restrictions, and backend
  security rules do the real protection.
- Validate everything server-side: scores, purchases, currency,
  inventory. The client reports; the server decides.
- Use HTTPS only. No cleartext traffic in release builds.
- Store auth tokens in encrypted storage / DataStore with
  platform keystore backing, not plain SharedPreferences.

## Official Links

- Firebase Android setup: https://firebase.google.com/docs/android/setup
- Firebase BoM and releases: https://firebase.google.com/support/release-notes/android
- Firestore: https://firebase.google.com/docs/firestore
- Firebase Auth: https://firebase.google.com/docs/auth/android/start
- FCM: https://firebase.google.com/docs/cloud-messaging/android/client
- Crashlytics: https://firebase.google.com/docs/crashlytics/android/get-started
- Remote Config: https://firebase.google.com/docs/remote-config
- Supabase: https://supabase.com/docs
- Appwrite: https://appwrite.io/docs
- PocketBase: https://pocketbase.io/docs/
- Parse Server: https://docs.parseplatform.org/parse-server/guide/

<!-- ===== docs/30-ci-cd-and-automation.md ===== -->

# CI/CD and Automation

Automating builds, tests, and releases for Kotlin Android
projects: GitHub Actions as the reference setup, Gradle
caching, Play publishing automation, and device testing.

## What CI Should Do on Every Change

1. Check out the code.
2. Set up JDK 17 (the current Android Gradle Plugin baseline).
3. Build debug and run lint + unit tests.
4. On release branches/tags: build a signed AAB.
5. Optionally: run instrumented tests on Firebase Test Lab
   or an emulator, and distribute to testers.

A red build blocks merging. Never normalise a failing pipeline.

## GitHub Actions — Core Workflow

Android builds run on Linux runners (`ubuntu-latest`). Use a
Windows runner only if a step genuinely needs Windows (rare
for Android; the Android SDK and Gradle are cross-platform).

```yaml
name: Android CI
on:
  push:
    branches: [ main ]
  pull_request:

jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - name: Set up JDK 17
        uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: '17'

      - name: Set up Gradle
        uses: gradle/actions/setup-gradle@v4

      - name: Lint and unit tests
        run: ./gradlew lintDebug testDebugUnitTest

      - name: Build debug APK
        run: ./gradlew assembleDebug
```

Notes:
- Pin actions to current major versions and review updates.
  Check each action's releases page when you adopt it.
- `gradle/actions/setup-gradle` handles the Gradle cache
  correctly, including cache keys per dependency state. Prefer
  it over hand-rolled cache steps.
- Make `gradlew` executable in the repo (`chmod +x gradlew`)
  or add a step that does it.

## Gradle Cache and Speed

- Enable the Gradle build cache and configuration cache in
  `gradle.properties` once the build is stable with them.
- Keep dependency versions in the version catalog so CI and
  local builds resolve identically.
- Avoid downloading the Android SDK manually; the runner image
  and AGP handle SDK components. If you must pin SDK tools,
  use the Android SDK setup action from a trusted publisher
  and record why.
- Split jobs when the pipeline grows: lint/test in parallel
  with assemble, instead of one long serial job.

## Release Builds in CI

```yaml
  release:
    if: startsWith(github.ref, 'refs/tags/')
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: '17'
      - uses: gradle/actions/setup-gradle@v4

      - name: Build release AAB
        run: ./gradlew bundleRelease
        env:
          KEYSTORE_BASE64: ${{ secrets.KEYSTORE_BASE64 }}
          KEY_ALIAS: ${{ secrets.KEY_ALIAS }}
          KEY_PASSWORD: ${{ secrets.KEY_PASSWORD }}
          STORE_PASSWORD: ${{ secrets.STORE_PASSWORD }}
```

Signing design:
- The **upload keystore** lives only as CI secrets (base64 in
  an encrypted secret) and in your offline backup. It is never
  committed to the repo.
- Decode it to a temp file in the job, sign, and let the runner
  be destroyed. Play App Signing holds the final app signing
  key (see AGENTS.md rule 4).
- Read passwords from secrets in the Gradle signing config via
  environment variables — never hard-code them in build files.

## Play Publishing Automation

The Gradle Play Publisher plugin automates uploads to Play
Console: AAB upload, release notes, track promotion.

- Plugin: `com.github.triplet.play` (Gradle Play Publisher)
- Docs: https://github.com/Triple-T/gradle-play-publisher

```kotlin
// build.gradle.kts (app module) — shape only; check current docs
play {
    serviceAccountCredentials.set(file("play-service-account.json"))
    track.set("internal")
    releaseStatus.set(com.github.triplet.gradle.androidpublisher.ReleaseStatus.DRAFT)
}
```

Safety rules:
- The Play service account JSON is a **secret**: store it in CI
  secrets, never in the repo. Grant it only the permissions the
  release job needs, for only this app.
- Default to **draft** releases and internal/closed tracks.
  Promote to production deliberately, ideally as a manual step.
- Keep release notes in the repo per locale
  (`src/main/play/release-notes/...`) so they are reviewed in PRs.

Alternatives: Fastlane (`supply`) can drive Play publishing
from Ruby, and the Play Developer API can be called directly
from a script. Pick one route and document it; do not mix.

## Device Testing — Firebase Test Lab and Alternatives

Unit tests do not prove an app runs on real devices.

Options:
- **Firebase Test Lab** — real and virtual devices, Robo tests
  and instrumented tests, results in the Firebase console.
  Trigger from CI with the `gcloud` CLI or a Test Lab action.
- **Firebase App Distribution** — sends test builds to tester
  groups; a good middle step before Play tracks.
- **Play internal testing track** — real Play delivery to up
  to 100 testers; closest to production behaviour.
- **GitHub Actions emulator runner** — the Android emulator
  action on a macOS or Linux runner for instrumented tests
  without an external service. Slower, but self-contained.

Pragmatic split used by many teams:
- Every PR: lint + unit tests + assemble.
- Nightly or pre-release: instrumented tests on Test Lab.
- Release candidate: internal track, then staged rollout.

## Secrets Handling — The Full List

| Secret | Where it lives |
|---|---|
| Upload keystore file | CI secret (base64) + offline backup |
| Keystore / key passwords | CI secrets |
| Play service account JSON | CI secret |
| Firebase config for CI jobs | CI secret if sensitive in context |
| API keys for third-party services | CI secrets for builds; runtime keys via backend or restricted keys |

Never:
- Print secrets in logs (masking helps, not printing is better).
- Put secrets in Gradle files, `gradle.properties` in the repo,
  or workflow YAML in plain text.
- Reuse a production service account for pull-request builds
  from forks. Fork PRs should run without release secrets.

## Versioning in Automation

- Derive `versionCode` from a single source: a checked-in
  counter bumped by the release job, or CI run numbers mapped
  carefully. It must always increase.
- `versionName` follows your scheme (for example semantic
  versioning) and is set in the release commit/tag.
- Tag releases in git (`v1.4.0`) and let the tag trigger the
  release workflow, so the shipped AAB is traceable to a commit.

## Common Pipeline Failures

- JDK mismatch: AGP needs JDK 17 for current versions. A runner
  defaulting to an older JDK fails with confusing errors.
- Missing SDK licenses on a fresh runner setup.
- Lint treated as optional until it blocks a release — run it
  on every PR instead.
- Flaky instrumented tests retried until green. Fix or quarantine
  flaky tests; do not hide them behind retries.

## Official Links

- GitHub Actions: https://docs.github.com/en/actions
- setup-java: https://github.com/actions/setup-java
- Gradle setup action: https://github.com/gradle/actions
- Gradle build cache: https://docs.gradle.org/current/userguide/build_cache.html
- Gradle Play Publisher: https://github.com/Triple-T/gradle-play-publisher
- Play Developer API: https://developers.google.com/android-publisher
- Firebase Test Lab: https://firebase.google.com/docs/test-lab
- Firebase App Distribution: https://firebase.google.com/docs/app-distribution
- Fastlane supply: https://docs.fastlane.tools/actions/supply/


---

<!-- ===== docs/31-migration-java-and-views-to-modern-kotlin.md ===== -->

# Migration — Java and Views to Modern Kotlin

How to move an existing Android project forward in small, safe
steps: Java to Kotlin, Views to Compose, and the old async and
storage APIs to their modern replacements. Migrate
incrementally — big-bang rewrites are how projects die.

## Ground Rules for Any Migration

1. The app must build and pass tests after every step.
2. One migration axis at a time: language, UI, or storage —
   never all three in one change.
3. Keep the old and new code interoperable during the move.
   Kotlin and Views/Compose are both designed for this.
4. Add tests around behaviour before changing its technology,
   especially for data migrations.

## Java to Kotlin — Interop First

Kotlin and Java interoperate fully in one module. Convert file
by file, starting with leaf classes: models, utilities, and
small helpers, then activities and complex classes last.

### Nullability Is the Main Work

Java types arrive in Kotlin as platform types (`String!`),
which carry no null guarantee. Fix the boundary:

```java
// Java: annotate so Kotlin sees real nullability
import androidx.annotation.NonNull;
import androidx.annotation.Nullable;

public class User {
    @NonNull private final String id;
    @Nullable private String nickname;
    // getters...
}
```

```kotlin
// Kotlin caller now gets String and String? — no guessing.
fun greeting(user: User): String =
    user.nickname ?: user.id
```

In converted Kotlin code:
- Prefer `val` over `var`, data classes for models.
- Replace static utility classes with top-level functions or
  an `object` only where a singleton is truly intended.
- Watch SAM conversions, getters/setters becoming properties,
  and checked exceptions disappearing (Kotlin has none) —
  callers in Java may have relied on them.

Android Studio's Java-to-Kotlin converter is a starting point,
not a finish: review every converted file for platform types,
`lateinit` overuse, and Java idioms that have a cleaner Kotlin
form. Do not convert generated code or third-party sources.

## Views to Compose — Incremental by Design

Compose interoperates with the View system in both directions,
so migrate screen by screen.

### Compose Inside a View Screen

```kotlin
// In an existing Fragment/Activity layout, a ComposeView hosts Compose.
binding.composeView.setContent {
    MyTheme { ProfileHeader(user) }
}
```

### Views Inside Compose

```kotlin
@Composable
fun LegacyMap(modifier: Modifier = Modifier) {
    AndroidView(
        factory = { context -> MapView(context) },
        modifier = modifier,
        update = { view -> /* push new state into the view */ }
    )
}
```

Migration order that works well:
1. New screens in Compose first — no migration risk.
2. Simple existing screens (settings, about, lists) next.
3. Complex custom views last, or keep them wrapped in
   `AndroidView` if they work well.
4. Move navigation last, once most destinations are Compose.

Theme bridge: define colours/typography once and share them.
During transition, Compose's `MaterialTheme` can wrap migrated
screens inside View-based activities via `ComposeView`.

Do not migrate and redesign in the same step. First make the
Compose version look and behave like the View version; redesign
later, as its own change.

## AsyncTask and Loaders to Coroutines

`AsyncTask` is deprecated and `Loaders` are superseded. Replace
both with coroutines plus a ViewModel that survives rotation.

```kotlin
// Before (conceptual): AsyncTask doing network then updating UI.
// After:
class FeedViewModel(
    private val repo: FeedRepository
) : ViewModel() {
    private val _state = MutableStateFlow<UiState>(UiState.Loading)
    val state: StateFlow<UiState> = _state.asStateFlow()

    fun load() {
        viewModelScope.launch {
            _state.value = try {
                UiState.Data(repo.fetchFeed()) // suspend, off main thread in repo
            } catch (e: IOException) {
                UiState.Error("Could not load. Check your connection.")
            }
        }
    }
}
```

Rules:
- `viewModelScope` for UI work, repository dispatchers for IO.
- Never `GlobalScope`. Every launch has an owner.
- Loaders' job (survive config change, deliver once) is covered
  by ViewModel + `StateFlow` collection with lifecycle awareness.

## SQLite to Room

Moving from raw `SQLiteOpenHelper` to Room:

1. Keep the same database name and version history. Room can
   open the existing database if the schema matches.
2. Define entities to match existing tables exactly, export the
   schema, and compare with the live schema.
3. Reuse your existing migrations: wrap the same SQL in Room
   `Migration` objects so upgrades behave identically.

```kotlin
val MIGRATION_3_4 = object : Migration(3, 4) {
    override fun migrate(db: SupportSQLiteDatabase) {
        db.execSQL("ALTER TABLE notes ADD COLUMN pinned INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0")
    }
}

Room.databaseBuilder(context, AppDb::class.java, "app.db")
    .addMigrations(MIGRATION_3_4)
    .build()
```

Note the AGENTS.md rule: Room 2 and Room 3 are different
majors. Decide which major the project uses first (doc 11)
and use only that major's setup and migration APIs.

Test migrations with real old-version database files — a
migration bug destroys user data, the worst failure class.

## SharedPreferences to DataStore

DataStore replaces SharedPreferences for settings and small
key-value state: it is asynchronous, transactional, and does
not block the main thread.

```kotlin
val Context.settingsStore by preferencesDataStore(name = "settings")

val darkModeKey = booleanPreferencesKey("dark_mode")

suspend fun setDarkMode(context: Context, enabled: Boolean) {
    context.settingsStore.edit { prefs -> prefs[darkModeKey] = enabled }
}

val darkModeFlow: Flow<Boolean> = context.settingsStore.data
    .map { prefs -> prefs[darkModeKey] ?: false }
```

Migration path:
- DataStore provides a `SharedPreferencesMigration` that moves
  existing preferences once. Use it so users keep settings.
- Move simple flags to Preferences DataStore.
- Move structured settings (multiple related fields) to Proto
  DataStore when the schema matters.
- Keep secrets out of plain DataStore files; encrypt sensitive
  values or use a keystore-backed store (see doc 29 rules).

## Other Common Replacements

| Old | Modern replacement |
|---|---|
| `AsyncTask` | Coroutines + ViewModel |
| `Loaders` | ViewModel + StateFlow |
| Raw `SQLiteOpenHelper` | Room |
| `SharedPreferences` | DataStore |
| Java `Executor` plumbing | Coroutine dispatchers |
| `LiveData` (new code) | `StateFlow` / `SharedFlow` |
| Standalone ExoPlayer 2 | Media3 (`androidx.media3`) |
| `findViewById` | View Binding, then Compose |
| Listeners everywhere | Flow / coroutines channels where apt |

## A Safe Migration Plan (Template)

1. Baseline: green build, tests, a release you can roll back to.
2. Convert Java leaf classes to Kotlin; ship.
3. Replace AsyncTask/Loader hotspots with coroutines; ship.
4. Move SharedPreferences to DataStore with migration; ship.
5. Wrap SQLite in Room with tested migrations; ship.
6. Migrate screens to Compose one by one; ship each.
7. Remove View-era leftovers only when nothing references them.

Each "ship" can be an internal track if the app is sensitive.
The point is small, reversible steps with a working app between.

## Official Links

- Kotlin/Java interop: https://kotlinlang.org/docs/java-interop.html
- Calling Kotlin from Java: https://kotlinlang.org/docs/java-to-kotlin-interop.html
- Compose/View interop: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/compose/migrate/interoperability-apis
- Migration strategy to Compose: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/compose/migrate/strategy
- Coroutines: https://developer.android.com/kotlin/coroutines
- Room migrations: https://developer.android.com/training/data-storage/room/migrating-db-versions
- DataStore: https://developer.android.com/topic/libraries/architecture/datastore
- DataStore migration from SharedPreferences: https://developer.android.com/topic/libraries/architecture/datastore#preferences-migration

<!-- ===== docs/32-patterns-cookbook.md ===== -->

# Patterns Cookbook

Short, copy-adaptable recipes for problems nearly every Kotlin
Android app hits. Each recipe is a sketch: adapt names, models,
and error handling to your project, and verify library versions
against the official release pages before pinning.

## 1. Offline-First Repository

UI reads the local database. Network refreshes the database.
The UI never talks to the network directly.

```kotlin
class ArticleRepository(
    private val dao: ArticleDao,
    private val api: ArticleApi,
    private val io: CoroutineDispatcher = Dispatchers.IO
) {
    val articles: Flow<List<Article>> =
        dao.observeAll().map { rows -> rows.map { it.toDomain() } }

    suspend fun refresh() = withContext(io) {
        val remote = api.fetchArticles() // throws on failure
        dao.replaceAll(remote.map { it.toEntity() })
    }
}
```

## 2. Paging with Paging 3

For long lists backed by network and/or Room. Paging 3 current
line at time of writing: **3.5.3** in the `3.5.x` series —
verify on the AndroidX Paging release page before pinning.

```kotlin
class ArticlePagingSource(private val api: ArticleApi) : PagingSource<Int, Article>() {
    override fun getRefreshKey(state: PagingState<Int, Article>) =
        state.anchorPosition?.let { pos ->
            state.closestPageToPosition(pos)?.prevKey?.plus(1)
                ?: state.closestPageToPosition(pos)?.nextKey?.minus(1)
        }

    override suspend fun load(params: LoadParams<Int>): LoadResult<Int, Article> {
        val page = params.key ?: 1
        return try {
            val data = api.fetchPage(page, params.loadSize)
            LoadResult.Page(
                data = data,
                prevKey = if (page == 1) null else page - 1,
                nextKey = if (data.isEmpty()) null else page + 1
            )
        } catch (e: IOException) {
            LoadResult.Error(e)
        }
    }
}

val pagerFlow: Flow<PagingData<Article>> = Pager(
    config = PagingConfig(pageSize = 20, enablePlaceholders = false),
    pagingSourceFactory = { ArticlePagingSource(api) }
).flow.cachedIn(viewModelScope)
```


## 3. Search with Debounced Flow

Wait until typing pauses, skip duplicate queries, cancel the
previous search when a new one starts.

```kotlin
class SearchViewModel(private val repo: SearchRepository) : ViewModel() {
    private val queries = MutableStateFlow("")
    val results: StateFlow<List<Result>> = queries
        .debounce(300)
        .map { it.trim() }
        .distinctUntilChanged()
        .flatMapLatest { q ->
            if (q.length < 2) flowOf(emptyList())
            else flow { emit(repo.search(q)) }
        }
        .stateIn(viewModelScope, SharingStarted.WhileSubscribed(5_000), emptyList())

    fun onQueryChange(text: String) { queries.value = text }
}
```

## 4. Upload with WorkManager

Uploads must survive process death and bad networks. WorkManager
with constraints and backoff is the tool.

```kotlin
class UploadWorker(
    context: Context,
    params: WorkerParameters
) : CoroutineWorker(context, params) {
    override suspend fun doWork(): Result {
        val fileId = inputData.getString(KEY_FILE_ID) ?: return Result.failure()
        return try {
            uploader.upload(fileId)
            Result.success()
        } catch (e: IOException) {
            if (runAttemptCount < 3) Result.retry() else Result.failure()
        }
    }
    companion object { const val KEY_FILE_ID = "file_id" }
}

fun enqueueUpload(context: Context, fileId: String) {
    val request = OneTimeWorkRequestBuilder<UploadWorker>()
        .setConstraints(
            Constraints.Builder()
                .setRequiredNetworkType(NetworkType.CONNECTED)
                .build()
        )
        .setBackoffCriteria(BackoffPolicy.EXPONENTIAL, 30, TimeUnit.SECONDS)
        .setInputData(workDataOf(UploadWorker.KEY_FILE_ID to fileId))
        .build()
    WorkManager.getInstance(context)
        .enqueueUniqueWork("upload-$fileId", ExistingWorkPolicy.KEEP, request)
}
```

## 5. In-App Update (Play Core)

Prompt for updates through Play instead of shipping your own
updater. Use the Play In-App Updates API (AppUpdateManager).

```kotlin
val updateManager = AppUpdateManagerFactory.create(context)
updateManager.appUpdateInfo.addOnSuccessListener { info ->
    if (info.updateAvailability() == UpdateAvailability.UPDATE_AVAILABLE &&
        info.isUpdateTypeAllowed(AppUpdateType.FLEXIBLE)
    ) {
        updateManager.startUpdateFlowForResult(
            info, AppUpdateType.FLEXIBLE, activity, REQUEST_CODE
        )
    }
}
```

Flexible for minor updates (downloads in background), Immediate
for critical fixes. Always handle the user declining: the app
must keep working on the old version unless policy truly forbids it.

## 6. Deep Link Handling

Declare intent filters for your links, prefer App Links
(verified https links) over custom schemes, and route in
navigation.

```xml
<intent-filter android:autoVerify="true">
    <action android:name="android.intent.action.VIEW" />
    <category android:name="android.intent.category.DEFAULT" />
    <category android:name="android.intent.category.BROWSABLE" />
    <data android:scheme="https" android:host="example.com" android:pathPrefix="/item/" />
</intent-filter>
```

```kotlin
// Navigation Compose destination with a deep link
composable(
    route = "item/{id}",
    deepLinks = listOf(navDeepLink { uriPattern = "https://example.com/item/{id}" })
) { entry -> ItemScreen(id = entry.arguments?.getString("id").orEmpty()) }
```

Host `assetlinks.json` on the domain for verification. Test
links with `adb shell am start -a android.intent.action.VIEW -d <url>`.

## 7. Error UI State

One sealed state type per screen keeps loading, content, and
error handling honest.

```kotlin
sealed interface UiState<out T> {
    data object Loading : UiState<Nothing>
    data class Data<T>(val value: T) : UiState<T>
    data class Error(val message: String, val retry: () -> Unit) : UiState<Nothing>
}

@Composable
fun <T> StateHost(state: UiState<T>, content: @Composable (T) -> Unit) {
    when (state) {
        is UiState.Loading -> LoadingIndicator()
        is UiState.Error -> ErrorPanel(state.message, state.retry)
        is UiState.Data -> content(state.value)
    }
}
```

Error messages say what happened and what to do next, in plain
words. Never show a raw exception message to the user.

## 8. Settings Screen with DataStore

```kotlin
val Context.store by preferencesDataStore(name = "settings")
val KEY_DARK = booleanPreferencesKey("dark_mode")
val KEY_NAME = stringPreferencesKey("display_name")

class SettingsRepository(private val context: Context) {
    val settings: Flow<Settings> = context.store.data.map { prefs ->
        Settings(darkMode = prefs[KEY_DARK] ?: false, name = prefs[KEY_NAME] ?: "")
    }
    suspend fun setDarkMode(enabled: Boolean) {
        context.store.edit { it[KEY_DARK] = enabled }
    }
    suspend fun setName(name: String) {
        context.store.edit { it[KEY_NAME] = name }
    }
}

@Composable
fun SettingsScreen(repo: SettingsRepository) {
    val settings by repo.settings.collectAsStateWithLifecycle(
        initialValue = Settings(darkMode = false, name = "")
    )
    val scope = rememberCoroutineScope()
    Switch(
        checked = settings.darkMode,
        onCheckedChange = { scope.launch { repo.setDarkMode(it) } }
    )
}
```

## Recipe Rules

- Every recipe assumes coroutines + Flow; add the lifecycle
  collection (`collectAsStateWithLifecycle`) in real screens.
- Versions shown are orientation, not gospel: check the
  library's release page on the day you copy a recipe.
- Add the failure path before the happy path ships. Every
  recipe above has one — keep it when you adapt the code.

## Official Links

- Paging: https://developer.android.com/topic/libraries/architecture/paging/v3-overview
- WorkManager: https://developer.android.com/topic/libraries/architecture/workmanager
- In-app updates: https://developer.android.com/guide/playcore/in-app-updates
- App Links: https://developer.android.com/training/app-links
- DataStore: https://developer.android.com/topic/libraries/architecture/datastore
- Flow: https://developer.android.com/kotlin/flow

<!-- ===== docs/33-ai-assisted-development.md ===== -->

# AI-Assisted Development — How an AI Should Use This Repo

Instructions for any AI assistant (ChatGPT, or another model)
that has downloaded this repository and is using it to write
Android apps and games in Kotlin. These rules exist because
Android development has many version-gated APIs and renamed
libraries, and confident invention is the main failure mode.

## Start in the Right Place

1. **Read `manifest.json` at the repo root first.** It describes
   what this knowledge base contains and how it is organised.
2. **Read `AGENTS.md` at the repo root next.** It holds the
   non-negotiable rules for code written with this repo. Those
   rules outrank any single doc in `docs/`.
3. Then read the doc for your task area. Use the index in
   `README.md` / `ai-bundle.md` to find it instead of guessing
   file names.
4. For a game, read doc 23 (choosing a route) before any other
   game doc, and state the chosen route before writing code.

## State the Target Before Writing Code

Before generating code, state in plain words:
- compileSdk, targetSdk, and minSdk
- App or game — and for a game, the route (Compose Canvas,
  libGDX + KTX, KorGE, Godot via godot-jvm, or native AGDK)
- Kotlin version family (2.x) and whether Compose is used
- The major versions that matter for the task: Room major,
  Billing Library (8+), Play Games Services (v2 only),
  Media3 (not standalone ExoPlayer 2), Coil major

If the user has not specified these and the choice changes the
code, ask — or pick the current documented defaults from doc 02
and say that you did.

## Verify Versions on Official Pages Before Pinning

**Never pin a version from memory — including from this repo.**

This repo is a snapshot. Versions move. Before writing any
version number into a build file:

1. Check the official source:
   - AndroidX libraries: the AndroidX release notes and
     Google's Maven repository listing
   - Kotlin and plugins: kotlinlang.org release pages
   - Firebase: the Firebase Android release notes — and use
     the latest BoM, never individual pinned versions (doc 29)
   - Play services: the Play services release notes
   - Engines: the engine's own releases page (libGDX/KTX,
     KorGE, godot-jvm — see doc 25 warnings)
2. If you cannot check (offline, tool unavailable), use the
   version this repo documents and label it clearly:
   "version as documented in KotlinDev, not re-verified today."
3. Prefer version catalogs (`libs.versions.toml`) so versions
   live in one place in the generated project.

## Never Invent AndroidX APIs or Versions

The single most damaging AI habit on Android is inventing
plausible-looking APIs. Rules:

- Do not write a Maven coordinate you cannot trace to Google
  Maven, Maven Central, or the library's official docs.
- Do not write an API call from a half-remembered name. If the
  exact signature is uncertain, say so and show the lookup the
  human should do, rather than fabricating code that will not
  compile.
- Watch the known traps named in `AGENTS.md`: Room 2 vs Room 3
  mixing, `kotlinCompilerExtensionVersion` in Kotlin 2.x
  projects, PGS v1 APIs in new games, Billing below version 8,
  Coil 2 coordinates in a Coil 3 project, standalone
  ExoPlayer 2 instead of Media3.
- Blog posts, videos, and forum answers are leads, not proof.
  An API is real when official docs or the artifact itself
  shows it.

## Mark Claims: Verified, Partially Verified, Unverified

Borrow the labelling habit this repo uses for its own claims:

- **Verified** — you checked it against an official source in
  this session, or the code was compiled and run.
- **Partially Verified** — the approach is documented, but a
  detail (a version, a device behaviour) was not checked.
- **Unverified** — plausible, but not checked. Say what would
  verify it.

Use these labels for anything the user will act on: version
choices, policy claims (Play deadlines, billing rules), and
"this will fix it" statements about bugs.

## Test Claims

- Code you generate has not been tested until it compiles.
  Say "not compiled" plainly when you cannot run a build.
- When you can run tools: build first, then unit tests, then
  a device/emulator run if the change is UI or lifecycle code.
- For data migrations (Room, DataStore, save formats), describe
  the upgrade test from the old version — untested migrations
  are the highest-risk code in an app.
- Never report a test as passing that you did not run. Report
  what ran, what passed, and what could not be run.

## Working Habits That Prevent Damage

- **Small steps.** Generate one screen or feature end to end,
  not a whole app in one answer. Each step should compile.
- **No secrets.** Never write real API keys, keystore
  passwords, or service account JSON into code or examples.
  Use placeholders that are obviously placeholders.
- **Respect platform limits.** Permissions at point of use,
  background work through WorkManager or declared foreground
  services, no exact alarms or special permissions without a
  qualifying feature (see `AGENTS.md` rule 10).
- **Keep the user's data safe.** Atomic file writes, versioned
  save/database schemas, backups before destructive migration.
- **Plain language.** Explain choices in everyday words, define
  a technical term the first time it appears, and give the
  reason behind a recommendation — not just the recommendation.

## When This Repo and the Live Docs Disagree

Live official documentation wins. This repo is curated, but it
ages. If a version, policy date, or API in these docs conflicts
with developer.android.com, kotlinlang.org, or a library's
current release notes:

1. Follow the live source.
2. Tell the user there was a conflict and which source you used.
3. Suggest the repo doc be updated — do not silently mix both.

## Quick Self-Check Before Answering

- [ ] Did I read `manifest.json` and `AGENTS.md` guidance?
- [ ] Did I state the target (SDK levels, app/game route)?
- [ ] Is every version traceable, or labelled as unverified?
- [ ] Is every API call one I can source?
- [ ] Did I avoid the named traps (Room mixing, PGS v1,
      Billing < 8, ExoPlayer 2, Coil mixing, KAPT over KSP)?
- [ ] Did I label test status honestly?
- [ ] Are there zero real secrets in my output?

## Official Links

- Android developer docs: https://developer.android.com/
- Kotlin: https://kotlinlang.org/
- AndroidX releases: https://developer.android.com/jetpack/androidx/releases
- Google Maven: https://maven.google.com/
- Maven Central: https://central.sonatype.com/
- Play Console help: https://support.google.com/googleplay/android-developer
- Firebase release notes: https://firebase.google.com/support/release-notes/android


---

<!-- ===== reference/adb-and-tooling-commands.md ===== -->

# Reference — adb and Tooling Commands

Everyday command-line tools. `adb`, `bundletool`, `apkanalyzer`, and
`lint` ship with the Android SDK (platform-tools / cmdline-tools);
paths below assume they are on `PATH`. Snapshot checked November 2026.

## adb — devices and install

```bash
adb devices -l                       # list connected devices/emulators
adb -s <serial> install -r app.apk   # install (replace) on one device
adb -s <serial> uninstall com.example.app
adb shell pm clear com.example.app   # wipe app data
adb shell am start -n com.example.app/.MainActivity
adb shell pidof com.example.app
adb kill-server && adb start-server  # restart a wedged adb
```

## adb — debugging

```bash
adb logcat -v threadtime --pid=$(adb shell pidof com.example.app)
adb logcat *:E                       # errors only (noisy — prefer pid filter)
adb shell dumpsys activity top       # what is on screen / activity state
adb shell dumpsys meminfo com.example.app
adb shell dumpsys gfxinfo com.example.app framestats   # jank data
adb shell dumpsys battery
adb bugreport bugreport.zip          # full device report for hard bugs
adb shell screencap -p /sdcard/shot.png && adb pull /sdcard/shot.png
adb shell screenrecord /sdcard/demo.mp4   # Ctrl-C to stop, then adb pull
adb shell input tap 500 500          # synthetic input
adb shell settings get global animator_duration_scale
```

## adb — files, permissions, app info

```bash
adb push local.txt /sdcard/Download/
adb pull /sdcard/Download/remote.txt .
adb shell pm list packages | grep example
adb shell dumpsys package com.example.app | grep -A5 permissions
adb shell pm grant com.example.app android.permission.POST_NOTIFICATIONS
adb shell pm revoke com.example.app android.permission.CAMERA
adb shell appops set com.example.app POST_NOTIFICATION allow
adb reverse tcp:8080 tcp:8080        # device localhost -> dev machine (local servers)
adb forward tcp:8080 tcp:8080        # dev machine -> device
```

## Emulator (AVD)

```bash
emulator -list-avds
emulator -avd Pixel_9_API_36 -no-snapshot-load     # cold boot
emulator -avd Pixel_9_API_36 -wipe-data
sdkmanager --list | grep "system-images;android-36"
sdkmanager "platforms;android-36" "platform-tools" "emulator"
avdmanager list device | grep pixel
# Inside the emulator: extended controls give location, network
# throttling, battery, foldable posture, and snapshot control.
```

Useful emulator habits: keep one API 36 AVD for the target, one at
minSdk for compatibility, and one large-screen/foldable AVD for form
factor checks ([`../docs/21-form-factors-wear-tv-auto-foldables.md`](../docs/21-form-factors-wear-tv-auto-foldables.md)).

## bundletool — AAB inspection and testing

Play ships AABs; bundletool turns one into testable APKs locally.

```bash
# Build a universal APK for quick device testing
java -jar bundletool.jar build-apks --bundle=app-release.aab \
  --output=app.apks --mode=universal \
  --ks=upload.jks --ks-key-alias=upload --ks-pass=pass:... --key-pass=pass:...

java -jar bundletool.jar install-apks --apks=app.apks   # device-specific set
java -jar bundletool.jar get-size total --apks=app.apks # download-size estimate
java -jar bundletool.jar validate --bundle=app-release.aab
java -jar bundletool.jar dump manifest --bundle=app-release.aab
```

Never commit the keystore or its passwords (see `AGENTS.md` rule 9);
the commands above take them from your local secret store.

## apkanalyzer — APK/AAB contents

```bash
apkanalyzer apk summary app.apk                 # package, versionCode/Name
apkanalyzer manifest print app.apk | head -n 80
apkanalyzer dex packages app.apk | sort -k3 -rn | head   # method/size hotspots
apkanalyzer files list app.apk
apkanalyzer apk compare old.apk new.apk         # size diff between builds
```

## Lint

```bash
./gradlew lintRelease                 # full lint on the release variant
./gradlew :app:lintDebug
# Reports: app/build/reports/lint-results-release.html (+ .xml / .sarif)
```

Treat new lint errors as build failures in CI; baseline only known,
accepted issues (`lint-baseline.xml`) and burn the baseline down over
time ([`../docs/30-ci-cd-and-automation.md`](../docs/30-ci-cd-and-automation.md)).

## Gradle everyday

```bash
./gradlew assembleDebug               # debug APK
./gradlew bundleRelease               # release AAB (the Play artifact)
./gradlew test                        # JVM unit tests, all modules
./gradlew connectedDebugAndroidTest   # instrumented tests on a device/emulator
./gradlew :app:dependencies --configuration releaseRuntimeClasspath
./gradlew --refresh-dependencies ...  # after a repository/version fix
./gradlew clean
```

## scrcpy — mirror a real device

[scrcpy](https://github.com/Genymobile/scrcpy) (free, open source)
mirrors and controls a physical device over adb from the dev machine —
the fastest way to demo, record, and test touch/controller flows on
real hardware without extra tooling.

```bash
scrcpy                       # mirror the connected device
scrcpy --record demo.mp4     # mirror + record
scrcpy -m 1024 --video-bit-rate 4M   # lighter stream for slow links
```

Related: [`../docs/16-testing-strategy.md`](../docs/16-testing-strategy.md),
[`../docs/19-build-signing-release-and-google-play.md`](../docs/19-build-signing-release-and-google-play.md).

<!-- ===== reference/api-levels.md ===== -->

# Reference — Android API Levels

Every Android platform version, its API level, and the
`Build.VERSION_CODES` constant. `compileSdk` / `targetSdk` /
`minSdk` in a project are these numbers. Snapshot checked November
2026 — the authoritative list is
https://developer.android.com/reference/android/os/Build.VERSION_CODES.

| API | Version | Codename | `VERSION_CODE` constant |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1.0 | — (Base) | `BASE` |
| 2 | 1.1 | — (Base 1.1) | `BASE_1_1` |
| 3 | 1.5 | Cupcake | `CUPCAKE` |
| 4 | 1.6 | Donut | `DONUT` |
| 5 | 2.0 | Eclair | `ECLAIR` |
| 6 | 2.0.1 | Eclair 0.1 | `ECLAIR_0_1` |
| 7 | 2.1 | Eclair MR1 | `ECLAIR_MR1` |
| 8 | 2.2 | Froyo | `FROYO` |
| 9 | 2.3–2.3.2 | Gingerbread | `GINGERBREAD` |
| 10 | 2.3.3–2.3.7 | Gingerbread MR1 | `GINGERBREAD_MR1` |
| 11 | 3.0 | Honeycomb | `HONEYCOMB` |
| 12 | 3.1 | Honeycomb MR1 | `HONEYCOMB_MR1` |
| 13 | 3.2 | Honeycomb MR2 | `HONEYCOMB_MR2` |
| 14 | 4.0–4.0.2 | Ice Cream Sandwich | `ICE_CREAM_SANDWICH` |
| 15 | 4.0.3–4.0.4 | Ice Cream Sandwich MR1 | `ICE_CREAM_SANDWICH_MR1` |
| 16 | 4.1 | Jelly Bean | `JELLY_BEAN` |
| 17 | 4.2 | Jelly Bean MR1 | `JELLY_BEAN_MR1` |
| 18 | 4.3 | Jelly Bean MR2 | `JELLY_BEAN_MR2` |
| 19 | 4.4 | KitKat | `KITKAT` |
| 20 | 4.4W | KitKat Watch | `KITKAT_WATCH` |
| 21 | 5.0 | Lollipop | `LOLLIPOP` |
| 22 | 5.1 | Lollipop MR1 | `LOLLIPOP_MR1` |
| 23 | 6.0 | Marshmallow | `M` |
| 24 | 7.0 | Nougat | `N` |
| 25 | 7.1 | Nougat MR1 | `N_MR1` |
| 26 | 8.0 | Oreo | `O` |
| 27 | 8.1 | Oreo MR1 | `O_MR1` |
| 28 | 9 | Pie | `P` |
| 29 | 10 | Quince Tart | `Q` |
| 30 | 11 | Red Velvet Cake | `R` |
| 31 | 12 | Snow Cone | `S` |
| 32 | 12L | Snow Cone v2 | `S_V2` |
| 33 | 13 | Tiramisu | `TIRAMISU` |
| 34 | 14 | Upside Down Cake | `UPSIDE_DOWN_CAKE` |
| 35 | 15 | Vanilla Ice Cream | `VANILLA_ICE_CREAM` |
| 36 | 16 | Baklava | `BAKLAVA` |
| 36.1 | 16 (QPR minor release) | Baklava 36.1 | `BAKLAVA_36_1` |
| 37 | 17 | Cinnamon Bun | `CINNAMON_BUN` |

## How to use this table

- **minSdk** — the oldest API the app installs on. Common modern
  choices: 24 (covers the large majority of active devices) or 26.
  Every API below minSdk never needs a runtime version check.
- **targetSdk** — the newest API whose behavior changes the app has
  opted into. Play requires target **36** for new apps and updates
  since August 31, 2026 (see
  [`play-requirements-and-deadlines.md`](play-requirements-and-deadlines.md)).
- **compileSdk** — the API the code compiles against; must be ≥
  targetSdk. Use 36 normally; the newest Compose features may
  require compileSdk **37**.
- Guard newer APIs with `if (Build.VERSION.SDK_INT >= Build.VERSION_CODES.… )`
  checks, or use AndroidX compat helpers that do it for you.
- Codenames stopped being public dessert names after Android 9 (Pie);
  the internal dessert codenames above are still the `VERSION_CODE`
  names, which is why code refers to `TIRAMISU`, `BAKLAVA`, etc.

Related: [`../docs/02-android-platform-and-versions.md`](../docs/02-android-platform-and-versions.md).

<!-- ===== reference/game-route-comparison.md ===== -->

# Reference — Game Route Comparison

The route matrix from [`../docs/23-games-overview-choosing-a-route.md`](../docs/23-games-overview-choosing-a-route.md)
in table form. Every route ships as an AAB, targets API 36, uses
Billing 8+ and PGS v2 — the routes differ in rendering, language
surface, and effort, not in Play obligations.

| | Compose Canvas 2D | libGDX (+ KTX) | KorGE | Godot (+ godot-kotlin/jvm) | Native (AGDK / NDK) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Language surface** | Pure Kotlin + Compose | Kotlin over Java framework (KTX idioms) | Pure Kotlin, multiplatform | GDScript / C# / Kotlin-JVM via plugin | C/C++ (+ Kotlin shell) |
| **Rendering** | Compose `Canvas` / draw scope | OpenGL ES via libGDX | OpenGL (Korge's AG layer) | Godot renderer (Vulkan/GLES) | OpenGL ES / Vulkan, hand-rolled or engine |
| **Best for** | Puzzle, board, card, word, light arcade; games that are mostly UI | 2D and mid 3D, cross-platform desktop+mobile | 2D multiplatform (Android/iOS/desktop/web) | Full games with editor-driven scenes, 2D and 3D | Performance-critical 3D, existing C++ engines |
| **Editor / tooling** | Android Studio only | Tiled + libGDX setup tools; no scene editor | KorGE editor tooling (lighter) | Full Godot editor | Android Studio + AGDE (Visual Studio) |
| **Team fit** | Android devs, no engine experience | JVM devs wanting one codebase | Kotlin devs wanting KMP reach | Designers + devs, scene-based workflow | Engine/native programmers |
| **Performance ceiling** | Modest — fine at puzzle/arcade loads; watch recomposition and allocations | High for its class | Moderate–high 2D | High | Highest — you own the frame |
| **App size / startup** | Smallest; normal app startup | Moderate | Moderate | Larger runtime | Varies; native libs must be 16 KB-aligned |
| **Play Games services** | PGS v2 from Kotlin directly | PGS v2 via Android backend glue | PGS v2 via Android target glue | PGS v2 via Android plugin/extension | PGS via Java/Kotlin shell or C API glue |
| **Google Play Games on PC** | Works if input/resizability rules met | Supported route — test keyboard/mouse + window sizes | Test carefully — **Unverified** per-title | Supported via Godot Android export — test per title | Supported — test per title |
| **Biggest risks** | Outgrowing Canvas (particles, many sprites) late | Java-era APIs leaking through KTX gaps; asset pipeline discipline | Smaller community; version churn — pin after Maven check | Kotlin plugin's Godot-version window; export preset upkeep | Build complexity, 16 KB alignment, crash symbolication |
| **Docs in this repo** | [`24`](../docs/24-games-2d-with-compose-canvas-and-custom-rendering.md) | [`25`](../docs/25-games-engines-libgdx-korge-godot.md) | [`25`](../docs/25-games-engines-libgdx-korge-godot.md) | [`25`](../docs/25-games-engines-libgdx-korge-godot.md) | [`26`](../docs/26-games-native-graphics-opengl-vulkan-agdk.md) |

## Choosing in one pass

1. Is the game mostly screens, boards, and UI? → **Compose Canvas**.
2. Is it a sprite-heavy 2D or modest 3D game in Kotlin? → **libGDX + KTX**
   (or **KorGE** if multiplatform reach matters more than community size).
3. Does the project need a visual scene editor / an existing Godot
   team? → **Godot**.
4. Is peak native performance or an existing C++ codebase the point?
   → **Native AGDK**.
5. Hybrid is legitimate: Compose menus/HUD over an engine/native
   surface is a common production shape — budget the interop work.

Cross-cutting game topics for every route:
[`../docs/27-games-services-play-games-and-play-games-on-pc.md`](../docs/27-games-services-play-games-and-play-games-on-pc.md) (PGS v2, Play Games on PC),
[`../docs/28-games-performance-input-audio-and-saves.md`](../docs/28-games-performance-input-audio-and-saves.md),
[`../checklists/game-release-checklist.md`](../checklists/game-release-checklist.md).
